As noted above, we allow here empty alphabets, calling an alphabet

virtual
in this case. Since
given two possibly virtual
alphabets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
,
since a generalized matrix
M
is a function
from
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
to a ring
R
, then in the case that one of these is
empty, we have
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
, and from Remark

2.1
there is only one such function,

\emptyset=M\subseteq(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B})\times R=\emptyset\times R=\emptyset
. We call this a
virtual
matrix.

If the
ring is ordered, we partially order the collection of generalized
matrices, as follows.
Given alphabets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
, let

M
and
\widehat{M}
be generalized nonnegative integer matrices,
respectively of sizes
(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B})
and

(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
. We say that

M\leq\widehat{M}
iff
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
,
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
and for all
(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
we have

M_{ab}\leq\widehat{M}_{ab}
.

For where this partial order gets used below see e.g. Definitions
2.11

and
2.43
.

We note that:

Lemma 2.3
.

For generalized matrices,
addition and multiplication and transpose satisfy the usual
properties.

If the ring
R
is a field then the collection

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
of

(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B})
matrices is a vector space.

Proof.

This is clear when nonvirtual matrices, so we check that for virtual
matrices,
thus when
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
is empty. Then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
has one
element,
\emptyset
, which is the zero element so this is a vector
space, of dimension
0
.
∎

Example 2.4
.

Given
\mathbf{a}
a fixed alphabet
and a matrix
M
all of whose entries are
(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{a})
matrices
with entries in say
\mathbb{R}
,
then

M
has entries in the noncommutative ring
R=\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{a}}
. So this agrees with the above definition of generalized
matrix.

Definition 2.5
.

We extend the definition of generalized matrix
to include rectangular matrices as entries, as follows.
For alphabets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and an
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
matrix
M
, suppose that
each symbol,
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathcal{B}
is itself an alphabet, and that
each matrix entry
M_{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}}
is an
(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b})
matrix with
entries in some ring.

Now write
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\cup_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{a}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}=\cup_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{b}

and
let
\widehat{M}
denote the
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
matrix
with blocks
M_{\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}}
. In this case we say that
M
is a
block
matrix

for
\widehat{M}
.

For a concrete example see Definition
3.3
below.

To show this makes sense, we have:

Lemma 2.6
.

Matrix multiplication agrees with the block structure.
That is, given alphabets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
,
then for
M
an
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
matrix and
N
a

\mathcal{B}\times{\mathcal{C}}
matrix, and supposing that the symbols in
each alphabet are themselves alphabets, then
the
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
matrix
\widehat{M}
defined above is
naturally identified with
M
, and similarly for the

\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
matrix
\widehat{N}
, and we have that

\widehat{M}\widehat{N}=\widehat{MN}.

Proof.

Each
entry
M_{ab}
itself an
a\times b
-matrix, and similarly,
each entry
N_{bc}
is a
b\times c
-matrix, so
we check that the multiplication formula

(MN)_{ac}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}M_{ab}N_{bc}
gives the
ac
- block
submatrix of the matrix
\widehat{MN}
, and equals the
ac
- block of the product
of
\widehat{M}
and
\widehat{N}
.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

We emphasize that for any finite unordered alphabets (indeed this
could be extended to
any
index sets, as long as addition of the
elements is defined)
the usual rules for matrix addition, multiplication and
transpose make perfect sense.

Remark 2.8
.

In what follows, all
alphabets and hence matrices
will be assumed
nonvirtual
(i.e. nonempty) unless expressly
stated otherwise.
Virtual alphabets and matrices will become important
in §
3
when we discuss the upper triangular block
form.

For the special case of nonnegative integer entries, then from a generalized matrix
M
we
define an
edge alphabet

\mathcal{E}(M)
to be a set
\mathcal{E}
together with a
function
e\mapsto(e^{-},e^{+})
from
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
, such that
the number of edges with tail (i.e. initial symbol)
e^{-}=a
and head
(final symbol)
e^{+}=b
is
M_{ab}
.
This illustrates one of the reasons we need to allow for unordered
alphabets:
even in the case where alphabets are ordered, in general there is
no natural order for the edge alphabets.

Definition 2.9
.

(from generalized matrices to Bratteli subdiagrams)

Given a Bratteli diagram, from the pair
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A})
we define an
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
sequence of
nonnegative generalized integer matrices

M=(M_{k})_{k\geq 0}
, with the
ab^{\text{th}}
matrix entry of
M_{k}

equal to the number of edges from
symbol
a
in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
to
b
in
\mathcal{A}_{k+1}
.

Conversely, given an
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
sequence of
nonnegative generalized integer matrices

M=(M_{k})_{k\geq 0}
we know the alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}
; we define
the edge
set sequence

\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{k}
has
\widehat{l}_{k}=\sum_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{k+1}}(M_{k})_{ab}
elements, with
corresponding heads and tails.
We write

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E},M}
, or simply

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{M}
, for the
Bratteli diagram determined by
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E})
and equivalently by the
generalized integer matrix sequence
M
.

We extend this to ring-valued matrices as follows: to an
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
sequence of
ring-valued matrices

N=(N_{k})_{k\geq 0}
, we associate a
0-1
matrix sequence
L
where
the nonzero elements of
N_{i}
and
L_{i}
correspond. The Bratteli
diagram associated to
N
is then that for
L
. We label an edge
e_{k}

with
a=e_{k}^{-}
,
b=e_{k}^{+}
by the
ab
- entry of
N_{i}
, thinking of
this as a “weight” on that edge, as e.g. for (row–stochastic) probability matrices. Matrix
multiplication then has the geometrical interpretation of multiplying
followed by summing
these weights along the edges. In practice, we shall need this notion
for nonnegative real entries.

Setting
l_{k}=\#\mathcal{A}_{k}\geq 1
, given a choice of orders
on
\mathcal{A}_{k}
we may then take

\mathcal{A}_{k}=\{1,\dots,l_{k}\}
, and this choice
determines an
(l_{k}\times l_{k+1})
matrix sequence in the usual
sense.
A different sequence of orders
corresponds to conjugation by a sequence of permutation
matrices.

An
(allowed) edge path
in the diagram
\mathfrak{B}_{M}
,
also known as an edge
string
, is
e=(.e_{0}e_{1}\dots)\in\Pi_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{i}
such that for all
k\geq 0
,
e_{k}^{+}=e_{k+1}^{-}
.
We denote by
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}

the collection of all edge paths.

The combinatorial space

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
with the relative topology inherited from the product topology on

\Pi_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{i}
(where each
\mathcal{E}_{i}
has the discrete topology)
is called a
Markov
compactum

[
Ver81
]
,
[
LV92
]
. It is
understood that this comes
equipped with
its Borel
\sigma
– algebra.

We also use vertex path spaces, defined
as follows.
Given
a sequence
(L_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})

nonnegative generalized matrices with entries in
\{0,1\}
,
by an
(allowed) vertex path
we mean

x=(.x_{0}x_{1}\dots)
with
x_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
such that the
(x_{k}x_{k+1})^{\text{th}}

entry of
L_{k}
equals
1
.
An allowed edge path

e=(.e_{0}e_{1}\dots)
in the Bratteli diagram of
L
determines the allowed vertex path

x=(.x_{0}x_{1}\dots)=(.e_{0}^{-}e_{1}^{-}\dots)
. For such
0-1
matrices there
are no multiple edges, so this correspondence is one-to-one, with
the vertex and edge path
spaces canonically identified (technically speaking this
correspondence is given by a bijective
two-block code
,

x_{k}x_{k+1}=e_{k}^{-}e_{k}^{+}\mapsto e_{k}
) .
In what follows we
use
L
when referring to a vertex representation, with

\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
denoting the collection of vertex paths, and

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
for the edge path space even if the entries of
M

happen to be
0
and
1
. See

[
LM95
]
,

[
Kit98
]
for edge and vertex shift spaces in the stationary
case. (From now on unless mentioned explicitly we shall use edge spaces exclusively).

Recalling Def. 2.6 of
[
AF05
]
, we have the following notion:

Definition 2.10
.

Given
N=(N_{k})_{k\geq 0}
an

(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
matrix
sequence,
then for
k\leq n
, we write
N_{k}^{n}

for the product
N_{k}N_{k+1}\dots N_{n}
, which is
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{n+1})
, so
N_{k}^{k}=N_{k}
. Thus always for
k\leq m\leq n
,
N_{k}^{m}N_{m+1}^{n}=N_{k}^{n}
.
A
gathering
of
a sequence of generalized ring-valued matrices is a new sequence give by taking partial
products along a subsequence. Thus, given

0=n_{0}<n_{1}<\dots
, we call the
(\mathcal{A}_{n_{i}}\times\mathcal{A}_{n_{k+1}})
sequence
\widetilde{N}_{k}=N_{n_{k}}^{n_{k+1}-1}\equiv N_{n_{k}}N_{n_{k}+1}\cdots N_{n_{k+1}-1}
the

gathering along the times

(n_{k})_{k\geq 0}
. If
\widetilde{N}
is a
gathering of
N
then we say that
N
is a
dispersal
of
\widehat{N}
.

For the case of nonnegative integer entries, the edge alphabets for the gathered sequence are naturally labelled
by the finite edge paths of the original diagram, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{0}=\{\widetilde{e}_{0}=(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n_{1}-1})\},\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}=\{\widetilde{e}_{1}=(e_{n_{1}}\dots e_{n_{2}-1})\}
and so
on; we call
(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{k})_{k\geq 0}

the
gathered edge alphabet
sequence.
The gathering of this
matrix sequence
defines what is often called a
telescoped
Bratteli diagram while
a dispersal gives a
microscoping

of the diagram. (One can
think of collapsing a pirate’s spyglass, when telescoping; we like to
use the terms gathering and dispersal to avoid confusion as to which
is which!)

Given a multiple-edged diagram, and hence equivalently a nonnegative
integer matrix sequence
M
, we can produce a single-edged diagram
in the two canonical ways illustrated in Fig.
5
; even
if the alphabets are ordered,
the formalism of
generalized matrices will now be necessary, since as noted above the edge alphabets have no
reason to be ordered.
Assuming the edge alphabet
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is nonempty, so that
\#\mathcal{E}_{k}\neq 0
,
we define

0-1
generalized matrices
A_{k},B_{k}
as follows:

A_{k}
is
(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{E}_{k})
with
the
ae_{k}-
entry of
A_{k}
equal to
1
iff for
e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
we have

e_{k}^{-}=a
, while
B_{k}
is
(\mathcal{E}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
with the
e_{k}b-
entry equal to
1
iff

e_{k}^{+}=b
; we then have
M_{k}=A_{k}B_{k}
. This procedure, called
state-splitting
or
symbol splitting
,
factors
each matrix as a product of

0-1
generalized matrices; defining
L_{k}=B_{k}A_{k+1}
, we have a second sequence of

0-1
generalized matrices, now
(\mathcal{E}_{k}\times\mathcal{E}_{k+1})
, which
give the allowed transitions from edges in
\mathcal{E}_{k}
to those in

\mathcal{E}_{k+1}
, where the edge alphabets are vertex sets of a new graph.

So the sequence
(A_{0}B_{0}A_{1}B_{1}\dots)
is a dispersal of both
M
and of
L
.

Figure 5.
Symbol splitting:
A_{k}B_{k}=M_{k}
and
B_{k}A_{k+1}=L_{k}

To introduce a shift map,
we write
\sigma M=(M_{1},M_{2},\dots)
for the shifted matrix sequence and
define
\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}=\Sigma_{\sigma^{k}M}^{0,+}
for all
k\geq 0
.
This is the
k^{\text{th}}

component
of the disjoint union

\Sigma_{M}^{+}\equiv\coprod_{k=0}^{\infty}\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}
, the
(one-sided)
nonstationary shift of finite type
(
nsft
) defined from the
matrix sequence, see the Introduction.
We give each component the product topology
inherited from
\Pi_{k}^{+\infty}\mathcal{E}_{i}
, so
\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}
is a
compact topological space; each component is
declared to be open in
\Sigma_{M}^{+}
(and so is clopen, since the
union of the remaining components is open).
We define the
word metric

d(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\Sigma_{M}^{+}
as in
[
AF05
]
,
which gives this same topology, as follows.
Beginning with
the
0^{\text{th}}
component,
for
0\leq j\leq k
, we write

w(j,k)
for the number of allowed edge paths from

j
to
k

(so by definition
w(j,j)=l_{j}
). Then,
given
e\neq f
in
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
,
we define
d(x,y)=1
if
e_{0}\neq f_{0}
; otherwise,

d(e,f)=\{(w(0,m))^{-1}\}

(2)

where
m
is the largest nonnegative integer such that

e_{i}=f_{i}
for
0\leq\ i\leq m
. We extend this metric to the

k^{\text{th}}
component by the same formula applied to the shifted
matrix sequence. Lastly, points in
different components are declared to be distance
1
apart.

The left shift map
\sigma
sends the edge path

e=(.e_{k}e_{k+1}\dots)\in\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}
to

\sigma(e)=(.e_{k+1}e_{k+2}\dots)\in\Sigma_{M}^{k+1,+}
(the
“decimal point” is placed to
the left of the zeroth coordinate in each component).
This defines the nonstationary shift dynamics of the
mapping
family

(\Sigma_{M}^{+},d(\cdot,\cdot),\sigma)
,
a sequence of maps along the sequence of components of the
nsft
, as
in
[
AF05
]
; see
the diagram
(
1
)
.

We
need this notation:
given an allowed edge string
e\in\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
and
m\geq 0
, we write

[.e_{0}\dots e_{m}]=\{\tilde{e}\in\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}:\,\widetilde{e}_{0}=e_{0},\dots,\widetilde{e}_{m}=e_{m}\};

(3)

we call a nonempty
subset of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
of this
form a
thin (edge) cylinder set
.
For a vertex path space
\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
, with
(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
an

(\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{k+1})
sequence of
generalized
0-1
matrices,
a
thin (vertex) cylinder set
is

[.x_{0}\dots x_{m}]=\{\tilde{x}\in\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}:\,\widetilde{x}_{0}=x_{0},\dots,\widetilde{x}_{m}=x_{m}\}\subseteq\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
.
Cylinders for other components are defined similarly.

We define general cylinder sets to be nonempty sets with restrictions in other slots, for
example
[.*e_{1}*e_{3}]\subseteq\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
equals
\{(.f_{0}f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}\dots):\;f_{1}=e_{1},f_{3}=e_{3}\}
;
the
*
indicates an arbitrary allowed entry. Note that the cylinder subset
[.*\dots*e_{k}\dots e_{m}]\subseteq\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
can be uniquely
decomposed as a union of thin
cylinders of the form

[.f_{0}\dots f_{k-1}e_{k}\dots e_{m}],
where
these are the allowed left-continuations.
The same holds for vertex
paths. Of course the reason for the name “cylinder set” is that
these are products of subsets of the “axes”
\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots

like an actual cylinder in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Definition 2.11
.

We say
an
(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})
generalized real matrix sequence
(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is
column–reduced

iff these matrices have no all-zero
columns,
row–reduced
if it has no all-zero rows.

We note that the property of a row or column being
all-zero, and hence the notion of a reduced matrix
sequence, makes sense for generalized matrices.

We have immediately:

Lemma 2.12
.

(i) For nonnegative integer matrices, being row–reduced is equivalent to that any admissible finite edge path

e_{k}\dots e_{m}
for
k>0
can be
continued infinitely to the right; being column–reduced is equivalent to that any
allowed string can be
continued to the left.

(ii)
If the matrix sequence is both
row–and-column–reduced then
any allowed string
e_{k}\dots e_{m}
defines a (nonempty) general
cylinder
set

[.*\dots*e_{k}\dots e_{m}]\subseteq\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.

∎

Remark 2.13
.

For an example, consider the stationary vertex shift with

L=\left[\begin{matrix}1&1\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right]
and with alphabet
\{a,b\}
; the word
ab
is
allowed but
[.ab]
as defined by equation (
3
) is empty, hence
not a cylinder.
On the other hand, for the matrix
\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right]
the word
ba
is allowed and
[.ba]
is
nonempty, hence a cylinder, but the shifted set

[.*ba]
is empty as there exists no
c
such that
cba
is an allowed string.
Viewed as edge shifts, let for the first example
e
denote the edge
with
e^{-}=a,e^{+}=b
; then
e_{0}=e
is an allowed finite string but

[.e_{0}]=\emptyset
.
For the second, with
e
denoting the edge
with
e^{-}=b,e^{+}=a
; then
e_{0}=e
is an allowed finite string and

[.e]
is nonempty, but
[.*e]=\emptyset
.

Requiring that cylinder sets be nonempty avoids technical issues,
for instance in the definition of central measures.
Fortunately one can always produce an essentially equivalent matrix
sequence that is reduced. Precisely,
there exists
a canonical
reduced sequence

\breve{N}\leq N
in the sense of
Definitions
2.2
and
2.43
,
with possibly smaller alphabets.
For
M
with nonnegative integer entries,
this canonical reduced sequence
\breve{M}
has two special properties:

(i) it has the same allowed (infinite) edge paths as the original non-reduced sequence,
i.e.
\Sigma_{\breve{M}}^{0,+}=\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, and

(ii) as noted above, every allowed finite symbol string defines a (nonempty) cylinder
set.

See also Proposition 2.2.10 of

[
LM95
]
,
where a reduced graph for a (stationary)
sft
is termed
essential
.

Our proof here follows that of
Lemma 2.2 of
[
Fis09
]
. In that proof we work with
0-1

matrices, and assume the the shift space is
nonempty, whence the matrix sequence and also the reduced sequence
have no virtual matrices.

Here we treat the general case, allowing for virtual (i.e. empty) matrices as well as virtual vertex and edge alphabets.
We
note that if a matrix
M_{k}
is all-zero, then the edge alphabet
\mathcal{E}_{k}

is empty.

As we shall see,
the reduced matrix sequence still can be defined but will have some
virtual alphabets and matrices. For example, if a matrix is all-zero, then in the reduced matrix sequence we will replace
this by a virtual matrix.

Lemma 2.14
.

Given an alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
and an

(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})
generalized nonnegative real matrix
sequence
M=(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, and corresponding edge alphabet
sequence
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{E}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
,
then there exists a unique sequence
\widetilde{M}\leq M
with alphabets
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
such that:

(i)
\widetilde{M}
is reduced;

(ii) if the matrices have nonnegative integer entries, then
\Sigma^{+}_{\widetilde{M}}=\Sigma^{+}_{M}
, i.e. the
corresponding nested Bratteli diagrams
\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
have
exactly the same infinite edge paths.

Proof.

We follow the proof
of Lemma 2.2 in
[
Fis09
]
,
making the needed changes. We define a
0-1
matrix sequence
L=(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
of the same dimensions to have a
0
iff
M
does. We shall produce a
reduced version
\widetilde{L}
of
L
and then define
\widetilde{M}_{k}
to have the
same size as
\widetilde{L}_{k}
except with the entry copied from
M_{k}
. This
matrix sequence
is then also reduced. Moreover, it is clear that (for the integer
entry case) since the edge paths
of
L
and
\widetilde{L}
correspond, so do the edge paths of
M
and
\widetilde{M}
.

To give the proof for
L
then, the only new ingredients added to the
case studied in
[
Fis09
]
are
generalized matrices, including the
possibility of virtual alphabets or matrices.

First
we note that the property of a row or column being
all-zero, and hence the notion of a reduced matrix
sequence, makes sense for generalized matrices. Now for the proof, we consider the
finite sequence
L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
and produce a row-reduced version.
We list all the vertex alphabets and matrix elements of this finite sequence, giving a
finite set
X
, and form the product space
\mathcal{F}=\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{X}
, which is the
collection of all functions from
X
to
\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
. So the matrix
sequences correspond bijectively to this function space
\mathcal{F}
. Changing a symbol from
\mathbf{1}
to

\mathbf{0}
corresponds to removing a given letter or matrix.
The collection
\mathcal{F}
is partially ordered coordinate-wise: one
function is
\leq
a second iff that holds for each coordinate.
We
define a decreasing operator
\mathcal{R}_{n}
on
\mathcal{F}
, which removes all zero rows from

L_{n}
, giving
\widetilde{L}_{n}
, also removing the corresponding letters from
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, giving

\widetilde{A}_{n}
. We also remove the corresponding columns from
L_{n-1}
giving
\widetilde{L}_{n-1}
. This results in
the vertex alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{n-1},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and matrix sequence
L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{n-2},\widetilde{L}_{n-1},\widetilde{L}_{n}
, which are of compatible sizes. If
L_{n}
is all zero, then
\widetilde{L}_{n}
is
virtual as all rows have been removed, and then so is
\widetilde{A}_{n}
.
Continuing to the next step, we define an operator
\mathcal{R}_{n-1}

which removes all zero rows from
\widetilde{L}_{n-1}
and corresponding letters
from
\mathcal{A}_{n-1}
and columns from
L_{n-2}
.
In this way we get operators
\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n-1},\dots\mathcal{R}_{0}.
This
last operator removes all zero rows from
L_{0}
and corresponding
letters from
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Relabeling the matrix sequence as
L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
, we note that
the matrices
L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{n},L_{n+1}
and alphabets
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1}

are still compatible, hence so are

L_{0},L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
with
m>n
with corresponding alphabets; we
apply the above procedure to this sequence and note that this is
consistent with the previous steps since the results are nonincreasing for the order on
\mathcal{F}
.
This yields an infinite row-reduced sequence, relabelled as

(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, with corresponding alphabets.

Next we remove zero columns. We define an operator
\mathcal{C}_{0}
on
\mathcal{F}
which removes the all-zero columns from
L_{0}
, and the
corresponding letters from
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Next,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
removes the all-zero columns from
L_{1}
, and the
corresponding letters from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, and also corresponding rows from

L_{2}
. Applying these in the order
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots
results
in an alphabet sequence
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
for matrix sequence
\widetilde{L}
which is now both row-and column-reduced.

Lastly, if the reduced sequence
\widetilde{L}
has a virtual alphabet

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}

then both
\widetilde{L}_{k-1}
and
\widetilde{L}_{k}
are virtual matrices.
We note that if
\widetilde{L}_{k}
is virtual, that does
not
force
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}

or

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
to be virtual, since the empty function is a subset of
any set (in particular of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k+1}
) (think of
the Bratteli diagram, with no edges in one spot).
If

\widetilde{L}
has no virtual matrices, then the edge shift spaces for

L
and
\widetilde{L}
are identical, and hence also for
M
and
\widetilde{M}
,
and these are nonempty as there exist infinite allowed edge paths. If

\widetilde{M}
has a virtual matrix
\widetilde{M}_{k}
, then the edge space is empty
as is the edge space for
M
. Thus in all cases the edge space for

M
and the reduced sequence are the same.

∎

Remark 2.15
.

We remark that the same proof works for biinfinite matrix sequences,
like those encountered in our forthcoming work.

Now we return to focus on nonnegative integer matrix sequences and their Bratteli diagrams.
The
stable set
of an edge path
e\in\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}
is
the collection of points in the
nsft
which are forward asymptotic to

e
,

W^{s}(e)=\{\widetilde{e}=(.\widetilde{e}_{k}\widetilde{e}_{k+1}\dots):\,\exists m\geq 0\text{ with }\widetilde{e}_{i}=e_{i}\;\forall i\geq m\}
.
These are the equivalence classes for the
stable
or
tail

equivalence relation
on the
nsft

\Sigma_{M}^{+}
.
Transversal to the shift dynamics is the action of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{k}
, the group of finite
coordinate changes on the component

\Sigma_{M}^{k,+}
, generated by the involutions
\gamma
which
interchange two thin cylinder sets:
\gamma([.e_{k}e_{k+1}\dots e_{n}])=[.f_{k}f_{k+1}\dots f_{n}]
where
e_{n}^{+}=f_{n}^{+}
, the tails being
left unchanged by
\gamma
. Note that the
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{k}
– orbit of
e
is its
stable set
W^{s}(e)
.

This brings us to
Vershik’s construction of a single map which has the same
orbits as this group, after the removal of a countable set.
We shall use the following notation:
for
b\in\mathcal{A}_{k+1}
we set
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{+}(b)=\{e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}:\,e_{k}^{+}=b\}
.
Thus the collection of sets
\{\mathcal{E}_{k}^{+}(b):\,b\in\mathcal{A}_{k+1}\}
partitions

\mathcal{E}_{k}
according to the final symbol.

We define
a
stable order

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}^{s}
on
\mathcal{E}
to be a sequence
of partial orders
\mathcal{O}_{k}
on

\mathcal{E}_{k}
, which restrict to a
linear
(i.e. total) order
\mathcal{O}_{k}(b)
on
each
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{+}(b)
. Thus,
edges of
\mathcal{E}_{k}
are comparable for
\mathcal{O}_{k}
if and only if they have the same
final symbol.
By an
ordered Bratteli diagram

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}}

one means the diagram
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}}
together with a stable order
\mathcal{O}
.

This linear order on the sets of edge alphabets for a given time
and final symbol then passes to a
partial order on the collection
of edge paths.
Thus
for
e,\widetilde{e}
in the same stable equivalence class,
supposing

n

is the
least
index such that
\widetilde{e}_{i}=e_{i}\;\forall i\geq n
,
then for
k=n-1
,
e_{k}^{+}=\widetilde{e}_{k}^{+}\equiv b
; we declare that

e<\widetilde{e}
iff

e_{k}<\widetilde{e}_{k}
in the order
\mathcal{O}_{k}
on
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{+}(b)
. This
anti-lexicographic order
defines a partial
order on each component, which is a linear
order when restricted to any stable equivalence class
W^{s}(e)
. One can picture this order
geometrically,
in the
stable tree model
; see Fig.
2
.

The map
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{(k)}
is defined
to send a string

e
in the
k^{\text{th}}
component to its successor in this
order.
This does not quite define a transformation in the ordinary sense, that is,
a map
T:X\to X
where the domain and range spaces agree. One has
however the following suitable notion, see e.g. Def. 2.2 of
[
FM77
]
:

Definition 2.16
.

Given a set
X
, a
partial transformation
of
X
is a
bijection
T:A\to B
for some
A,B\subseteq X
, with
inverse

T^{-1}:B\to A
. The
T
–
orbit
of a point
x
is

\{T^{n}(x):\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ such that this is defined, where
$T^{0}(x)=x$ for all $x$}\}.

Where defined, we call
T(x)
the
successor
of
x
,
T^{-1}(x)
its

predecessor
; the set of points with no
successor is
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}=X\setminus A
, those with no predecessor is
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}=X\setminus B
.
Setting
\mathcal{N}\equiv(\cup_{i\leq 0}T^{i}\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S})\cup(\cup_{i\geq 0}T^{i}\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})
, then
T
is a bijection on
X\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

That is, given a partial transformation
T:A\to B
, we can extract a
bijective transformation
in a canonical way, simply by restricting attention to the full two-sided
orbits; this is

(X\setminus\mathcal{N},T)
.

So given a stable order
\mathcal{O}
, and focusing on the
0^{\text{th}}
component
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, we
define

\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}
as above,
and will see shortly that these sets are at most
countable (and are finite for bounded alphabet size). We call the partial transformation
T_{\mathcal{O}}=T_{\mathcal{O}}^{(0)}:\,\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}\to\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}
the

Vershik map
; this Borel measurable map has the same orbits (the stable equivalence classes) as the group of
homeomorphisms
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{0}
on the compact space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.

For the particular case of the Vershik map, we call the bijective map

T_{\mathcal{O}}
on the noncompact space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}

the
adic transformation

defined by the order
\mathcal{O}
.

Example 2.17
.

Instead of producing a transformation by restriction, one might try to
extend the partial
transformation to the whole space. The easiest example is the most
classical example of adic transformation, the
Kakutani-von Neumann
dyadic odometer
(or
adding machine
).

Recall that given a
0-1
matrix sequence
L
and alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}
, the
vertex shift
space is

\Sigma_{L}^{+,0}=\{\mathbf{x}=(.x_{0}x_{1}\dots)\}:x_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\}
. To define a
vertex
order
on this space we begin with a linear order
\mathcal{O}_{k}
on each alphabet

\mathcal{A}_{k}
. For
vertex paths

\mathbf{x},\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
in the same stable equivalence class,
supposing

n

is the
least
index such that
\widetilde{x}_{i}=x_{i}\;\forall i\geq n+1
,
then we define

\mathbf{x}<\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
iff

x_{n}<\widetilde{x}_{n}
in the order
\mathcal{O}_{n}
on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. This induces an
edge order which only depends on the initial symbols of the edges
entering
b\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
. See

[
Fis09
]
.

We recall the vertex and
edge shift models for the odometer:
taking alphabet
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
and
L=\left[\begin{matrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{matrix}\right]
, then
the vertex
shift space
\Sigma^{+}_{L}
is
\Pi_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
with the vertex
order
0<1
, so the vertex map is

T:(.000\dots)\mapsto(.100\dots)\mapsto(.0100\dots)\mapsto(.1100\dots)\mapsto(.00100\dots)
and so on.
For the edge shift model, the vertex alphabet is a single point and
the edge alphabet is
\mathcal{E}=\{0,1\}
with matrix
M=[2]
.
In both cases,

\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}=\{.111\dots\}
,
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}=\{.000\dots\}
.
Intuitively, thinking of
\Sigma^{+}_{L}
as a car odometer written
backwards, when the odometer gets all the way to
.111\dots
the next
step is to “turn over” to
.000\dots
.
But this is just the unique continuous
extension of the map to all of
\Sigma^{+}_{L}
.

In general however, finding an extension
which is nice, e.g. in the
sense of being bijective or continuous, may not be possible. Thus,
given some function
f:\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}
, we define
T_{\mathcal{O},f}(e)
to be

T_{\mathcal{O}}(e)
for
e\notin\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
, and to be
f(e)
on
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
, calling
this the
f-
extension
of
T_{\mathcal{O}}
. However there
are examples
where
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}\neq\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}
so no bijective extension exists. This fact
depends only on the matrix sequence, and there are other examples where depending on the
choice of order
\mathcal{O}
,
a continuous extension does or does not exist.
See
Examples 3,6
in
[
Fis09
]
, and the example after
Proposition 5 in
[
Med06
]
.

Example 2.18
.

As we showed in Remark 5.1 of

[
FFT09
]
, for the Chacon adic there exists no
continuous extension of the Vershik map on the noncompact space

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}

to
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.
Briefly, in our current notation: as in Figs.
1
,
2
,
we label the edges in the Bratteli diagram by
a,b,c,d,e
. These are ordered

a<b<c<d
;
e
is ordered trivially.
Then
T:(.dd\dots db*)\mapsto(.ee\dots ec*)
so, writing
\underline{e}=(.ee\dots)
and so on, the continuous
extension should be
T(\underline{d})=\underline{e}
; however

T:(.dd\dots da*)\mapsto(.aa\dots ab*)
so we should have
instead
T(\underline{d})=\underline{a}
, and there is no
continuous extension.

We note that
\underline{e}
has no predecessor and no
successor.
Now
\underline{d}
also has no successor and
\underline{a}
no
predecessor.
So
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}=\{\underline{d},\underline{e}\}
and
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}=\{\underline{a},\underline{e}\}
. Thus
\mathcal{N}
is the
countably infinite set
\{\underline{e},T^{n}(\underline{a}),T^{-n}(\underline{d})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

We now show, as promised, that the set
\mathcal{N}
is countable.
The elements of

\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
are the
minimal and maximal elements with respect to the partial order defined by
\mathcal{O}
on this path space.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of
(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})

nonnegative integer matrices, with
l_{i}=\#\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
and let
\mathcal{O}
be an order on the associated Bratteli diagram.

(i) Assume first that
M
is primitive. Then for each
e\in\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
,

W^{s}(e)
is finite iff
\liminf l_{i}=1
, otherwise is countably infinite.

(ii)
The number of minimal and maximal elements,
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}
,
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
, are bounded above by
\liminf l_{i}
.

(iii)
There is always at least one minimal and one maximal element:

\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}\geq 1
,
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}\geq 1
. There exist examples with
l_{i}=N
for all

i
but with
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}=\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}=1
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, given an edge path
e
let us say it
branches (to the
left)
at time
(k+1)
iff there exists an edge path
f
with
f_{k}\neq e_{k}
and

e_{k}^{+}=f_{k}^{+}
. If
\liminf l_{i}=1
then
e

branches to the left only finitely many times so there is
a largest such time, hence
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
is finite (and so
W^{s}(e)
is finite).

Next suppose
l_{i}>1

infinitely often. Given an edge path
e
, we shall show
W^{s}(e)

is countably infinite.
This consists of those paths
f
such that
f

branches to the left from
e
at some maximal time
k
and from then on
agrees with
e
.

There exists
n\geq 0
such that
l_{n}\geq 2
. Since
M
is primitive, there exists such an
n>0
such that also

M_{0}^{n-1}>0
. Hence
a=e_{0}^{-}
is connected to
b=e_{n}^{+}
by the
path
e
, and there is some other path
f
with
a=f_{0}^{-}
and
c=f_{n}^{+}

with
c\neq b
. Since
M
is primitive, there exists
m>n
such that

M_{n}^{m-1}>0
. Hence there is a finite allowed edge path connecting

c\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
to
d=e_{m}^{+}
. Hence we can define
\widetilde{f}
to equal
f
from

0
to
n
and then continue in this way to agree with
e
at time

m
and for all larger times. Thus
f\neq e
and
f
is in
W^{s}(e)
. We repeat the
argument from
m
to a larger time; doing this
k
times we get at
least
2^{k}

paths in
W^{s}(e)
, which is therefore infinite.

For
(ii)
, now considering the order, suppose
e,f
are minimal elements. If for some
n,e_{n}^{+}=f_{n}^{+}
, then
certainly
e_{k}=f_{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq n
. Therefore, if
e\neq f
, there exists some least
m\geq 0
such that
e_{m}\neq f_{m}
. Thus

e_{m}^{-}=f_{m}^{-}
and for each
k\geq m
,
e_{n}^{+}\neq f_{n}^{+}
. That is, if
e\neq f
are both minimal elements, there
exists some least
m\geq 0
such that before that time the paths
are identical and after it, have always different vertex paths. So
given any finite collection
F
of minimal elements, there is a

n\geq 0
such that from that time on, all have different vertex
paths, so
\#F\leq l_{m}
for all
m\geq n
, whence
\#F\leq\liminf l_{k}
and so
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}\leq\liminf l_{k}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
, proving
(ii)
.

(iii)
Let
l_{i}=m=\liminf l_{i}
and let
j<i
with
l_{j}=m
. Starting at vertex
a_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{m}
, there exists a
unique least edge
e_{m}
with
e_{m}^{+}=b_{m}
. Calling
a_{m-1}\equiv e_{m}^{-}
, similarly there exists a
unique least edge
e_{m-1}
with
e_{m-1}^{+}=a_{m-1}
. We do this for
each
b_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{m}
, producing edge paths from some
b_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
to

b_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{m}
.
We note that existence of a unique least edge only holds to the left, not to the
right. For an example, the diagram could begin
with a single vertex with
k
edges, and then continue with
k

constant edge paths, each being both minimal and maximal. One can also
have an edge path
e_{0},\dots,e_{m}
which
is least at each vertex
e_{k}^{+}
but cannot be further extended to the
right while maintaining minimality.

To produce an infinite minimal path, we instead make use of the
compactness of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
. Thus, for
m_{k}

increasing, let

\widehat{e}_{k}
be a finite minimal edge string from time
0
to time

m_{k}
. By compactness here exists a convergent subsequence
\widehat{e}_{k}\to\widehat{e}
.
This is an infnite minimal path, because out to an arbitrarily large
index, it agrees with one of the paths
\widehat{e}_{k}
.
This proves that there is at least one such path.

That we have at best an upper bound in
(ii)
is shown by the example of
the
N
-adic odometer, with
constant alphabet of size
N
and with
\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}=\#\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}=1
.

∎

2.2.
Group actions, partial transformations, orbit equivalence relation

We next examine how the basic ergodic theory for these
are related: the
Vershik map, its associated adic transformation, the
action of the
countable group
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
, and the stable equivalence
relation. See
[
FM77
]
,

[
KM04
]
and
[
Aar97
]
for additional
background.

One can summarize by saying that
the idea of countable equivalence relation subsumes all:
the notions of
invariant set and measure, wandering set, ergodic, and conservative
for a map
or a group action
correspond to that for the orbit equivalence relation.
This fact leads
us to a natural definition of all these notions for a partial transformation such as the
Vershik map.

A subtle point is that this is not quite the case for measurability, which
needs to be defined
separately for maps. A (Borel) measurable map does generate a measurable
equivalence
relation (Corollary
2.22
), however
A. Kechris
[
Kec15
]
has shown us an example of an
invertible map of a Polish space which is non-Borel-measurable yet whose orbit equivalence
relation is Borel, in fact such that there exists a measurable map with the same orbits.

As we note below, conservativity has a natural
definition for countable equivalence relations, and
thereby for group actions;
see Proposition

2.23
below.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
X
be a Polish space (a topological space with an
equivalent metric which makes it a complete separable metric space)
with Borel
\sigma
– algebra
\mathcal{B}
and Borel measure
\nu
; throughout, “measurable” will
mean Borel measurable. We say
T:X\to X
is a
measurable map
or
transformation
iff
T^{-1}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. It is a
measurable
invertible transformation
of
X
iff
T
is invertible and both
T
and its
inverse are measurable. The measure
\nu
is
preserved
by a measurable map
T
on
X

iff for each measurable set
A
,
\nu(T^{-1}(A))=\nu(A)
.
A measurable subset
A\subseteq X
is
invariant

iff
T^{-1}(A)=A
.
It is
wandering
iff
\{T^{-i}(A)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
(here
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
) are
disjoint sets, and is
two-sided wandering
if the map is
invertible and this holds for

\mathbb{Z}
in place of
\mathbb{N}
.

Let
G
be a countably infinite group which acts
measurably on
X
. That is, we are given a map
\Phi:G\times X\to X
such
that, writing
g(x)=\Phi(g,x)
, then
g:X\to X
is an invertible
measurable map on
X
,
satisfying
(g_{1}g_{2})(x)=g_{1}(g_{2}(x))
. The action is

measure-preserving
if that holds for each element of
G
simultaneously, and
a set is

invariant
iff it is invariant for each element.
A set
A\subseteq X
is
wandering
for the action iff
\{\gamma(A)\}_{\gamma\in G}
is an infinite
disjoint family of sets, equivalently iff
\gamma(A)\cap A=\emptyset
for every
\gamma\neq e
.

A
Borel relation

R
is a Borel subset of
X\times X
. Writing
x\sim_{R}y
(
x
is
related to

y
) iff
(x,y)\in R
, this is a
Borel
equivalence relation
iff
x\sim_{R}y
is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive. It is

countable
(
countably infinite
)
iff
that is true for
each equivalence class. The
saturation

R(A)
of a set
A\subseteq X
is
the union of equivalence classes which meet it.
The set
A
is

invariant
for
R
iff
R(A)=A
, and
is
wandering
iff
R
is countably infinite for points in
A
and

A
meets an
equivalence class in at most one
point. A
bijective equivalence
is a bimeasurable partial transformation

f
of
X
such that
x\sim_{R}f(x)
.
R
is
measure-preserving

iff two measurable sets
which are bijectively equivalent have the same measure.

A measurable set
A\subseteq X
is
trivial
iff either

\nu(A)=0
or
\nu(A^{c})=0
.
A measurable transformation, group action or equivalence relation is
ergodic
iff
any invariant set
A
is trivial; it is
conservative

iff any wandering
set has measure zero.
A transformation is
recurrent
iff for any set
A
of positive
measure, for a.e.
x\in A
there exists
n>0
such
that
T^{n}(x)\in A
.

The
orbit equivalence relation
of an invertible transformation,
a partial
transformation (Def.

2.16
), or a group action is the relation whose equivalence classes consist of
orbits.

We say a partial transformation of
X
is measurable iff its domain
and range are Borel subsets and it is a Borel map there.
For a measurable partial transformation, we define the notions of
measure-preserving, ergodic and conservative via
its orbit equivalence relation. This definition is consistent with
that for maps
by part

(i)
of the Proposition to follow.

To understand how these various points of view are related, we note
first that by
Theorem 14.12 of
[
Kec95
]
:

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
X,Y
be Polish spaces, and
f:X\to Y
. Then
f
is
Borel measurable iff the graph of
f
is a Borel subset of
X\times X
.
∎

Corollary 2.22
.

If
f:X\to X
is a Borel measurable function and is invertible, then so is
f^{-1}
. Thus
f

defines a Borel
\mathbb{Z}
– action, and
moreover
its orbit equivalence relation is Borel.

Proof.

The orbit equivalence relation
is a countable union of Borel subsets of
X\times X
, as it is the union of the
graphs of all iterates
f^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Proposition 2.23
.

(i) Let
X
be a Polish space, and
consider either

T
an invertible Borel transformation or
G
a countable group
with Borel
action on
X
, with
R
the corresponding orbit equivalence relation.

The properties of being measure-preserving, ergodic or conservative,
and the notions of a set being invariant or
wandering,
hold for
T
or the
G
– action
iff this holds for the corresponding orbit equivalence relation
R
.

(ii) A (possibly noninvertible) measure-preserving
transformation is
conservative iff it is recurrent.

(iii) When the measure of
X
is finite, a measure-preserving
transformation, group action or equivalence relation is conservative.

Proof.

(i)
Knowing the orbit equivalence relation is
measure-preserving a fortiori implies it
for the corresponding transformation or group action. For the converse, see
(c)\implies(d)
of Proposition 2.1 of

[
KM04
]
.

If
T^{-1}A=A
, then since
T
is
invertible,
A=T(T^{-1}A)=T(A)
, whence

A
is invariant for the
\mathbb{Z}
– action and so for the orbit equivalence relation.
That a set
A
is two-sided wandering for
T
is equivalent to
A
being wandering for
R
. We claim that if
A
is wandering
then it is two-sided wandering. We are to show that for
n<m
, then

T^{n}(A)\cap T^{m}(A)=\emptyset
. But
T^{-n}(T^{n}(A)\cap T^{m}(A))=A\cap T^{m-n}(A)=\emptyset

so that is true. It follows that the transformation is ergodic, or conservative, iff that
holds for its orbit
equivalence relation.

For a
G
– action, it is clear that a set is invariant, or wandering,
iff that holds for the corresponding orbit
equivalence relation.
Hence the notions of ergodicity and conservativity correspond there as well.

(ii)Assuming a map
T
is
conservative, let
A
be a subset of positive measure, and
define
B\subseteq A
to be the set of all

x\in A
which never return, i.e. there does not exist
y\neq x
in

A
and
n>0
such that

T^{n}(x)=y
.
Then in particular this holds for
x
in
B
itself. Hence
B\cap T^{-n}B=\emptyset
and so
T^{-k}B\cap T^{-(n+k)}(B)=\emptyset
whence

B
is wandering, and so
by conservativity
B
must have
measure zero. Thus
T
is recurrent.
Conversely, assuming
T
is recurrent, suppose that
A
is a wandering set;
then
A
must have measure zero, as otherwise
there would be
a contradiction.

(iii) Given an
equivalence relation,
then
if there is a wandering set of positive measure, by
measure preservation the total measure of the space would be
infinite, giving a contradiction. By part
(i)
this
implies the claim for group actions and invertible transformations.
For a noninvertible map, we use part
(ii)
and then apply the same reasoning.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

Combining
(iii)
with
(ii)
gives the Poincaré recurrence
theorem for maps, while

(iii)
can be thought of as the natural version of this for
actions of countably
infinite groups and countably
infinite equivalence relations, and so also for partial
transformations.

2.3.
Central measures, eigenvector sequences and cones

For
M
as in Proposition
2.19
we are interested, first of all, in the probability measures
on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
which are invariant for
the action of the group
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
, and so equivalently, by
(i)
of Proposition
2.23
, invariant for the stable
equivalence relation and the Vershik partial transformation. A
simple equivalent condition (studied by Bowen and Marcus in the
stationary case, see Lemma
2.4 of

[
BM77
]
) is given in
(i)
of the next
proposition.

Definition 2.25
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}
the collection of

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-invariant Borel probability measures on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.
These are
the
central measures
.
We are also interested in two infinite analogues of these: the
\sigma
– finite infinite conservative measures which are
positive finite on some nonempty open subset, and those which are
positive infinite on every nonempty open subset. We call these
respectively the

infinite central measures
and

locally infinite central measures
.

Remark 2.26
.

Vershik in
[
Ver81
]
introduced the term
central
measure
for an invariant Borel probability measure for an
adic transformation on the
(noncompact)
space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.
Given an
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
– invariant Borel measure, to have a central measure we
may need to
remove a finite number of point masses (on fixed points) and normalize to have a
probability measure,
see
(ii)
below.

Remark 2.27
.

Regarding the locally infinite measures, mentioned in the
Introduction, see Lemma
2.62
,
Theorem
2.63
, Remark

2.36
. Illustrative examples
include the Integer Cantor Set in its ternary model, Example
5.16

and certain
nested circle rotations, see Example
5.18
.

Lemma 2.28
.

Given an ordered Bratteli diagram with edge path space

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
,
the orbit equivalence relation
for the partial transformation
T_{\mathcal{O}}
and for
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
are the same: the
equivalence classes are the stable sets
W^{s}(e)
. For the adic
transformation on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}
, this is true
if we restrict the action of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
to that subset.

Proof.

If for
e\in\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
we have
T_{\mathcal{O}}(e)=f
, then there
exists
k>0
such that
e_{k}^{+}=f_{k}^{+}
and
e_{n}=f_{n}
for all
n>k
. There is

\gamma\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
with
\gamma([.e_{k}e_{k+1}\dots e_{n}])=[.f_{k}f_{k+1}\dots f_{n}]
which fixes the tails, whence
\gamma(e)=f
.
Conversely, if
\gamma\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
satisfies
\gamma([.e_{k}e_{k+1}\dots e_{n}])=[.f_{k}f_{k+1}\dots f_{n}]
where
e_{k}^{+}=f_{k}^{+}
while fixing
the tails, then for
f=(.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{k}f_{k+1}\dots)
and
e=(.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k}f_{k+1}\dots)
,
we have
f\in W^{s}(e)
, whence
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{m}(e)=f
for some

m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since any
\eta\in\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
can be written as a finite product of
generators, the same holds for
\eta
. The second statement follows.
∎

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be an alphabet sequence with
l_{i}=\#\mathcal{A}_{i}>0
.

(i)Given
L=(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
an

(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})

0-1
matrix sequence, then a measure

\nu
on the vertex space
\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
is a central measure
iff it is a probability measure such that for each thin cylinder set
we have

\nu([.\widetilde{x}_{0}\dots\widetilde{x}_{n}])=\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n}])\text{ whenever }\widetilde{x}_{n}=x_{n}=s\in\mathcal{A}_{n}.

(4)

For a nonnegative integer matrix sequence
M
,
\nu
on
the edge path space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
is a central measure iff it is a probability
measure such that

\nu([.\widetilde{e}_{0}\dots\widetilde{e}_{n-1}])=\nu([.e_{0}\dots e_{n-1}])\text{ whenever }\widetilde{e}_{n-1}^{+}=e_{n-1}^{+}=s.

(5)

(ii) Given some order
\mathcal{O}
on the Bratteli diagram, a (finite
or infinite) invariant Borel
measure for the adic transformation
T_{\mathcal{O}}
is
invariant for any extension
T_{\mathcal{O},f}
and for the action of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
. A
nonatomic
T_{\mathcal{O},f}
or
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
–  invariant Borel measure is invariant for

T_{\mathcal{O}}
.

(iii)
If a nonatomic invariant Borel measure is ergodic, or conservative, for one of
T_{\mathcal{O}}
,
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
or

T_{\mathcal{O},f}
then that holds for the other two.

Proof.

We give the proofs for an edge shift.
For
(i)
, considering the generators of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
,
\gamma

interchanges the two thin cylinder sets
[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
and
[.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}]
where
e_{n}^{+}=f_{n}^{+}
, and this is clear.

For
(ii)
, if
\nu
on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\setminus\mathcal{N}
is
T_{\mathcal{O}}
–
invariant, then the extension of
\nu
given by assigning
\mathcal{N}

measure zero is invariant for the extended map
T_{\mathcal{O},f}
on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.
Next, consider a generator

\gamma
of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
which interchanges
[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
and
[.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}]
with
e_{n}^{+}=f_{n}^{+}
. In the order
defined by
\mathcal{O}
one of these strings is least, say
(.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n})
, so there exists
m\geq 1
with
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-m}([.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}])=[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
. Moreover, the restriction of
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-m}
to
[.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}]
is
\gamma
. Thus the measure of any Borel subset
E\subseteq[.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}]
is preserved by
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-m}
hence by
\gamma
.
(Note: it is important to allow for subsets here, rather than just
approximating a Borel set by cylinders, as the cylinders
themselves may all have infinite measure. ) Thus
\nu
is
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}-
invariant.

For the converse, let
\nu
on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
be nonatomic. Then
\mathcal{N}
has

\nu-
measure zero since by Prop.
2.19
that set is
countable thus any nonzero measure on it would be atomic. Hence if

\nu
is
T_{\mathcal{O},f}
–  invariant then that holds for

T_{\mathcal{O}}
. Lastly, suppose that
\nu
is nonatomic and

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
– invariant. Then given a cylinder set
[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
,
if
e_{n}
has a successor
f_{n}
for
\mathcal{O}
in the collection of edges
which enter the vertex
e_{n}^{+}
, then, say,
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-1}([.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}])=[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
. But there is some
\gamma
which
interchanges these two cylinder sets,
\gamma([.f_{0}f_{1}\dots f_{n}])=[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]
, and moreover
\gamma
equals the
restriction of
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-1}
to that cylinder. Hence as before, the
measure of a Borel subset is preserved for
T_{\mathcal{O}}^{-1}
.

We claim that a general thin cylinder set is a countable union of
such cylinders, plus points in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
.
To prove this consider the tree of possible extensions of the string
(.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n})
, stopping a branch of this tree at a finite stage if there is a
successor for the last edge added. The branches which continue
infinitely are exactly the points in
[.e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}]\cap\mathcal{N}\mathcal{S}
, proving the claim. Since this has measure zero, we are done.

Part
(iii)
follows from Lemma
2.28
together with
(i)
of
Proposition
2.23
.
∎

We next show that central measures correspond to
sequences of nonnegative eigenvectors of eigenvalue one, which form a
finite-dimensional simplex, for which the ergodic central
measures are the extreme points.
First we need:

Lemma 2.30
.

Given a countable group or semigroup
G
acting continuously
on a compact
metric space
X
, the ergodic invariant Borel probability measures
are exactly the extreme points of the convex compact set of invariant
probability
Borel measures on
X
.

Proof.

Since
X
is a compact metric space, the collection
\mathcal{M}_{G}

of invariant Borel probability measures is
compact convex with the weak* topology, by the Banach-Alaoglu
theorem. Given a continuous transformation
T
on
X
, the
extreme points are identified as being the ergodic measures,
see e.g.
Proposition 3.4 of
[
Fur81
]
. This proof goes through
without change for the action of a countably generated semigroup.
∎

The special
importance of
the ergodic measures comes from Choquet’s
strengthening
[
Phe01
]
of the Krein-Milman Theorem
(pp. 66-70,
[
Rud73
]
): the Krein-Milman theorem tells us that
each invariant probability measure is represented as the barycenter
of a probability measure on the closure of the extreme points, while from
Choquet’s theorem this measure is supported on the extreme points
themselves, and moreover is unique. So in this sense each invariant
measure has an

ergodic decomposition
, as an (integral) convex combination of
the ergodic measures.

That is the general abstract framework. However in the present setting
things are much simpler: we show directly that the
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-invariant
Borel probability
measures on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}

form a finite-dimensional simplex. That implies the
Banach-Alaoglu theorem for this case, and also directly gives the
ergodic decomposition, with
the ergodic probability measures corresponding to the
extreme points. This gives us (see Proposition
2.42
) a first upper
bound,
\lim\inf\#\mathcal{A}_{n}
, for their
number. A second, better bound
follows later from this same basic idea together with the Frobenius
decomposition, see Corollary
5.7
, and this approach
also leads to a bound on the number of ergodic infinite central measures.

The simplex will be described using
intersections of nested cones of column vectors.

We recall that
a
cone

C
in a real vector space
V
is a nonempty subset such that

\alpha C\subseteq C
for each
\alpha\geq 0
; it is a
convex

cone iff
C+C\subseteq C
, iff
C
is a convex set, and is a
positive
cone iff
C\cap-C=\{\mathbf{0}\}
. The collection
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+}
of nonnegative
vectors is a positive convex cone, termed the
standard positive
cone
of
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Note that
\{\mathbf{0}\}
is a cone, the
trivial
cone
. If the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
is
empty, then
d=0
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{A}}=V=\{\mathbf{0}\}
(as for Lemma
2.3
).
For any cone
C
, the point

\mathbf{0}
is its
vertex
and if
C
is convex, is its only extreme point.
Denoting by
C_{i}
the standard positive cones of column
vectors in
V_{i}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
,
a nonnegative real
(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})
matrix sequence

N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
maps each into the next as follows:

\begin{CD}C_{0}@<{N_{0}}<{}<C_{1}@<{N_{1}}<{}<C_{2}@<{N_{2}}<{}<C_{3}\cdots\\
\end{CD}

(6)

A global point of view is useful here:

Definition 2.31
.

Given
alphabets
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and
(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
an

(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})
sequence of
real matrices, we make the following definitions. We write

V_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}V_{i}
, where
V_{i}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
, with the product topology, for this topological vector space.
Its zero element is

\mathbf{0}=(\mathbf{0}_{0},\mathbf{0}_{1},\dots)
, where

\mathbf{0}_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{A}_{n}}
.
We define
N:V_{\mathcal{A}}\to V_{\mathcal{A}}
,
sending

(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots)
to
(N_{0}\mathbf{v}_{1},N_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots)
. This is a continuous linear map. Note that
N=\Pi_{0}^{\infty}N_{i}
.

By an
eigenvector sequence

\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots)
with

eigenvalues

\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
for
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
we mean that

(i)
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in V_{i}\setminus\mathbf{0}_{i}
and

(ii)

\lambda_{i}{\bf w}_{i}=N_{i}{\bf w}_{i+1},\text{ for all $i$.}

We call condition
(i)

never zero
. Thus an eigenvector
sequence is not only an eigenvector for the map
N
of
the vector space

V_{\mathcal{A}}
, and hence nonzero in that space, but in addition is nonzero
for each time projection
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in V_{i}
. In the special case where
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
for
all
i
, then we can normalize, setting
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathbf{w}_{0}
,

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbf{w}_{1}/\lambda_{0}
,

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{2}=\mathbf{w}_{2}/(\lambda_{0}\lambda_{1})
,
\dots,

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k}=\mathbf{w}_{k}/\lambda_{0}^{k-1}
for
\lambda_{0}^{k-1}=\lambda_{0}\cdots\lambda_{k-1}
to get an
eigenvector sequence
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue one.

We define the
positive cone of
V_{\mathcal{A}}
to be
C_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}C_{i}
.

Assume that the
N_{i}
are nonnegative, as in (
6
);
equivalently,

N:C_{\mathcal{A}}\to C_{\mathcal{A}}.

We denote the collection of fixed points for this map by
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}.
That
is,

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\equiv\{\mathbf{w}=({\bf w}_{0}{\bf w}_{1}\dots)\text{ such that }{\bf w}_{i}\in C_{i}\,\text{ and }{\bf w}_{i}=N_{i}{\bf w}_{i+1}\text{ for all }i\}.

\mathcal{V}_{N}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
denotes

\mathbf{w}
such that
{\bf w}_{i}\in C_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{i}\}
for all
i
;
these are the positive
(right) eigenvector
sequences with eigenvalue one.

We write
\widetilde{\mathbf{0}}=\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{N}
.
If
\mathbf{w}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{0}}
is not the
identically zero
sequence

\mathbf{0}
(which is the vertex of the cone
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
), we
call it a

partially zero
sequence.

On the space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we use the
L^{1}
– norm

||{\bf u}||=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}|;

with this choice of norm
the map
{\bf u}\mapsto{{\bf u}}/{||{\bf u}||}

projects the positive cone
C={\mathbb{R}}^{d+}
of column vectors minus its vertex
{\bf 0}
,
onto the
d
– simplex
\Delta
.

We write
\Delta_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
for this norm-
1

simplex,
and
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}\equiv\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}:\,\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}\}
.

Normalizing the sequence

\mathbf{w}
so its first
element is in
\Delta_{0}\subseteq R^{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
, we define the projection

\mathbf{w}\mapsto\mathbf{w}/||\mathbf{w}_{0}||
whenever
\mathbf{w}_{0}\neq\mathbf{0}
from
\mathcal{V}_{N}
, the collection
of all positive right eigenvector sequences with
eigenvalue one,
to
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}\equiv\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}:\,\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}\}
.

Lemma 2.32
.

Given
alphabets

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}

and
(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
a sequence of

(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})

nonnegative
real matrices, then:

(i)

C_{\mathcal{A}}
is a closed convex cone in
V_{\mathcal{A}}
. So is
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}\equiv\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}:\,\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}\}
is a compact convex subset of
V_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(ii) If
N
is column–reduced, then

\widetilde{\mathbf{0}}=\{\mathbf{0}\}
, and so
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}=\mathcal{V}_{N}\cup\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

(iii) If
N
is
primitive and row-reduced, then for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
, if
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
for some
k
,

\mathbf{v}_{i}\neq\mathbf{0}_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

(i)
That these are convex cones is clear, and
C_{\mathcal{A}}

is a closed subset of
V_{\mathcal{A}}
(with respect to the product
topology).

We prove that
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
is closed.
Let
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
,
and suppose that
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\to\mathbf{v}\in C_{\mathcal{A}}
.
We are given that each
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}
is a fixed point for
N
. Thus by continuity of this map
we have

N(\mathbf{v})=N(\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{v}^{(k)})=\lim_{k\to\infty}N(\mathbf{v}^{(k)})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{v}^{(k)}=\mathbf{v}
.
Since
N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
, we have that
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
, as claimed.

To show that
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
is compact, let
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
; and suppose that
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\to\mathbf{v}\in C_{\mathcal{A}}
. Then as
above
N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
. Since
(\mathbf{v}^{(k)})_{0}\in\Delta_{0}
, which is compact,
so is
\mathbf{v}_{0}
;
hence each element of the sequence
\mathbf{v}
is nonzero, and thus
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}

as well.

(ii) Let
\mathbf{v}\geq 0
and
N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
. Suppose
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
; we claim
that then this is true for all
k
. Certainly
\mathbf{v}_{i}=\mathbf{0}_{i}

for all
i<k
. Now suppose

\mathbf{v}_{k+1}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k+1}
; that is, there exists

s\in\mathcal{A}_{k+1}
with
(\mathbf{v}_{k+1})_{s}>0
. Then since
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
, the
s-
column
of
N_{k}
is all zero, contradicting that
N
is column–reduced.

(iii) Let
\mathbf{v}\geq 0
with
N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
; suppose
N
is primitive and

\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. We know there exists
m_{0}>k
such that for any

m\geq m_{0}
, all entries of

N_{k}^{m}
(recalling Def.
2.10
) are greater than zero. Certainly
\mathbf{v}_{m+1}\neq\mathbf{0}_{m+1}
, since
otherwise we would have
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Now using the fact that
N_{k}^{m}
is strictly
positive, we have that in fact
\mathbf{v}_{k}=N_{k}^{m}\mathbf{v}_{m+1}
is strictly positive. Since
N_{j}
is row-reduced for

j=k-1
, the same holds for
\mathbf{v}_{j}
and inductively
for all
j<k
. We have shown that if some
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
then in
fact it is strictly positive and moreover that is true for all lesser
indices as well. But no larger index
\mathbf{v}_{l}
can be
\mathbf{0}_{l}
since that
would imply
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
, a contradiction. This shows
\mathbf{v}_{l}\neq\mathbf{0}_{l}

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
whence all are strictly positive.
∎

It will be useful, here and below in §
4
, to have available two different notations for
iteration.

Definition 2.33
.

As in Def.
2.10
, we denote the product of the matrices from
i
to
n

by
N_{i}^{n}=N_{i}N_{i+1}\cdots N_{n}
. Thus on column vectors as in (
6
),
N_{i}^{n}:V_{n+1}\to V_{i}
.
This is
iteration from time
i
to time
n
along the
sequence of maps.

Secondly,

N^{m}
denotes the
m^{\text{th}}
iteration of the total map, that
is, of the linear operator
N

on
V_{\mathcal{A}}
.

These are related as follows:
defining

(N^{m})_{i}=N^{m+i-1}_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
,
then
(N^{m})_{i}:V_{i+m}\to V_{i}
and

N^{m}
is the product of maps

N^{m}=\Pi_{i=0}^{\infty}(N^{m})_{i}
.

For
0\leq k\leq n
we define:

C_{N}^{(k,n)}=N_{k}^{n}C_{n+1}.

Note that

C_{k}\supseteq N_{k}C_{k+1}\supseteq N_{k}N_{k+2}C_{k+2}\supseteq\cdots

that is,

C_{k}\supseteq C_{N}^{(k,k)}\supseteq C_{N}^{(k,k+1)}\supseteq\cdots

We set
C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}\equiv\cap_{n=0}^{+\infty}C_{N}^{(k,k+n)}
and note that for each
k
,

N_{k}C_{N}^{(k+1,+\infty)}=C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}
.

Each of
C_{N}^{(k,n)}
,
C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}
,
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}

is a closed convex cone. Since
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
is
compact convex,
\mathcal{V}_{N}\setminus\mathbf{0}
is compact modulo
projective equivalence.
We let
\text{Ext\,}C_{N}^{(k,n)}
,
\text{Ext\,}C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}
,
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}
denote
the union of the extreme rays.
By the Krein-Milman Theorem (for finite dimension), see after
Lemma
2.30
, every point in
\mathcal{V}_{N}
is a convex
combination of points in
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}
.

Lemma 2.34
.

Given
alphabets

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}

and
(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
a sequence of

(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})

nonnegative
real matrices, then:

(i)
All sequences
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
can be built inductively as follows:

(1)

Choose
{\bf w}_{0}
in
\Delta_{0}\cap C_{N}^{(0,+\infty)}.

(2)

Choose
{\bf w}_{i+1}\in C_{N}^{(i+1,+\infty)}
to be a preimage of
{\bf w}_{i}
.

(ii)

C_{N}^{(0,+\infty)}\equiv\cap_{n=0}^{+\infty}N_{0}^{n}C_{n+1}\neq\{\mathbf{0}\}
iff

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}\neq\emptyset.

(iii)
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
iff
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
and
for each
k
,
\mathbf{w}_{k}\in\text{Ext\,}C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}
.

(iv)

N
is a primitive sequence
\iff

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}\neq\emptyset
and
\forall\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta},\mathbf{w}_{i}>0
for all

i
.

(v) For
M=N
as above but with integer entries,

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\neq\emptyset
iff

C_{M}^{(0,+\infty)}\neq\{\mathbf{0}\}.

Proof.

(i)
Without loss of generality we can assume
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
is nonempty,
since otherwise this is trivially true. (That is the case if the
alphabets are nonempty).

Now there exist preimages since this is the definition of
C_{N}^{(0,+\infty)}
, so part
(i)
is clear.

(i)
Part
(ii)
follows from this.

(iii):
For
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
then if this is not extreme there exist distinct sequences
\mathbf{w}^{1},\mathbf{w}^{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
and
p,q>0
,

p+q=1
such that
\mathbf{w}=p\mathbf{w}^{1}+q\mathbf{w}^{2}
. But then there exists some time

k
such that they are different:
\mathbf{w}^{1}_{k}\neq\mathbf{w}^{2}_{k}
, and still
\mathbf{w}_{k}=p\mathbf{w}^{1}_{k}+q\mathbf{w}^{2}_{k}
so
\mathbf{w}_{k}^{1},\mathbf{w}_{k}^{2}
are not extreme in
C_{N}^{(k,+\infty)}
. Conversely if
\mathbf{w}
is extreme in
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
then this
cannot happen for any
k
.

(iv):
As above we can assume
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
is nonempty.
If
N
is primitive, then choosing
i\geq 0
, there exists
n>i
such
that
N_{i}^{n}>0
, so

N_{i}^{n}(C_{n+1}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})\subseteq\text{ int}\,C_{i}
,
and hence for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
,

\mathbf{w}_{i}
is nonzero. Therefore
\mathcal{V}_{N}
, and hence
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
, are
nonempty,
and for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\Delta}
, each
\mathbf{w}_{i}>0
.
For the converse, if
N
is not primitive,
then there exists
i\geq 0
such that for each
n>i

some column of
N_{i}^{n}

contains a zero. Let
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}^{n}
be the corresponding
standard basis vector, so
N_{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}^{n}
is that matrix column and
so has a zero entry. Defining
N_{j}\mathbf{w}_{j+1}^{n}=\mathbf{w}_{j}^{n}
, giving a finite nonnegative sequence

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{n},\mathbf{w}_{i+1}^{n},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{n+1}^{n}
such that each

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{n}
has at least one zero entry.
Then by a compactness argument there exists
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
such that
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is not nonzero.

(v):
By
compactness,
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}\neq\emptyset
iff
there exists an infinite sequence
of nested decreasing (nonempty) thin cylinder sets. This holds iff for all
n

there exists an allowed edge path of length
n
.
Now the number of allowed finite edge paths beginning with a
symbol
i\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
and ending with
j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
is equal to

{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{t}M_{0}^{n}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
where
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
is the standard basis vector
and
{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{t}
its transpose, so
the collection of allowed edge paths of length
n
is nonempty iff

M_{0}^{n}

has some non-zero entry, iff
M_{0}^{n}C_{n+1}
contains some
nonzero vector.
And
C_{M}^{(0,+\infty)}\neq\{\mathbf{0}\}
iff for all
n
,
C_{M}^{(0,n)}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\neq\emptyset
; again we use compactness, of
the intersection of the cone
C_{M}^{(0,n)}
with the closed unit
sphere.

∎

In particular, if the matrices are invertible then
the sequence
in part
(i)
of the lemma is
determined by choice of its first element
{\bf w}_{0}
.
Regarding part
(iv)
see Lemma 4.2 of
[
Fis09
]
.

Definition 2.35
.

We recall: a Borel measure is
inner regular
iff the measure of a set is the
sup of the measures of its compact subsets. It is
outer regular
iff
the measure of a set is the
inf of the measures of the open sets containing it, and is
regular
if it is both outer and inner regular.

We shall say a measure is
locally finite
if there exists a neigborhood
of each point with finite measure, and
positive
locally finite
if it is in addition strictly positive on each nonempty open subset.

We recall: a
Radon
measure is a Borel
measure which is both inner regular and locally finite.

Remark 2.36
.

Inner regularity will hold for all the measures considered in this paper, and outer
regularity for the finite measures, as noted in
the proof below.
Indeed, a countable sum of inner regular Borel measures is inner
regular, whence the tower measures constructed later on are inner
regular, though they may be locally infinite and hence not Radon.

The next theorem gives the basic information about finite
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-invariant
Borel measures on an edge space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, that is the elements of

{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}
, for the nonprimitive, nonstationary case. (For simplicity of
the statement and proofs we begin
a vertex space
\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
). Part
(i)
is Theorem 2.9 of

[
BKMS10
]
.

Theorem 2.37
.

Given nonempty alphabets

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
let

L=(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be an
(\mathcal{A}_{i}\times\mathcal{A}_{i+1})

0-1
reduced matrix sequence.

(i) There is an affine
homeomorphism

\Phi:\,{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}\to\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\Delta}
defined
by

\Phi(\nu)=\mathbf{w}
with

({\bf w}_{n})_{s}=\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n}])\text{ where }s=x_{n},\;\;

(7)

where
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
is a thin cylinder set.

(ii)
\Phi
takes the ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}-
invariant probability measures on

\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
, which is

\text{Ext\,}{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}
, bijectively to
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\Delta}
.

(iii)
L
is primitive
\iff
each
\nu\in{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}
is
strictly positive and finite
on each
cylinder set.

All of the above holds for edge spaces defined from a nonnegative integer
matrix sequence
M
, with the map

\Phi
from
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}
to

\mathcal{V}_{M}^{\Delta}
defined
by

\Phi(\nu)=\mathbf{w}
with

({\bf w}_{n})_{s}=\nu([.e_{0}\dots e_{n-1}])\text{ where }s=e_{n-1}^{+}.

(8)

These measures are regular and hence Radon.

Proof.

We address
(i)
.

First we consider the inverse of the map
\Phi
. Thus, assume we are given
an eigenvector sequence
\mathbf{w}
of eigenvalue one, with
\mathbf{w}_{0}
a
probability vector; that is,

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\Delta}
. Considering a thin cylinder set
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
,
we define its measure to be

\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n}=s])=({\bf w}_{n})_{s}.

(9)

We don’t yet know this will give us a measure on the

\sigma
-algebra; at this point
\nu
is a nonnegative real-valued function defined on the collection of
thin cylinders. We shall show this
has a unique extension to a Borel probability measure on

\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
. We write
\mathcal{B}
for the Borel
\sigma
– algebra and

\mathcal{B}_{0}
for the algebra generated by the thin cylinders. One checks
that elements
of

\mathcal{B}_{0}
are exactly the finite unions of
thin cylinders. We extend the definition to this algebra, defining
\nu(A)
for
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
simply to be the sum of the measures of
these sets. However there are many ways to decompose
A
, so to show
this is well-defined we need
to check these all give the same number.

Suppose first that
A
itself is a thin cylinder set
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
.
We then rewrite it as a union of thin cylinders of length
k
for some

k>n
. If
k=n+1
, then we have for any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,

[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}a]=\cup_{b\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}\{[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ab]:\,(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\{b:(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}}\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ab])

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{b:(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}}({\bf w}_{n+1})_{b}

(10)

Now by the definition of matrix multiplication,

\displaystyle{\bf w}_{n}=L_{n}{\bf w}_{n+1}

(11)

iff

\displaystyle({\bf w}_{n})_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}(L_{n})_{ij}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1})(j)

(12)

so since
\mathbf{w}
is an eigenvector sequence of eigenvalue one, which is (
11
),
(
10
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{\{b:(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}}({\bf w}_{n+1})_{b}=(L_{n}{\bf w}_{n+1})_{a}=({\bf w}_{n})_{a}=\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}a])

which shows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\{b:(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}}\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ab])=\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}a]).

That is to say, adding up those thin cylinder measures in the two decompositions of the thin cylinder set

[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
as itself,
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}a]

and as
\cup_{b\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}\{[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ab]:\,(L_{n})_{ab}=1\}
,
gives the same number.
Inductively this is true for any
k>n
,
so we are done in the case where

A
is a thin cylinder and the cylinders in its decomposition have equal length.

Now suppose
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
is written in two different ways as a
union of collections of thin cylinders
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, so

A=(\cup\mathcal{S}_{1})=(\cup\mathcal{S}_{2})
. We claim these give the same
number: that

\sum_{B\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\nu B=\sum_{B\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}\nu B
.
To show this we let
k
be the maximum length of the elements of

\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}
; then we decompose each
B\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
as a
union of thin cylinders of length
k
. By the previous step the sums of their measures agrees
with
\sum_{B\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\nu B
, and as this decomposition is now unique,
the sums for
i=1,2
are equal. Hence
\nu(A)

does not depend on the way in which
A
is decomposed into thin
cylinder sets, i.e.
\nu
is well-defined on
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

It follows that
\nu
is additive on
\mathcal{B}_{0}
: taking
A,B
disjoint in

\mathcal{B}_{0}
, then
A\cup B
is a union of thin cylinders composing
A
and
B
,
so what we have just shown demonstrates that
\nu(A\cup B)=\nu(A)+\nu(B)
.
Note that
\nu
is regular (Def.
2.35
) on the algebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
since, as we
noted above, elements of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
are unions of thin cylinders.

Now since the space
\Sigma_{L}^{0,+}
is compact and
\nu
is finite, then by Alexandroff’s Theorem

[
DS57
]
p. 138, Theorem 13, it has a unique regular extension from the
algebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
to a
\sigma
– additive measure on all of
\mathcal{B}
;
by Theorem 14 there, this extension is regular. Furthermore, since
{\bf w}_{0}
is in the unit simplex, the total mass is

\nu(\Sigma_{L}^{0,+})=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}\nu([.s])=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}(\mathbf{w}_{0})_{s}=1
.

Now we return to the statement of
(i)
, proving the other direction.
We are given
\nu\in{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}
; in particular
\nu

is defined for each thin
cylinder set
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
. Recall that for this to be a thin cylinder
(see above Definition
2.11
)
the string
x_{0}\dots x_{n}
is allowed, and there exists an infinite continuation of this string to the
right (equivalently
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}]
is nonempty). This follows from the hypothesis that
L
is row–reduced.

Turning around (
9
), for each
n\geq 0
and for any for any
s\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, we define a vector
\mathbf{w}_{n}
by

({\bf w}_{n})_{s}=\nu([.x_{0}\dots x_{n}=s])

(14)

This is well-defined:
L
is column– reduced, so by
(i)

of Lemma
2.12
. there exists
x_{0}\dots x_{n}
such that
[.x_{0}\dots x_{n}=s]
is an allowed string.
This is indeed a cylinder set (by definition, with an allowed string and nonempty)
since
the fact that

L
is row–reduced implies it is nonempty.
Furthermore, this number only depends on
n
and
s

as
\nu
is a central measure. Also,
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}
since

\sum_{s\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}(\mathbf{w}_{0})_{s}=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}\nu([.s])=\nu(\Sigma_{L}^{0,+})=1
. Thus we have a vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
; we are to show that

{\bf w}_{n}=L_{n}{\bf w}_{n+1}
.
By (
11
)-
(
12
), it is equivalent to show that

({\bf w}_{n})_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}(L_{n})_{ij}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1})(j)

We have

({\bf w}_{n})_{i}=[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}i]=\cup_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}\{[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ij]\}

Since
\nu
is a measure, by additivity

\nu[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}i]=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}\nu[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ij]

(15)

Now as before, by the definition of matrix multiplication,

\displaystyle{\bf w}_{n}=L_{n}{\bf w}_{n+1}

iff

\displaystyle({\bf w}_{n})_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}(L_{n})_{ij}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1})(j)

(16)

We have from (
14
) and (
15
)

\displaystyle({\bf w}_{n})_{i}=\nu[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}i]=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}\nu[.x_{0}\dots x_{n-1}ij]

(18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{A}_{n+1}}(L_{n})_{ij}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1})(j)

(19)

because the
i^{\text{th}}
row sum of
L
counts how many cylinders
of that type there are.

This proves (
16
).

We have shown that
\Phi
maps
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}
to

\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\Delta}
, and that this map
is invertible.

To finish the proof of
(i)
, from the definitions,
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{L}
,
\mathcal{V}_{L}^{\Delta}
are compact and convex,
and
\Phi
is an affine map. Since weak* convergence of measures
is equivalent to convergence of the measures of each thin cylinder
set, and since this correspondence is clearly bijective, this is a homeomorphism, completing the proof of
(i)
.

Next,
since from
part
(i)

\Phi
is affine, and using Lemma
2.30
, we have
(ii)
.
Part
(iii)
follows from
(iv)
of Lemma
2.34
via part
(i)
.

We next show how to derive the same results for edge spaces. Taking the state-splitting factorization

(A_{i}B_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of

(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, so

AB=(A_{i}B_{i})_{i\geq 0}=(A_{0},B_{0},A_{1},B_{1},\dots)
are
0-1

matrices,
and with
\widetilde{l}_{i}=\#\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
then
denoting by
C_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}
the cone of nonnegative column
vectors in
\widetilde{l}_{i}
– dimensional Euclidean space, the diagram of
(
6
)
extends to

\begin{CD}C_{0}@<{A_{0}}<{}<C_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}@<{B_{0}}<{}<C_{1}@<{A_{1}}<{}<C_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}@<{B_{1}}<{}<C_{2}\cdots\\
\end{CD}

A nonnegative eigenvector sequence
(\mathbf{w}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with eigenvalue one for the original sequence

(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}

extends uniquely to a sequence

({\bf w}_{0},{\bf w}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},{\bf w}_{1},{\bf w}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}\dots)
for the
dispersed matrix sequence
(AB)
, where

{\bf w}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}\equiv B_{i}{\bf w}_{i+1}
and so
{\bf w}_{i}=A_{i}{\bf w}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}
.
For the
0-1
matrices
L_{i}\equiv B_{i}A_{i+1}
, then

(\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}})_{i\geq 0}
is a nonnegative eigenvector sequence with eigenvalue one for

(L_{i})_{i\geq 0}
.

There is a natural bijective correspondence between these finite
allowed strings:
(.e_{0}\dots e_{n-1})

and
(.x_{0}e_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n-1}e_{n-1}x_{n})
for
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}

and
\Sigma_{AB}^{0,+}
, where
x_{0}=e_{0}^{-}
and for
i>0
,
x_{i}=e_{i}^{-}=e_{i-1}^{+}
.
This induces a bijection
from a cylinder set

[.e_{0}\dots e_{n-1}]
of the edge space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
to the cylinder set

[.x_{0}e_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n-1}e_{n-1}x_{n}]
of the vertex space
\Sigma_{AB}^{0,+}
.

Given
\nu_{M}
on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
we define
\nu_{AB}
on

\Sigma_{AB}^{0,+}
via
this correspondence between cylinder sets, setting

\nu_{AB}([.x_{0}e_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n-1}e_{n-1}x_{n}])\equiv\nu_{M}([.e_{0}\dots e_{n-1}]).

This gives a bijection from
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}
to
{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{AB}
.
From part
(i)
we have the bijection
\Phi_{AB}:\,{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{AB}\to\mathcal{V}_{AB}^{\Delta}
.
Thus
\nu_{AB}
determines a nonnegative eigenvector sequence
({\bf w}_{i}{\bf w}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{V}_{AB}^{\Delta}
,
which in turn specifies
(\mathbf{w}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, a nonnegative eigenvector sequence
with eigenvalue one for
(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, this correspondence also
being a bijection.

We claim that

{\bf w}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}\in\Delta_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
. This will show that the composition
of the three correspondences defines
a bijective map
\Phi_{M}:\,{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}\to\mathcal{V}_{M}^{\Delta}
.

We know from
(i)
that
({\bf w}_{i}{\bf w}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{V}_{AB}^{\Delta}
. So

{\bf w}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}
.
Each column of the matrix
A_{0}
has a
single
1
entry with the rest
0
(the edge
e_{0}
begins at a
well-defined symbol
e_{0}^{-}
). Thus
1=||\mathbf{w}_{0}||=||A_{0}\mathbf{w}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}||=||\mathbf{w}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}||

and so

{\bf w^{\mathcal{E}}}_{0}\in\Delta_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
