Example 4.6
.

For an example, consider alphabets
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{0,1\}
for all
i\geq 0
, and matrices

M_{0}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&0\\
\end{matrix}\right],M_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{matrix}\right]
for
i>0
.
Define sequences
\mathbf{v}^{a}
,
\mathbf{v}^{b}
by for
i=0
,

\mathbf{v}^{a}_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\end{matrix}\right]
,

\mathbf{v}^{b}_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
\end{matrix}\right]
,
and for
i\geq 1
,

\mathbf{v}_{i}^{a}=\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\end{matrix}\right]
,

\mathbf{v}_{i}^{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
1\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.
Then
\mathbf{v}^{a}
,
\mathbf{v}^{b}
are nonnegative fixed points for
N
.
The first gives an
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-invariant
probability measure on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
(point
mass on
(.00000\dots)
);
the second, partially zero, gives the zero measure on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
,
but on

\Sigma_{M}^{1,+}
gives point
mass on
(.11111\dots)
.

The word
0
is allowed (the single letters of
\mathcal{A}
are always allowed
in a vertex shift) but
[.0]
is empty hence not a cylinder set. By
adding on one identity matrix before
M_{0}
one has a similar edge
shift example, where for
e
with
e^{-}=1,e^{+}=1
, then
e_{0}=e
is
allowed, but
[.e_{0}]
is empty.

The cone
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
has only one nonzero extreme element up to multiplication by a
constant:
\mathbf{v}^{a}_{0}=\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\\
\end{matrix}\right]
. For times
\geq 1
, there are two elements.
Note that
\mathbf{v}_{b}
is a partially zero fixed point so

\widetilde{\mathbf{0}}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{N}=\{\mathbf{0},c\mathbf{v}_{b}:\,c>0\}.

For an example with no point masses, replace the matrices for
i\geq 1
by

\left[\begin{matrix}2&0\\
0&2\\
\end{matrix}\right]
; this gives a pair of dyadic odometers
and hence two nonatomic invariant ergodic probability measures on
\Sigma_{M}^{1,+}
.

Here is our general abstract definition for subdiagrams:

Definition 4.7
.

(Distinguished eigenvector sequence)
Given nonnegative real
generalized matrix sequences

N\leq\widehat{N}
, with
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(see Definition
2.2
), for each
i
we denote by
\mathtt{e}_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{i}}
the natural
embedding, with
\mathtt{e}:V_{\mathcal{A}}\to V_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
the product map
\mathtt{e}=(\mathtt{e}_{0},\mathtt{e}_{1},\dots)
.
We define
\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}/Ndist}^{\mathbf{0}}
to be the following collection of vector sequences:

(i)
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
and

(ii)
\iota(\mathbf{w})\equiv\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{N}^{m}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}))
exists.

We write

\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}/Ndist}
for those elements
\mathbf{w}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}/Ndist}^{\mathbf{0}}
which are never zero:
\mathbf{w}_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
for all

k\geq 0
. We say
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}/Ndist}
is
\widehat{N}/N
–
distinguished
.

Remark 4.8
.

Note that
(ii)
is equivalent to:

(ii^{\prime})

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\widehat{N}_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1}))

exists for
i
arbitrarily large.
These are equivalent since (by Lemma
4.4
) it is then true for all
i\geq 0
.

Lemma 4.9
.

If
\mathbf{w}
is a distinguished eigenvector sequence then
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\equiv\iota(\mathbf{w})
is
nonnegative and never zero.

Proof.

Given
N\leq\widehat{N}
as above, let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
be

\widehat{N}/N
–
distinguished. Thus

\iota(\mathbf{w})\equiv\lim_{m\to\infty}\widehat{N}^{m}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}))
exists.
Now
N\leq\widehat{N}
so

\mathbf{w}=N\mathbf{w}\leq\widehat{N}\mathbf{w}
. Thus
since
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\widehat{N}_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1}))

exists for all
i\geq 0
,
\mathbf{0}_{i}\neq\mathbf{w}_{i}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}N_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1}))\leq\lim_{n\to+\infty}\widehat{N}_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}_{n+1}))=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}
for all
i
. That is, while the vector

\mathbf{w}_{i}\in V_{i}
may have some zero entries, it also has some strictly
positive entries, and these same entries are also strictly
positive for
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\in\widehat{V}_{i}
.
∎

Definition 4.10
.

(General definition, short version)
In summary, a nonnegative vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{N}/N
- distinguished iff it
is a nonnegative, never zero
fixed point
for
N
, which under iteration by
\widehat{N}
converges, to
a sequence
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
, which is necessarily a nonnegative and never
zero fixed point for
\widehat{N}
.

The content of the nonstationary Frobenius–Victory
theorem (matrix form, Theorem
4.19
; diagram form,
4.27
) will be that each nonnegative, never
zero fixed point
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}

for
\widehat{N}

determines, and
is determined by, a
\widehat{N}/N
- distinguished
sequence
\mathbf{w}
for some primitive
submatrix sequence
N
.

Remark 4.11
.

What is not so clear is whether or not this cone is
closed
.
That would be important as then it is generated by its extreme
points. However, as we see below, there are counterexamples.

Let us consider what could go wrong.

Let
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}/Ndist}\subseteq V_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and suppose that
\mathbf{v}^{(k)}\to\mathbf{v}\in V_{\mathcal{A}}
.
By Lemma
2.32
,

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
is a closed cone, so
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
. Suppose that
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
.

For each
k
, we know that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{N}^{n}(\mathbf{v}^{(k)})
exists; call it
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}^{(k)}
.
By Lemma
4.4
each
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}^{(k)}
is a fixed point for

\widehat{N}
. By Lemma

4.9
, each
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}^{(k)}

is never zero.

Now if
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}^{(k)}
converges, say to
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
, then
from continuity of the linear transformation
\widehat{N}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
is also a fixed point,
and again by Lemma

4.9
is never zero.

The problem is that perhaps it blows up in the limit, and indeed this
can in fact happen. In that case,
\mathbf{v}
is not distinguished.

4.5.
Distinguished eigenvector sequences in the
(2\times 2)
block case

This case will serve to both
illustrate the main ideas and to provide the
inductive step for the proof of the general upper triangular block case of Theorem
4.19
. We are
given a
sequence
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{1,\dots,l_{i}\}
of nonempty alphabets
and an
(l_{i}\times l_{i+1})
sequence

(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of real matrices.
The alphabet is partitioned
into nonempty streams
\alpha,\beta
with

\alpha_{i}=\{1,\dots,l_{i}^{\alpha}\}
and

\beta_{i}=\{l_{i}^{\alpha}+1,\dots,l_{i}\}
, where
l_{i}=l_{i}^{\alpha}+l_{i}^{\beta}
.

Let us assume that
we have the
upper triangular block decomposition of (
24
):

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right];{\text{\, that is, for all $i$}},N_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{i}&C_{i}\\
0_{i}&B_{i}\end{matrix}\right]

(27)

(so

A_{i}
is
(\alpha_{i}\times\alpha_{i+1}),

B_{i}
is
(\beta_{i}\times\beta_{i+1}),

C_{i}
is
(\alpha_{i}\times\beta_{i+1}),
and

0_{i}
is the
(\beta_{i}\times\alpha_{i+1})

all-zero matrix.)

We have
V_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}V_{i}

where
V_{i}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{A}_{i}}={\mathbb{R}}^{l_{i}}
. We write

(V_{\alpha})_{i}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha_{i}}
and

(V_{\beta})_{i}={\mathbb{R}}^{\beta_{i}}
, and define

V_{\alpha}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}(V_{\alpha})_{i}
,
V_{\beta}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}(V_{\beta})_{i}
, so

V_{\mathcal{A}}=V_{\alpha}\times V_{\beta}
; we
decompose a vector

\mathbf{v}_{i}\in V_{i}
as

\mathbf{v}_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\\
\mathbf{w}_{i}\end{matrix}\right]

with

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\in(V_{\alpha})_{i}

and
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in(V_{\beta})_{i}.

The canonical
embeddings are both indicated now by the single map
\mathtt{e}
, which
sends

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
to
\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]
and

\mathbf{w}
to
\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
; thus
\mathtt{e}:V_{\alpha}\cup V_{\beta}\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{A}}
. Setting

\mathring{N}=\left[\begin{matrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right],

we define
\pi\mathbf{v},
for
\mathbf{v}\in V_{\mathcal{A}},
whenever the following
limit exists:

\pi\mathbf{v}=\pi(\mathbf{v})=\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathring{N}^{m}\mathbf{v}.

For

\mathbf{u}\in V_{\alpha}\cup V_{\beta}
,
we define, whenever the following
limit exists:

\iota\mathbf{u}=\iota(\mathbf{u})=\lim_{m\to+\infty}N^{m}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})).

For example, with
N
nonnegative, if
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}\subseteq V_{\alpha}
then
the above limit exists, since

\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
whence

\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})=\iota\mathbf{u}
is just the embedding.

Note that when
N
is nonnegative, then by Definition
2.43
,

A\leq N
and also
B\leq N
.

Our main goal in this section is to show that nonnegative eigenvector
sequences of eigenvalue one for nonnegative
N
come from those from either
A
or

B
, in a bidirectional algorithmic way. These latter are
the eigenvector
sequences which are

N/B
– distinguished.
This will be a version of the Frobenius–Victory theorem (Theorem

4.18
); see the summary at the end
of this subsection.

We have
V_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}V_{i}

where
V_{i}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{A}_{i}}={\mathbb{R}}^{l_{i}}
. We write

(V_{\alpha})_{i}\equiv{\mathbb{R}}^{\alpha_{i}}
and

(V_{\beta})_{i}={\mathbb{R}}^{\beta_{i}}
, and define

V_{\alpha}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}(V_{\alpha})_{i}
,
V_{\beta}\equiv\Pi_{i\geq 0}(V_{\beta})_{i}
, so

V_{\mathcal{A}}=V_{\alpha}\times V_{\beta}
; we
decompose a vector

\mathbf{v}_{i}\in V_{i}
as

\mathbf{v}_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\\
\mathbf{w}_{i}\end{matrix}\right]

with

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\in(V_{\alpha})_{i}

and
\mathbf{w}_{i}\in(V_{\beta})_{i}.

The canonical
embeddings are both indicated now by the single map
\mathtt{e}
, which
sends

\widehat{\mathbf{v}}
to
\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]
and

\mathbf{w}
to
\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
; thus
\mathtt{e}:V_{\alpha}\cup V_{\beta}\hookrightarrow V_{\mathcal{A}}
. Setting

\mathring{N}=\left[\begin{matrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right],

we define
\pi\mathbf{v},
for
\mathbf{v}\in V_{\mathcal{A}},
whenever the following
limit exists:

\pi\mathbf{v}=\pi(\mathbf{v})=\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathring{N}^{m}\mathbf{v}.

For

\mathbf{u}\in V_{\alpha}\cup V_{\beta}
,
we define, whenever the following
limit exists:

\iota\mathbf{u}=\iota(\mathbf{u})=\lim_{m\to+\infty}N^{m}(\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})).

For example, with
N
nonnegative, if
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}\subseteq V_{\alpha}
then
the above limit exists, since

\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
whence

\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{u})=\iota\mathbf{u}
is just the embedding.

Note that when
N
is nonnegative, then by Definition
2.43
,

A\leq N
and also
B\leq N
.

Remark 4.12
.

Although for the theory we are developing
N
will always be
nonnegative, we state propositions here in more
generality, so as to highlight just where that assumption is really
needed.

Lemma 4.13
.

For
N
a real matrix sequence in the block form of (
24
),
then given

\mathbf{v}\in V_{\mathcal{A}}
, let us write as above

\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
.
Then if
N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
(whence
B\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
),

(i)

\pi\mathbf{v}
exists iff
\iota\mathbf{w}
exists.

(ii) In this case,

\mathbf{v}=\pi\mathbf{v}+\iota\mathbf{w}
.

Proof.

(i)
Fixing
i\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
0\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}+\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}+\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}.

Thus

N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}=\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}+N_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w})_{n+1}

(28)

Since

N\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}
,
this is

\mathbf{v}_{i}=\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}+N_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w})_{n+1}

(29)

so one converges iff the other does.

(ii) This follows by taking the limit as
n\to\infty
of equation
(
29
).
∎

See Lemma
5.8
regarding a
condition for
convergence, in the nonnegative
(2\times 2)
case.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
N
be in the block
form of
(
24
).
For
\mathbf{z}\in V_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathbf{w}\in V_{\beta}
,
if
\mathring{N}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}
,
B\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
and
\iota\mathbf{w}

exists, then

N(\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w}
and
\pi(\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{z}.

Proof.

Since
\iota\mathbf{w}

exists, from
(ii)
of Lemma
4.4
,

\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]\equiv\iota\mathbf{w}
is a fixed point for
N
.
From
(i),(ii)
of Lemma
4.13
,

\pi\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
exists and

\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]=\pi\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]+\iota\mathbf{w}=\pi\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right].

Hence
\pi(\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{0}.

Since
\mathring{N}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}
, then
N(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}
so

N(\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w}
, and
from the definition of the map
\pi
,
\pi\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{z}
, so
\pi(\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{z}.

∎

It follows:

Corollary 4.15
.

For
N
as in (
24
) and nonnegative,
if
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}^{\mathbf{0}}
,
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}^{\mathbf{0}}
and
\iota\mathbf{w}

exists, then

\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
and
\pi(\mathbf{z}+\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{z}.

∎

Lemma 4.16
.

For
N
as in (
24
) and nonnegative, if
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
, then
\pi\mathbf{v}
exists.

Proof.

We first claim that for
i
fixed and
n\geq i
,

\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{i},

in the partial order (i.e. coordinate–by–coordinate) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{l_{i}}
:
writing
\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
, since
N
and
\mathbf{v}
are nonnegative,

\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}=\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}=\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}\leq\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}+N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}=N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}=\mathbf{v}_{i},

proving the claim.

Hence the
sequence of vectors
\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}

lies in a compact region of the positive cone
{\mathbb{R}}^{l_{i}+}
, so
there exists an increasing subsequence
n_{j}
such that

\mathring{N}_{i}^{n_{j}}\mathbf{v}_{n_{j}+1}
converges.
Using equation (
28
),
we see that therefore

N_{i}^{n_{j}}(\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w})_{n_{j}+1}
converges.
We claim that
we can deduce from this the convergence of

N_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w})_{n+1}
.

Proof:

\displaystyle N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}

\displaystyle=N_{i}^{n-1}N_{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}=N_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}0&C_{n}\\
0&B_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=N_{i}^{n-1}\biggl(\left[\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&B_{n}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}0&C_{n}\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right]\biggr)\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=N_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]+N_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}C_{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]

so

N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}

is nondecreasing in
n
, and
therefore convergence along
the subsequence implies convergence.
Finally since this holds for all
i
,
then again by equation (
28
),
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathring{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{v}_{n+1}

exists for all
i
, converging by definition to
\pi\mathbf{v}
.
∎

Definition 4.17
.

Given a convex cone
C
in a vector space
V
, we call
\mathbf{v}

an
extreme vector
iff it is a nonzero element of an extreme ray
of
C
. Equivalently,
\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}
and if
\mathbf{v}=a\mathbf{u}+b\mathbf{w}
for

\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}\in C
with
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}
linearly independent then either
a
or

b
is zero.
We say
two extreme vectors are
distinct
iff they are linearly independent.

Given nonvirtual nonnegative real matrix
sequences

B\leq N
, then

\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{B}
denotes the
collection of extreme vectors
of
the closed
convex cone
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
denotes the points in the convex cone
\mathcal{V}_{B}^{\mathbf{0}}
that are

N/B
– distinguished, and
\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{B}
the collection of extreme vectors of
the convex cone
\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

Recall that a matrix sequence
B
is
identically zero
if it is all–zero for all times.

We note that

\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
is indeed a convex cone; if there are no
distinguished points it is
\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
Recall from Remark
4.5
that if
B

is column–reduced then
\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}=\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}\cup\{\mathbf{0}\}
,
as it contains no partially zero
sequences.

Theorem 4.18
.

(Frobenius–Victory
Theorem,
(2\times 2)
block case)

Assume that we are given an
(l_{i}\times l_{i+1})
nonnegative real matrix
sequence
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
(2\times 2)
upper triangular
block form as in (
24
), so

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]
, with

A
column–reduced while
B
is either column–reduced
or identically zero. Then:

(i) Consider the map

\pi:\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}^{\mathbf{0}}\cong\mathcal{V}_{A}^{\mathbf{0}}
. The inverse image of
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}^{\mathbf{0}}
is
the collection of
points of the form
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{z}+\iota(\mathbf{w})
for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}=\iota(\mathcal{V}_{A}^{\mathbf{0}})+\iota(\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}})
, and in
fact,
defining
\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\iota\mathbf{v}+\iota\mathbf{w}
,

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{V}_{A}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\;\;\;\;\text{is\,a\,bijection.}

(iii)

\iota:\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A}\cup\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{B}\to\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}\;\;\;\;\text{is\,a\,bijection.}

Proof.

(i):
Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}
; thus
\mathbf{z}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]
, with
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
. This is the bijection
\mathtt{e}:\,\mathcal{V}_{A}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}
.
Now given
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
, by Lemma
4.16
,
\pi\mathbf{v}
exists,
thus by
(ii)
of Lemma
4.4
,
\mathring{N}(\pi\mathbf{v})=\pi\mathbf{v}
,
whence (since
A
is column–reduced)
\pi\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

Given
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}
, then
as above,
\mathbf{z}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]
is in
\mathcal{V}_{N}
and
\pi\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{z}
. From Corollary
4.15

we know that
for any
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
,
\pi(\mathbf{z}+\iota(\mathbf{w}))=\mathbf{z}
as
well. This is the general solution: if
\pi(\widetilde{\mathbf{z}})=\mathbf{z}
, then
defining
\mathbf{x}=\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}
, we have
\pi\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
; writing
\mathbf{x}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
, then by Lemma

4.13
,
\mathbf{x}=\iota(\mathbf{w})
, whence

\mathbf{w}
is either
\mathbf{0}
or distinguished, assuming that

B
is column–reduced. If
B=\mathbf{0}
, then
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0}
as is
\iota(\mathbf{w})
.

(ii):
If
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
, then
B
must be column–reduced (since
it cannot be zero).
By part
(ii)
of Lemma
4.4

\iota\mathbf{w}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
is a fixed point for
N
; it is nonzero since

\mathbf{w}
is. Thus
\iota\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
. If
B_{n}
is zero for all
n
, then

\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}=\emptyset
and for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
,

\iota\mathbf{w}\in\emptyset\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{N}
, trivially.

For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
,

\iota\mathbf{x}=\mathtt{e}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{N}
.
This shows that for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
,

\iota\mathbf{x}+\iota\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
.

We claim the map
\mathcal{I}
is onto.
Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
.
By Lemma
4.16
,
\pi\mathbf{v}
exists,
whence by
(ii)
of Lemma
4.4
,
\mathring{N}(\pi\mathbf{v})=\pi\mathbf{v}
,
and writing

\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]

and
\pi\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]

,
by
(i)
of Lemma
4.13

\iota\mathbf{w}
exists,
while by part
(ii)
of Lemma
4.13
,

\mathbf{v}=\pi\mathbf{v}+\iota\mathbf{w}=\iota\mathbf{x}+\iota\mathbf{w}
. Note that

A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}
and
B\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}
, whence, by the assumptions on
A
and
B
,

\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}^{\mathbf{0}}
and

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
.

Next we check that this map is injective.
If

\iota\mathbf{x}+\iota\mathbf{w}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]=\iota\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\iota\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\\
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right],

then

\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
, so
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
, hence
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
.

(iii)
Let
\mathbf{x}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A}
, so in particular
\iota\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{N};

we claim that
\iota\mathbf{x}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}.

Now

\iota\mathbf{x}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right];

if it is not an extreme vector, there exist non-
\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{v}_{b}
in distinct rays of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
, and

p,q>0
with
p+q=1
such that

\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]=p\mathbf{v}_{a}+q\mathbf{v}_{b}.

Then

\mathbf{v}_{a}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}_{a}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right],\mathbf{v}_{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}_{b}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]
; note that
\mathbf{x}_{a},\mathbf{x}_{b}
are distinct extreme vectors of

\mathcal{V}_{A}
,
with

\mathbf{x}=p\mathbf{x}_{a}+q\mathbf{x}_{b}
, contradicting that
\mathbf{x}
is extreme.

Let
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{B}
, so from
(ii)
,
\iota\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}.

If
\iota\mathbf{w}
is not extreme for

\mathcal{V}_{N}
,
there exist non-
\mathbf{0}

\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{v}_{b}
in distinct rays of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
, and

p,q>0
with
p+q=1
such that

\iota\mathbf{w}=p\mathbf{v}_{a}+q\mathbf{v}_{b},
with

\iota\mathbf{w}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right],

\mathbf{v}_{a}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{s}_{a}\\
\mathbf{w}_{a}\end{matrix}\right],

\mathbf{v}_{b}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{s}_{b}\\
\mathbf{w}_{b}\end{matrix}\right].
Then
\mathbf{w}=p\mathbf{w}_{a}+q\mathbf{w}_{b}
.
Since

\mathbf{v}_{a},\mathbf{v}_{b}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
, we know
\mathbf{w}_{a},\mathbf{w}_{b}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}
.
Furthermore from Corollary
4.15
,
\pi(\iota\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{0}
whence

\pi(\mathbf{v}_{a}),\pi(\mathbf{v}_{b})=\mathbf{0}
. Thus, as in the proof of
(ii)
above,
\mathbf{v}_{a}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{s}_{a}\\
\mathbf{w}_{a}\end{matrix}\right]=\iota(\mathbf{w}_{a})
and similarly for
\mathbf{w}_{b}
, and so

\mathbf{w}_{a},\mathbf{w}_{b}
are non-
\mathbf{0}
, distinguished points in distinct rays.
Since

\mathbf{w}
is extreme in
\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
,
this gives a contradiction.
Thus
\iota\mathbf{w}
is extreme.

Next we show this map is onto
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{v}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}
.
Writing
\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
, by Lemma
4.16
and Lemma
4.13
,

\mathbf{v}=\pi\mathbf{v}+\iota\mathbf{w}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]=\iota\mathbf{x}+\iota\mathbf{w}

with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}^{\mathbf{0}}
,

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}^{\mathbf{0}}
. Since
\mathbf{v}
is extreme, either
\iota\mathbf{x}
or
\iota\mathbf{w}
is
\mathbf{0}
.
Suppose
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0}
. If
\mathbf{x}
is not extreme for
\mathcal{V}_{A}
, then there exist
points

\mathbf{x}_{a},\mathbf{x}_{b}
in distinct rays of
\mathcal{V}_{A}
and
p,q>0
,

p+q=1
such that

\mathbf{x}=p\mathbf{x}_{a}+q\mathbf{x}_{b}
, but then

\mathbf{v}=p\cdot\iota\mathbf{x}_{a}+q\cdot\iota\mathbf{x}_{b}
, contradicting that
\mathbf{v}
is extreme.
If on the other hand
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{w}
is not
extreme in the convex
cone
\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
, then there exist
points
\mathbf{w}_{a},\mathbf{w}_{b}
in distinct rays of
\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}
, and
p,q>0
,

p+q=1
such that

\mathbf{w}=p\mathbf{w}_{a}+q\mathbf{w}_{b}
. But then

\mathbf{v}=p\iota\mathbf{w}_{a}+q\iota\mathbf{w}_{b}
so is not extreme, a contradiction.
Thus
\mathbf{v}

is indeed the image of a point in
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A}\cup\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{B}
.

Injectivity of
\mathcal{I}
was proved in part
(ii)
, and this proves
injectivity here, as points
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})
and
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{w})
in
V_{A}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\mathcal{V}_{B\text{dist}}^{\mathbf{0}}
correspond to

\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}
in
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A}\cup\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{B}
respectively.
∎

To summarize, the convex cone
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}
exhibits a product structure: any

\mathbf{v}
in
\mathcal{V}_{N}
is decomposed uniquely as follows:

\mathbf{v}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{z}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]
where
\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]=\iota(\mathbf{w})
and
\mathbf{z}=\pi(\mathbf{v})
, giving the
bidirectional correspondence detailed in the theorem; the extremity of rays
is preserved, and the
correspondence is algorithmic, in that the maps
\pi,\iota
give
fixed points which are limits of iterations.

4.6.
Distinguished eigenvector sequences for the upper triangular
case

Next we extend form the
(2\times 2)
to the general upper triangular
block case.
Let
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a
column–
reduced sequence of
(l_{i}\times l_{i+1})
nonnegative real
matrices with bounded alphabet size.
We write
V
for the collection of vector sequences
\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{v}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in V_{i}={\mathbb{R}}^{l_{i}}
; thus
V=\Pi_{i\geq 0}V_{i}
. Without loss of generality, by
taking a gathering, we
can achieve that
N
is
in fixed–size Frobenius
normal form for times
\geq 1
, as in the conclusion of Theorem

3.11
.
Furthermore, by Remarks
3.19
, and

4.26
, without loss of generality
we can assume this holds for times
\geq 0
.
Thus from now on,
we assume that, for all times

i\geq 0
,
the matrices
N_{i}
are

(l\times l)
with
(\widehat{l}\times\widehat{l})
block
form of (
23
),
with block sizes given by
l=\widetilde{l}_{1}+\dots+\widetilde{l}_{\widehat{l}}
:

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{1\widehat{l}}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0_{\widehat{l}1}&\dots&&A_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]

Furthermore, for each block index
1\leq j\leq\widehat{l}
, for each time
i\geq 0
, the matrix sequence
A_{j}=(A_{j})_{i}
is either
column–
reduced and primitive or identically zero. Moreover after a further
gathering we can assume, for each time
i\geq 0
, that
N_{i}
has zero
blocks
C_{*}=\mathbf{0}_{*}
in exactly the same above-diagonal locations.
Then since
A_{j}\leq N
for each
j
, it makes sense to examine the
N/A_{j}
– distinguished
eigenvector sequences. We do this with the help of the
(2\times 2)
–
block inductive step just treated.

Defining for each time

i\geq 0

and for
1<j\leq\widehat{l}
,

(V_{j})_{i}=\Pi_{\widetilde{l}_{1}+\dots+\widetilde{l}_{j-1}+1}^{\widetilde{l}_{1}+\dots+\widetilde{l}_{j}}{\mathbb{R}}
, we then suppress this time index and use the same notation
for vector sequences,
so
V=V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{\widehat{l}}
. We write the decomposition
of

\mathbf{v}\in V
as
\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{\widehat{l}})
where
\mathbf{v}_{j}\in V_{j}
.
We have the natural embeddings of
V_{j}
into
V
; we unite these
into a single map,

\mathtt{e}:V_{1}\cup\dots\cup V_{\widehat{l}}\to V
; thus

\mathtt{e}(\mathbf{w}_{j})=(\mathbf{0}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{0}_{j-1},\mathbf{w}_{j},\mathbf{0}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{0}_{\widehat{l}})
, with
\mathbf{0}_{j}
the zero
vector (sequence) in
V_{j}
.

It will be useful to factor this map: we write for the natural
embeddings

V_{j}\xhookrightarrow{\varphi_{j}}V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{j}\xhookrightarrow{\psi_{j}}V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{\widehat{l}}=V,

so
the restriction of

\mathtt{e}
to
{V_{j}}
is

\psi_{j}\circ\varphi_{j}
for each
j
.

We adopt the notation

V_{1,k}\equiv V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k}
,

\widehat{V}_{1,k}\equiv\psi_{k}(V_{1,k})=V_{1}\times\dots\times V_{k}\times\{\mathbf{0}\}\times\dots\times\{\mathbf{0}\}\subseteq V
,
so the above diagram becomes

\textstyle{V_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{e}}
\textstyle{V_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}
\textstyle{\widehat{V}_{1,k}\subseteq V.}

For
2\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
, we define
N_{k}
and
\mathring{N}_{k}
, both

(\widetilde{l}_{1}+\dots+\widetilde{l}_{k})\times(\widetilde{l}_{1}+\dots+\widetilde{l}_{k})
, by

N_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{1k}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0_{k1}&\dots&&A_{k}\end{matrix}\right],\mathring{N}_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{*}&0_{1k}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&&\\
\vdots&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
&&&A_{k-1}&0_{*}\\
0_{k1}&\dots&&0_{*}&0_{kk}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

We extend these to

(l\times l)
matrices
\widehat{N}_{k},{\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}}
by
filling in with zero blocks; note that
{\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}}=\widehat{N}_{k-1}
:

\widehat{N}_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{1k}&0_{*}&\dots&0_{*}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&C_{2k}&0_{*}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&&&&\\
0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&A_{k}&0_{*}&&\\
0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&\\
\vdots&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
0_{\widehat{l}1}&\dots&&&&\dots&0_{\widehat{l}\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right],{\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&0_{*}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&C_{*}&0_{*}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&&&&\\
0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&A_{k-1}&0_{*}&&\\
0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&0_{*}&0_{*}&\dots&\\
\vdots&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
0_{\widehat{l}1}&\dots&&&&\dots&0_{\widehat{l}\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right].

Since
A_{k}\leq N
, then
from Definition
4.7
,
\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{N/A_{k}dist}
denotes
those
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}N^{n}\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w}
exists. Note that
\mathbf{w}
is
N/A_{k}
–distinguished iff it is
N_{k}/A_{k}
–distinguished.

We define maps

\iota:\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}dist}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{V}_{{A_{\widehat{l}}dist}}\to\mathcal{V}_{N}

and

\iota_{k}:\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}\to\mathcal{V}_{N_{k}}
by

\iota\mathbf{w}=\iota(\mathbf{w})=\lim_{n\to\infty}N^{n}\mathtt{e}\mathbf{w}\;\;\;\text{and}

\iota_{k}\mathbf{w}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(N_{k})^{n}\varphi_{k}\mathbf{w}.

For
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{N_{k}}
, we define if the limit exists

\pi_{k}\mathbf{v}\equiv\lim_{n\to+\infty}({\mathring{N}_{k}})^{n}\mathbf{v}

Similarly, for
k\geq 2
, given
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}_{k}}
, we define, if the limit exists,

\widehat{\pi}_{k}\mathbf{v}\equiv\lim_{n\to+\infty}({\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}})^{n}\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}}^{\mathbf{0}}

The main result of this section is:

Theorem 4.19
.

(a nonstationary Frobenius–Victory theorem for matrices)

Assume that we are given a nonnegative column–reduced real matrix sequence
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in fixed–size Frobenius
form of (
23
) and with notations as above. Then:

(i) For each
2\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
,

\widehat{\pi}_{k}
defines a surjective map from
\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}_{k}}^{\mathbf{0}}
to

\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}_{k-1}}^{\mathbf{0}}
;

(ii)

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}=\iota(\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}dist}^{\mathbf{0}})+\dots+\iota(\mathcal{V}_{{A_{\widehat{l}}}dist}^{\mathbf{0}});
and in fact, defining

\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}})=\iota\mathbf{w}_{1}+\dots+\iota\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}},

then

\mathcal{I}:\Pi_{k=1}^{\widehat{l}}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\;\;\;\;\text{is\,a\,bijection.}

(iii)The map

\iota:\cup_{k=1}^{\widehat{l}}\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}\to\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{N}\;\;\;\;\text{is\,a\,bijection.}

We note that the initial streams by
construction come before the pool collections in the linear order

\preccurlyeq
, and that there is at least one,
\alpha_{1}
; they can be listed as

\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{s}
,
and
for the corresponding matrix sequences
A_{k}

we have

\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}=\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}
.
So
in part
(ii)
, the statement
can also be written as:

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\dots\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{s}}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{(s+1)}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\dots\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{\widehat{l}}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}\;\;\;\;\text{}{is\,a\,bijection.}

Proof.

For the rest of this subsection, we use the notation

\mathcal{V}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
,

\mathcal{V}_{1,k}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{N_{k}}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{N}_{k}}
,
{\mathring{\mathcal{V}}}_{{1,k}}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{N}_{k}}
and
{\mathring{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}}_{{1,k}}\equiv\mathcal{V}_{\mathring{\widehat{N}_{k}}}=\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{\widehat{N}_{k-1}}=\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k-1}
.

We have the conjugacies
indicated in the first two commutative diagrams; taking limits giving the
third diagram:

\textstyle{(\widehat{V}_{1,k})_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\mathring{\widehat{N}}}_{k})_{i}}
\textstyle{(\widehat{V}_{1,k})_{i}}
\textstyle{(V_{1,k})_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\psi_{k})_{i}}
\scriptstyle{{(\mathring{N}_{k})_{i}}}
\textstyle{(V_{1,k})_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\psi_{k})_{i+1}}

\textstyle{\widehat{V}_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathring{\widehat{N}}}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widehat{V}_{1,k}}
\textstyle{V_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathring{N}_{k}}}
\textstyle{V_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}

\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k-1}^{\mathbf{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}_{1,k}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathring{\mathcal{V}}}_{{1,k}}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}

To prove
(i)
,
we write
N_{k}
in block form as

N_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}N_{k-1}&C\\
0&A_{k}\end{matrix}\right]

which equals

\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]
in the notation of Theorem
4.18
.
By assumption
N
is column–reduced, whence so is
A=N_{k-1}
.
Also,

B=A_{k}
is either a primitive
or identically zero (Def.
4.17
) matrix sequence, fitting the
hypothesis of Theorem
4.18
. Applying
(i)

of that theorem, we have that the map

\pi_{k}:\mathcal{V}_{1,k}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathring{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k}^{\mathbf{0}}=\mathcal{V}_{1,k-1}^{\mathbf{0}}

is surjective, and hence so is

\widehat{\pi}_{k}
, by conjugation with the bijection

\psi_{k}
as in the diagram.

To prove
(ii)
, we note first that from the definitions, for each
k
with
1\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
we have these commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{\widehat{V}_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{N}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widehat{V}_{1,k}}
\textstyle{V_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{e}}}
\scriptstyle{{\varphi_{k}}}
\textstyle{V_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{N_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}
\textstyle{V_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}

\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,k}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{N}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{{\iota_{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}_{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k}}

The proof will be by induction on the number
\widehat{l}
of blocks on
the diagonal.
Supposing the statement holds for
k-1
blocks, we show it for
k
. Writing

\mathcal{I}_{j}
for the map at level
j
, the induction hypothesis provides a bijection

\mathcal{I}_{k-1}:\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\dots\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{k-1}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{1,{k-1}}^{\mathbf{0}}

Using the
block decomposition written above,
we have, from
(ii)
of Theorem
4.18
, a
bijection

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{V}_{1,k-1}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{1,k}^{\mathbf{0}}
. Writing
id
for the identity map on

\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}
,
we then have the composition of bijections

\mathcal{I}_{k}=\mathcal{I}\circ(\mathcal{I}_{k-1},\text{id}):(\mathcal{V}_{A_{1}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\times\dots\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{k-1}dist}^{\mathbf{0}})\times\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}\to\mathcal{V}_{1,k}=\mathcal{V}_{N_{k}}^{\mathbf{0}}.

This proves
(ii)
. The proof of
(iii)
follows by induction in just the same way.
∎

To summarize, the
nonnegative eigenvector sequences with eigenvalue one for
N
have been classified in
terms of the much simpler primitive case (for which, see
[
Fis09
]
), as the extreme points for
N

correspond bijectively to the extreme points for the
distinguished eigenvector sequences of the primitive blocks

A_{k}
. Specifically, such an eigenvector sequence

\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{v}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
has a
unique decomposition: there are unique
\mathbf{w}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}^{\mathbf{0}}=\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}\cup{\mathbf{0}}
(see Remark
4.5
) at
least one nonzero, such that

\mathbf{v}=\iota(\mathbf{w}_{1})+\iota(\mathbf{w}_{2})+\dots+\iota(\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}})=\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}})=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
\mathbf{v}_{3}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v}_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{w}_{1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{2}\\
\mathbf{0}_{3}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}_{1,2}\\
\mathbf{w}_{2}\\
\mathbf{0}_{3}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]+\,\cdots\,+\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{u}_{1,\widehat{l}}\\
\mathbf{u}_{2,\widehat{l}}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{u}_{\widehat{l}-1,\widehat{l}}\\
\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]

The maps
\widehat{\pi}_{k}
project along the filtration of cones

\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\mathbf{0}}=\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,\widehat{l}}^{\mathbf{0}}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,2}^{\mathbf{0}}\supseteq\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}^{\mathbf{0}}
, as follows:

\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,\widehat{l}}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}_{\widehat{l}}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,\widehat{l}-1}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}_{{\widehat{l}}-1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}_{{3}}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,2}^{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}_{2}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}^{\mathbf{0}}}

with

\widehat{\pi}_{{\widehat{l}}}\mathbf{v}=\iota(\mathbf{w}_{1})+\iota(\mathbf{w}_{2})+\dots\iota(\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}-1}).

(30)

So iterations determine successively
\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}},\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}-1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{1}
,
with
\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}}
the last coordinate of
\mathbf{v}-\widehat{\pi}_{\widehat{l}}\mathbf{v}
,

\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}-1}
the last coordinate of
(\mathbf{v}-\widehat{\pi}_{\widehat{l}-1}\mathbf{v})-\widehat{\pi}_{\widehat{l}}\mathbf{v}
,
and so on.
Some of the
\mathbf{w}_{k}
may be (identically) zero: this is
always the case for a
zero block at index
k
on the diagonal, and
furthermore if
\mathbf{v}
is an extreme point then all but one of the
\mathbf{w}_{k}

is zero.

We see later (Remark
5.5
) that (
30
) corresponds to
the ergodic decomposition of a central measure.

4.7.
Distinguished eigenvector sequences, general case (subdiagrams)

Now we are ready to consider the general case, with nested sequences
of nonnegative integer matrices
M\leq\widehat{M}
and corresponding
nested Bratteli diagrams
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}}}.

As noted in
(ii)
of Remark
2.44
,
without loss of generality we can take
\mathcal{A}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

We study this via the the
canonical cover matrix
, see
Theorem
2.58

setting

\widetilde{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{M}&C\\
0&M\end{matrix}\right],

with the sequence
C
satisfying

C_{i}=\widehat{M}_{i}-M_{i}
for each
i\geq 0
.
Thus we shall need to compare two notions of distinguished: with respect to
\widehat{M}
and

\widetilde{M}
; see Corollary
4.22
.

For this, we begin with two lemmas. To show that the linear algebra in
these proofs remains valid in more
generality, the statements here are for real entries. Below we
specialize to our main case of Bratteli diagrams, i.e. for integer
entries, where the “canonical cover” is really a cover.

Suppose we are given an
(l_{i}\times l_{i+1})
real nonnegative matrix
sequence
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with upper triangular block
decomposition
as in (
24
). Thus,

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]

(31)

with subalphabet sequences denoted
\alpha,\beta
.
Recalling the partial product notation
N_{i}^{n}=N_{i}\cdots N_{n}
of Definition

2.33
, we have:

Lemma 4.20
.

For
N
real nonnegative and upper triangular as in (
31
), defining, for
i\geq 0
and
n\geq i
,
blocks

\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}
so as to satisfy the equation

\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]_{i}^{n}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{i}^{n}&\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\\
0&B_{i}^{n}\end{matrix}\right],

(32)

(i)
then equivalently,

\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}=\sum_{k=i}^{n}A_{i}^{k-1}C_{k}B_{k+1}^{n}\text{ where }A_{j}^{j-1}=I_{(\alpha_{j}\times\alpha_{j})},B_{j}^{j-1}=I_{(\beta_{j}\times\beta_{j})}

(33)

(ii)
For a vector sequence
\mathbf{w}\in V_{\beta}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]
exists iff both

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}

exist.

(iii)
In particular,

\mathbf{w}
is
N/B
-distinguished
iff

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}
and for all
i\geq 0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}

exists.

Proof.

The definition of
\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}
in (
33
) is equivalent to:

\widehat{C}_{i}^{i}=C_{i}
, and, for
n\geq i
,

\widehat{C}_{i}^{n+1}=A_{i}^{n}C_{n+1}+\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}B_{n+1}

(34)

We prove (
33
) from this:
we verify that
\widehat{C}_{i}^{i}=C_{i}
in (
33
) as well, then assume as an induction
hypothesis that (
33
) holds for
\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}
, from which,
by way of (
34
), this follows
for
(n+1)
.

Now from (
32
),

N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\\
B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right],

proving the first claim.

From Definition
2.31
,

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}
means
\mathbf{w}
is a nonnegative and never zero fixed point for

B
. So
B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=\mathbf{w}_{i}
, and thus

\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]
exists iff

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
exists.
Therefore
\mathbf{w}
is
N/B
-distinguished iff
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{B}
and this converges for all
i\geq 0
.
∎

In an important special case this condition
simplifies considerably:

Lemma 4.21
.

For
N
as in the previous lemma, let now
B_{i}\leq A_{i}
be the same size,
(\widetilde{l}_{i}\times\widetilde{l}_{i+1})
for
i\geq 0
.
Let
C_{i}=A_{i}-B_{i}
for all
i
. Let
\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}
be defined by
(
33
)
above.
Then:

(i)

\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}=A_{i}^{n}-B_{i}^{n}.

(ii)
A vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
N/B
-distinguished
iff
\mathbf{w}
is
A/B
-distinguished.

For the next three statements we assume that
D\leq B
, with both of the same size.

(iii)
A vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
N/D
-distinguished
iff

\mathbf{w}
is both
B/D
and

A/D
-distinguished.

(iv) If

\mathbf{w}
is both
N/D
and

B/D
-distinguished, it is
A/D
-distinguished.

(v) If

\mathbf{w}
is

B/D
-distinguished, then it is
A/D
-distinguished iff
it is
N/D
-distinguished.

Proof.

Assuming the statement is true for
n
, then
from (
34
) we have that

\widehat{C}_{i}^{n+1}=A_{i}^{n}C_{n+1}+\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}B_{n+1}=A_{i}^{n}(A_{n+1}-B_{n+1})+(A_{i}^{n}-B_{i}^{n})B_{n+1}=A_{i}^{n+1}-B_{i}^{n+1},

so we are done by induction with
(i)
.
Then applying part
(iii)
of Lemma
4.20
proves
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
, we are to show that for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}
, then
convergence of
\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
is equivalent to
convergence of
\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
, for all

i\geq 0
.

But by
part
(ii)
of Lemma
4.20
, convergence of

\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
is
equivalent to
that of
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
. By

(i)
above, convergence of
\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
is
equivalent to that of

\lim_{n\to\infty}(A_{i}^{n}-B_{i}^{n})\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
. Thus
convergence of

\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
is
equivalent to
that of
\lim_{n\to\infty}(A_{i}^{n}-B_{i}^{n})\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
,
and hence to

\mathbf{w}
being both
B/D
and
A/D
-distinguished.

For
(iv)
,
we are to show that for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}
, then
convergence of
\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}

is equivalent to
convergence of
\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
for all
i\geq 0
.
Now convergence of

\lim_{n\to\infty}N_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
is
equivalent to
that of
\lim_{n\to\infty}(A_{i}^{n}-B_{i}^{n})\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
, which in turn implies convergence of

\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}
.
Part
(v)
follows logically from
(iii)
and
(iv)
.
∎

Corollary 4.22
.

(We number these so as to match the
corresponding parts of the previous lemma).
Given nested nonnegative real matrix
sequences

A\leq N\leq\widehat{N}
, with
\widetilde{N}
the canonical cover matrix (defined
by the formula of Theorem
2.58
), then

(ii)a vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{N}/N
–
distinguished
iff it is
\widetilde{N}/N
–
distinguished.

(iii)
A vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
\widetilde{N}/A
–
distinguished iff it is both
N/A

and
\widehat{N}/A
–
distinguished.

(iv) If
\mathbf{w}
is both
\widetilde{N}/A
–
distinguished and

N/A
–  distinguished, it is
\widehat{N}/A
–
distinguished.

(v)
If

\mathbf{w}
is

N/A
-distinguished, then it is
\widehat{N}/A
-distinguished iff
it is
\widetilde{N}/A
-distinguished.

Proof.

To prove
(ii)
: As noted in
(ii)
of Remark
2.44
, we can without loss of generality assume
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}
,
so
\widehat{N}
and
N
have the same size; we then directly apply part

(ii)
of Lemma
4.21
. To prove parts
(iii),(iv),(v)
we apply
the corresponding parts of the
Lemma.

∎

Remark 4.23
.

Part
(v)
of the Corollary is used in the proof of
(iii)
of Theorem
4.27
.
Parts
(ii)
and
(iii)
are used in the proof of Theorem

5.3

(iii)
below.

Next we see that the definition of distinguished has
a weaker formulation, which will prove useful
in applications.

Proposition 4.24
.

For
N\leq\widehat{N}
, as above with
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}
, then a vector sequence
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{N}/N
distinguished iff

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
and
\liminf_{n}||\widehat{N}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}||<\infty
, for
infinitely many
i
.

Proof.

For the canonical cover matrix

\widetilde{N}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{N}&C\\
0&N\end{matrix}\right]

with
C=\widehat{N}-N
, and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
, then as in the proof of Lemma
4.16
,

\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}=\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]+\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}C_{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{matrix}\right]

so

\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\leq\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}.

Since by hypothesis the lim inf of the norms is bounded, we have that by compactness of the
intersection of the positive cone with a ball, there exists
a subsequence
n_{j}
such that
\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n_{j}}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n_{j}+1}
converges. We have just seen that

\widetilde{N}_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{w}\end{matrix}\right]_{n+1}

is nondecreasing in
n
, giving
convergence for the sequence itself.
This is true for each
i\geq 0
, so
\mathbf{w}
is
\widetilde{N}
–  distinguished. Finally from Corollary

4.22
,

\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{N}/N
distinguished as well.
∎

As a consequence we have:

Corollary 4.25
.

The notion of being
\widehat{N}/N
– distinguished
is preserved under the taking of gatherings and dispersals. Moreover
this notion only depends on the tail of the matrix sequence.

Proof.

For
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, we recall that given a
subsequence
0=n_{0}<n_{1}<\dots
, the gathered
matrix sequence
K
is

K_{i}=N_{n_{i}}^{n_{i+1}-1}
. If
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
, then
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}=\mathbf{w}_{n_{i}}\in\mathcal{V}_{K}
,
since

K_{i}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i+1}=\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}
. The reverse holds, i.e. a sequence in

\mathcal{V}_{K}
can be uniquely interpolated to one in
\mathcal{V}_{N}
, so this
correspondence is bijective.

Now given
N\leq\widehat{N}
, we claim that then for
\widehat{K}
,
K
the
gathered
sequences,
if
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{N}
, and if the
corresponding sequence
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}
is

\widehat{K}/K
– distinguished, then
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{N}/N
– distinguished.
But in that case

\widehat{K}_{i}^{m}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{m+1}=\widehat{N}_{n_{i}}^{n_{m+1}-1}\mathbf{w}_{n_{m+1}}

converges, and hence by the Proposition

\lim_{j\to\infty}\widehat{N}_{i}^{j}\mathbf{w}_{j+1}
exists, for each
i\geq 0
.

It follows that the notion of distinguished is unchanged by erasing
a finite initial part of the matrix sequence.
∎

Remark 4.26
.

In particular, passage from a fixed–size Frobenius block form

(\widehat{N}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
to
(\widehat{N}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, where
\widehat{N}_{0}
may not have the
fixed size, does not affect the notion of distinguished. See also Remark
3.19
.

Now we return to nested Bratteli diagrams and hence nonnegative
integer entries for our matrix sequences
M,\widehat{M},\widetilde{M}
.

Theorem 4.27
.

(Frobenius–Victory Theorem for nested Bratteli diagrams) Let
\mathfrak{B}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}},\widehat{M}}
be a reduced Bratteli diagram with
bounded alphabet size.

(i)
Let

\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{M}}.
Then
there exists an eventually unique maximal primitive
M\leq\widehat{M}
,
such that there exists
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{M}
which
is
\widehat{M}/M
– distinguished, with
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}=\iota(\mathbf{w})
.

(ii) Let
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{M}}.
Then there exist unique
primitive submatrices
\mathcal{A}_{k}
with disjoint streams and a unique
decomposition of
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
as a sum
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}=\iota\mathbf{w}_{1}+\dots+\iota\mathbf{w}_{\widehat{l}}
where
\mathbf{w}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}
.

(iii)
Suppose we are given nested Bratteli diagrams, that is,
M\leq\widehat{M}
, and
let
\widetilde{M}
denote the canonical cover matrix.
Suppose

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{M}
. Then there exists a maximal primitive
A\leq M

and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
such that
\mathbf{v}
is
M/A
distinguished. Furthermore,
\mathbf{v}
is

\widehat{M}/A
distinguished iff it is
\widetilde{M}/A
distinguished.

Proof.

(i)
By the nonstationary Frobenius Decompostion Theorem

3.11
, there exists a reordering of the alphabets and a
gathering such that the diagram has fixed-size Frobenius normal form
for times
\geq 1
. From Remark
4.26
the change of
starting time from
0

to
1
does not affect the property of being distinguished.

Now, without loss of generality
we assume
\widehat{M}
is in this form.
Applying Theorem
4.19
, since
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{M}}
,
there exists a unique
k
and
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
such that

\iota(\mathbf{w})=\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
.
M\equiv A_{k}\leq\widehat{M}
is the unique maximal primitive
submatrix sequence.

(ii) Next we apply part
(ii)
of
Theorem
4.19
.

(iii) We apply part
(v)
of Corollary

4.22
. (Note that this works because, as in the Corollary, we can assume the alphabets
for
M
,
A
have been extended to that of
\widehat{M}
.) Then by
(i)
applied to
M
,
we are done.
∎

5.
The classification of invariant Borel measures

Here we come to our main goal: to identify the
ergodic invariant
measures for
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
(or equivalently
from
(ii),(iii)
of Proposition
2.29
for adic
transformations) under the assumption that
the measure is
finite on
some subdiagram. The notion of distinguished eigenvector sequence
(Def.
4.7
)
provides a
necessary and sufficient condition for the measure to be finite. We
shall begin with the stronger assumption that the measure is finite
on some nonempty open subset; this will lead us to the general
case. After treating
adic transformations, we carry the analysis over to adic towers.

We harvest the work of the previous sections, first in
§
5.1
assuming the upper
triangular form of §
4.6
(i.e. fixed–size Frobenius
normal form).
Our approach is inspired by
[
BKMS10
]
; in particular,
the proof of
(i)
follows the line of reasoning for the stationary case in Lemma 4.2 in
that paper. Then, in §
5.2
we
address the general case of invariant Borel measures
which are positive finite on some sub-Bratteli diagram. Lastly we
apply these results to the study of some simple
examples.

5.1.
The upper triangular block case

In this and the following subsection,
\nu_{\mathbf{w}}
denotes the measure on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
which is defined from
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{M}
in
(
8
) of Theorem
2.37
. For

A\leq M
, we denote by
\nu^{A}_{\mathbf{w}}
the measure on
\Sigma_{A}^{0,+}
defined
from
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{A}
, as in Definition

2.38
.

With
A_{k}\leq M
the diagonal blocks of
M
from the fixed–size Frobenius
normal form of Definition
3.3
, see Fig.
(
23
),
then
\mathcal{A}^{k}=(\mathcal{A}^{k}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
denotes the subalphabet sequence associated
to the matrix sequence
A_{k}
.

In the next theorem we show there is a bijective correspondence of the

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
– invariant conservative ergodic Borel measures
on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
with those on
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
, given by restriction.
Via this correspondence we identify
the measures which are finite on some open subset, and those which have finite total
mass.

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume we are given a
sequence of nonnegative integer
matrices
(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in fixed–size Frobenius
normal form with diagonal
blocks
A_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
(so in particular these blocks are either
identically zero or
reduced primitive), and
a nonzero ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
– invariant Borel measure
\nu
on

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
. Then:

(i)
There exists
a unique

k
,
1\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
, such that
the restriction

\nu_{{A_{k}}}
of
\nu
to
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
is
positive.
This measure is
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{A_{k}}
– invariant and
ergodic, with
\nu
its invariant extension to

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
. It
is either positive infinite, or
positive finite, on all nonempty open
subsets of
\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
. This correspondence, between ergodic
Borel measures on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
and on
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
for some

k
, is bijective. Also, one is conservative iff the other is.
Note that here
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
is in general not open
in
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.

(ii)

\nu
is positive
finite on some open set of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
if and only if

\nu_{{A_{k}}}
from
(i)
is positive finite, if and only if

\nu_{{A_{k}}}
equals
\nu^{A_{k}}_{\mathbf{w}}
for some
point
\mathbf{w}
in an extreme ray of

\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
.

The measure
\nu
is itself finite iff
\mathbf{w}
is

M/A_{k}
–distinguished, i.e.

\nu=\nu_{\iota(\mathbf{w})}
.

Proof.

(i):
From the hypothesis, there exists an edge cylinder set
[f_{0}\dots f_{m}]
of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
with

\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{m}])
positive finite or infinite. Define
k
to be
the maximal index
from
1
to
\widehat{l}
such that there exists an allowed extension
f_{0}\dots f_{m}f_{m+1}\dots f_{q}
of
this word satisfying
\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{m}f_{m+1}\dots f_{q}])>0
, with
f_{q}^{+}\in\mathcal{A}^{k}
.
Then since

(A_{k})_{0}^{\infty}
is reduced, there exists an allowed word
e_{0}\dots e_{q}
within the stream

\alpha_{k}
such that
e_{q}^{+}=f_{q}^{+}
.
By invariance

\nu([e_{0}\dots e_{q}])=\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{q}])>0
.
Now
\nu([e_{0}\dots e_{q}])=\nu([e_{0}\dots e_{q}]\cap\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+})
,
since for any subcylinder
[e_{0}\dots e_{q}g_{q+1}\dots g_{t}]

of positive measure then
g_{q+1},\dots,g_{t}
must be in

\alpha_{k}
, for if
g_{i}\in\alpha_{j}
with
j<k
this string would
not be allowed by the upper triangular form, while if
j>k
the measure is zero since
k
is
maximal.
Since
\nu
is
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
-invariant, it follows that, a fortiori, the restricted measure

\nu_{{A_{k}}}
is
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{A_{k}}
– invariant.
We
denote by
\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}
the tower
measure on
\Sigma_{M/A_{k}}^{0,+}\subseteq\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
. This is
the
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
-invariant extension of
\nu_{{A_{k}}}
from Theorem

2.49
. By
(i)
,
(ii)
of that theorem the invariant ergodic Borel measures on base

\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
and adic tower
\Sigma_{M/A_{k}}^{0,+}

correspond bijectively. Now

\nu_{{A_{k}}}<<\nu

whence

\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}<<\nu
, and both are
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
– invariant, thus

\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}=\nu
:
they are equal on the base
\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
of the
tower, hence on the tower,
which is invariant and thus must be a.s. the whole space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
by ergodicity of
\nu
.

By Lemma
2.62
, because of the
primitivity of
A_{k}
, the measure
\nu_{{A_{k}}}

is either positive finite or infinite on all open subsets of
\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
.

By
(i)
of Theorem

2.49
the invariant Borel measures on base

\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
and adic tower
\Sigma_{M/A_{k}}^{0,+}

correspond bijectively, and by

(ii)
of that theorem the ergodicity of

\nu=\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}
and
\nu_{{A_{k}}}
correspond, as well.

To complete the proof of part
(i)
, we show the base and tower have been uniquely identified in the
above construction,
by verifying the uniqueness of this index
k
. Suppose that for some

j\neq k
, the restricted measure

\nu_{{A_{j}}}
is positive; without loss of generality
j>k
. Let
j

be the maximal such index. Then there exists an edge cylinder set
[f_{0}\dots f_{m}]
with

\nu_{{A_{j}}}([f_{0}\dots f_{m}])
positive. Define
l
to be
the largest index
such that there exists an allowed extension
f_{0}\dots f_{m}f_{m+1}\dots f_{q}
of
this word satisfying
\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{m}f_{m+1}\dots f_{q}])>0
, with
f_{q}^{+}\in(\mathcal{A}_{l})_{q+1}
. Then as above there is a
cylinder
[e_{0}\dots e_{q}]
in
\Sigma_{A_{l}}^{0,+}
with

e_{q}^{+}=f_{q}^{+}
and with positive
\nu
-measure. But then
l=j
since

j
was maximal. This implies in particular that the
stream
\alpha_{j}
can have no front-connections to
\alpha_{k}
. But then by
the same reasoning as given above for
k
, the tower
measure
\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{j}}}
is equal to
\nu
and hence to

\widetilde{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}
, which implies that there are front-connections to

\alpha_{k}
, since then
\Sigma_{A_{j}}^{0,+}
belongs to the tower

\Sigma_{M/A_{k}}^{0,+}
, a contradiction.

From
(ii)
of Theorem
2.49
, a measure on the tower
is conservative iff that holds for the restriction to the base.

We have noted in the statement of
(i)
that
\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
itself is in general not an open
subset of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
; see Proposition
3.17
and
Cor.
3.18
.

(ii):
If
\nu
is finite positive on some open subset of

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, equivalently we have
0<\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{m}])<\infty
for
some thin edge cylinder of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
. Then by the above reasoning there
exists an extension
f_{0}\dots f_{m}f_{m+1}\dots f_{q}
and
e
in the stream
\alpha_{k}
with
e_{q}^{+}=f_{q}^{+}
so that
0<\nu_{{A_{k}}}([e_{0}\dots e_{q}])=\nu([f_{0}\dots f_{q}])<\infty
. Therefore by part
(i)
,
\nu_{{A_{k}}}
is
finite on all
open sets of
\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}
and hence in particular
\nu_{{A_{k}}}
has finite
total mass.

Equivalently
by Theorem
2.37
,

\nu_{{A_{k}}}=\nu^{A_{k}}_{\mathbf{w}}
for some

\mathbf{w}
in an extreme ray of

\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
.

Lastly we consider the case where
\nu
itself is finite.
Now by Theorem
2.37
, there is a bijection
\Phi

from the collection
\text{Ext\,}{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}}_{M}
of
finite ergodic central measures
\nu
to

\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M}^{\Delta}
. We recall the definition of this map. For
\Phi(\nu)=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i},\dots)
, then
for any
s\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, where
e_{i-1}^{+}=s
, we have that
\nu([.e_{0}\dots e_{i-1}])=(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i})_{s}.

On the other hand, from part
(iii)
of Theorem

4.19
there is a bijection
\iota
from

\cup_{k=1}^{\widehat{l}}\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
to
\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M}
.
Thus there is a unique

j
and
\mathbf{w}^{j}\in\text{Ext}_{\text{dist}}\mathcal{V}_{A_{j}}
with
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\iota(\mathbf{w}^{j})=(\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots,\mathbf{u}^{j-1},\mathbf{w}^{j},\mathbf{0}^{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{0}^{\widehat{l}})
, where the upper index is used for for the stream
decomposition and the lower index will be reserved for time.

Now since
\mathbf{w}^{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{A_{j}},
it determines a measure

\nu^{A_{j}}_{\mathbf{w}^{j}}
on
\Sigma_{A_{j}}^{0,+}
by the similar formula: for each cylinder set

[.e_{0}\dots e_{i-1}]
of
\Sigma_{A_{j}}^{0,+}
with
e_{i-1}^{+}=s\in\mathcal{A}_{i}^{j}
,
\nu^{A_{j}}_{\mathbf{w}^{j}}([.e_{0}\dots e_{i-1}])=(\mathbf{w}^{j}_{i})_{s}
. But
this is exactly equal to the previous value, for these
s\in\mathcal{A}_{i}^{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Hence

\nu^{A_{j}}_{\mathbf{w}^{j}}=\nu_{{A_{j}}}
, i.e. it is the restriction of
\nu

to

\Sigma_{A_{j}}^{0,+}
. Since
\mathbf{w}^{j}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A_{j}}
, this measure is
ergodic for the action of
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
on
\Sigma_{A_{j}}^{0,+}
.

From part
(i)
, the index
j
is unique, so
j=k
from parts
(i)

and the first part of
(ii)
above. That is,
\mathbf{w}
as above equals
\mathbf{w}^{j}
, and so
since
\iota(\mathbf{w}^{j})=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{w}
is
M/A_{k}
–distinguished and

\nu=\nu_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\nu_{\iota(\mathbf{w})}
.

∎

Remark 5.2
.

We note that in case
(ii)
above both measures are conservative, since
the base measure is finite hence this holds by the Poincaré
Recurrence Theorem, see Remark
2.24
.

We comment on an important but subtle technical point.
At some points in this paper we have used
reduced matrix sequences and at others, only
column–reduced. In the above theorem, we only needed the primitive diagonal
blocks reduced, but did not assume this for
M
; this flexibility
proves useful in the proof of part
(iii)
of Theorem
5.3
.

For other examples, in the Frobenius decomposition
theorem,
starting with a reduced matrix sequence
we showed we could achieve diagonal blocks which are either zero or
reduced
primitive.
However in the
Frobenius–Victory Theorem, both the inductive
(2\times 2)
block case of Theorem
4.18
and the general case

4.19
,we were careful to prove everything under the
weaker requirement of the matrices and diagonal block submatrices
being column–reduced. The reason for this
is that otherwise we could have a problem in the inductive step of the
general case, as the property of

N
being reduced may not be inherited by the
submatrices
N_{k}
to which we apply the
(2\times 2)
block step. By
contrast, being
column–reduced
is
passed on from
N
to
N_{k}
.

Of course our ultimate focus is on the measure theory, where the basic result of
Theorem

2.37
relates the invariant Borel measures to the nonnegative eigenvector
sequences of eigenvalue one. And for that theorem we again need the
condition of being reduced: both for the full matrix sequence and for
the primitive diagonal blocks. Now fortunately that much is guaranteed
by the Frobenius Decomposition Theorem.

In summary, we use reduced at the beginning (for the Decomposition
Theorem), and at the end (to conclude about the measure theory), while
in the middle, to prove the Frobenius–Victory Theorem, we need to
to work with the weaker condition of being column-reduced.

5.2.
The general case

In this section we bring together the ingredients developed
throughout the paper – the
stream and
Frobenius decompositions, the definition of distinguished eigenvector
sequence for a subdiagram, the notion of the
canonical cover, and the nonstationary Frobenius-Victory theorem – to
prove in Theorem

5.3
our main result: a characterization of the invariant measures for a
Bratteli diagram
in terms of measures on a subdiagram or sub-subdiagram and related
distinguished eigenvector sequences. See Example
5.4

and the further examples in §
5.3
.

Beginning with an
ergodic

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
–invariant Borel measure
\nu
on the path space
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}

of a Bratteli diagram
\widehat{\mathfrak{B}}
with matrix sequence
\widehat{M}
, we show
in part
(i)
that
\nu
determines an eventually unique maximal
primitive reduced subdiagram
\mathfrak{B}
with matrix sequence

M
. Its path space
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
is the base of an adic tower
on which the restriction
\nu_{M}
of
\nu
to
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
can
be finite or infinite. In part
(ii)
we consider
measures which are positive finite on some open set of
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
,
with finiteness of
\nu
characterized
in terms of
\widehat{M}/M
distinguished eigenvector sequences.
This extends to general diagrams the case of fixed-size (upper triangular) Frobenius
form from Theorem
5.1
.

For the proof we first
use the Frobenius decomposition to find an eventually unique maximal (ordered by
containment) primitive subdiagram. Then we use the
fixed–size Frobenius normal
form proved in Theorem

3.11
together with
Theorem
5.1
to
describe the invariant Borel measures.

Now to achieve the fixed-size form from the streams we needed to permute
the alphabets and gather the matrix sequence; so now we must transfer
this analysis
back to the streams and hence to
the orginal diagram. For this we have
from Remarks
3.12
,
that a change of order on the alphabets, or a gathering, induces a
topological conjugacy of the shift spaces and
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-actions.
Furthermore, by Remarks
3.19
,
4.26

Proposition

2.39
,
and
Corollary
4.25
these operations and also a
change of the starting time does not affect
the collections of invariant Borel measures or
distinguished eigenvector sequences. We also make use of the eventual
uniqueness of the stream collection and Frobenius decomposition from
Theorem

3.9
and Theorem

3.11
. That does not affect the
analysis of invariant measures, also because these edge spaces are
identical after some fixed time, using Corollary
2.56
.

The main subtlety of the theorem comes in the proof of
part
(iii)
where we consider the case much more general than
(ii)

of a measure perhaps not finite on any open subset but finite on a
subdiagram. For such a subdiagram defined by
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}
, and further for a sub-subdiagram defined by
M\leq M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}
, we specify which of these measures are finite or infinite in
terms of distinguished eigenvector
sequences.

For the proof we apply part
(ii)
of Theorem

5.1
twice, once for

M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}

and once for a further subdiagram
M\leq M^{\prime}
. To carry this out we first
achieve an upper triangular form, but this requires several steps. The first step,
in the case of a general subdiagram, is to build the canonical cover. That
allows us to
place the submatrix
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}
as a
diagonal block in the cover matrix
\widetilde{M}
. If
M^{\prime}
happens to be primitive we are then
back in the situation of
(ii)
, and are done.
If not, we find an upper triangular form for
M^{\prime}
, reordering the subalphabet for
M^{\prime}
by
the Frobenius Decomposition Theorem, and finding a primitive diagonal
block
M
with positive measure. Here we make use of Proposition

2.67
that the canonical cover is measure isomorphic
to the original path space.
We keep track of this reordering by
a permutation
matrix, so that we can carefully follow the changed eigenvector sequence.
That the resulting eigenvector sequence is indeed
distinguished then makes full use of our analysis of the
(2\times 2)
-block case, specifically via two applications of Corollary
4.22
.

This leads in Corollary
5.7
to an improved measure count.
In Theorem
5.6
we describe, similarly, measures for
adic towers.

Theorem 5.3
.

(Measure classification for Bratteli diagrams of bounded rank)
Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{B}}\equiv\widehat{\mathfrak{B}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}},\widehat{M}}
be a reduced Bratteli diagram with
bounded alphabet size, with alphabet sequence
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, and let
\nu
be an ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{\widehat{M}}
– invariant Borel
measure.
Then:

(i)
There exists an eventually unique maximal primitive reduced
M\leq\widehat{M}
,
with alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, such that the
restriction
\nu_{M}
of
\nu
to the path space
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
is positive.
This measure is

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
– invariant and ergodic, and is positive infinite, or
positive finite, on all open
subsets of
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
, and
\nu=\widehat{\nu}_{M}
is the invariant
extension of
\nu_{M}
on the base
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
to its tower

\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
.

This correspondence, between ergodic Borel
measures on
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
and on
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
for some
maximal primitive reduced
M\leq\widehat{M}
, equivalently on a tower
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
,
is bijective, up to eventual uniqueness of the sequence
M
.
Also, one is conservative iff the other is.

(ii)

\nu
is positive
finite on some open set of
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
if and only if

\nu
is positive finite on the maximal primitive subdiagram

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
from
(i)
,
if and only if

\nu_{M}
equals
\nu^{M}_{\mathbf{w}}
for some
point
\mathbf{w}
in an extreme ray of

\mathcal{V}_{M}
.

The total measure
\nu(\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+})
is finite iff
\mathbf{w}
is

\widehat{M}/M
–distinguished, iff

\nu=\nu_{\overline{\mathbf{w}}}
for
\overline{\mathbf{w}}={\iota_{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{w})}
.

(iii)
Suppose that
\nu
is positive finite
for some subdiagram with matrix sequence
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}
, thus

0<\nu(\Sigma_{M^{\prime}}^{0,+})<\infty
. (This includes the case in
(ii)

of being finite on some open subset).
Then
\nu
is the tower measure over
(\Sigma_{M^{\prime}}^{0,+},\nu_{M^{\prime}})
,
which is ergodic.
We know there exists a unique
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{M^{\prime}}=\nu_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}
.
There exists an eventually unique maximal primitive reduced
sub-subdiagram with matrix sequence
M\leq M^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M}
such that
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\iota_{M^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})
, and

\nu
also is the tower
measure over

(\Sigma_{M}^{0,+},\nu_{M})
. The measure
\nu

is finite iff
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is
\widehat{M}/{M^{\prime}}
– distinguished,
iff
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{M}/M
– distinguished.

(iv)
Lastly, suppose that
\nu
is ergodic and infinite on a subdiagram defined by
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}
. Such measures are analyzed further as in

(i)-(iii)
. That is, there exists an eventually unique maximal primitive
reduced
\widetilde{M}\leq M^{\prime}
,
such that
\nu_{\widetilde{M}}
is positive. If
\nu_{\widetilde{M}}
is finite, we proceed as
in
(ii)
. If it is finite on some further subdiagram, for
\widetilde{M}
, we proceed as in
(iii)
.

Proof.

of Theorem
5.3
:

(i), (ii)
:
If
\widehat{M}
happens to be in fixed–size Frobenius normal form, then
parts
(i)
,
(ii)
are just those parts from Theorem

5.1
. That is to say, from that theorem there is a unique
k
such that

{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
has positive measure; and then

M=A_{k}\leq\widehat{M}
is the eventually unique maximal primitive reduced
submatrix sequence claimed in
(i)
. Part
(ii)
follows.

Now we consider general
\widehat{M}
.
To prove
(i)
, first,
if
\widehat{M}
is primitive, then
M=\widehat{M}
. If not, then
let
\{\alpha(1)\dots,\alpha(d),\mathcal{P}\}

be the stream decomposition guaranteed by
Theorem
3.9
. By permuting each alphabet we can
place the matrix sequence in Frobenius normal form. This stream
decomposition is eventually unique. By performing a (non-unique)
gathering, we can achieve fixed–size Frobenius normal form, with primitive reduced
diagonal blocks
A_{l}
.

Writing for simplicity
\nu
also for the measure on
this new path space, then from part

(i)
of Theorem
5.1
, there is a unique

k
such that the restriction of
\nu
to
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
is
positive, the restricted measure
\nu_{{A_{k}}}
is invariant and
ergodic, and the tower measure
\widehat{\nu}_{{A_{k}}}
over
\nu_{{A_{k}}}

equals
\nu
. Further,

\nu_{{A_{k}}}
is positive infinite, or
positive finite, on all open
subsets of
{\Sigma_{A_{k}}^{0,+}}
.

Let
\{\alpha(1)\dots,\alpha(d),\mathcal{P}\}

be the list of ungathered streams associated to the diagonal blocks

A_{1},\dots A_{d}
and pool stream for the Frobenius normal form, so

\alpha(k)
denotes the stream associated to the (gathered)
matrix sequence
A_{k}
. We define
M
to be the ungathered matrix
sequence for this stream.

By Remarks
3.19
,
4.26

Proposition

2.39
,
Corollary
2.56
, and
Corollary
4.25
the operations of gathering,
alphabet permutation, and changing of the starting time do not affect
the collections of invariant Borel measures or
distinguished eigenvector sequences.

We claim that
M

is the eventually unique such matrix
sequence (that is maximal, primitive, reduced and with positive
measure).
Let

\alpha^{\prime}(j)
be a maximal primitive stream not in the list; then by
Theorem
3.9
there exists a stream
decomposition
\{\alpha^{\prime}(1)\dots,\alpha^{\prime}(d),\mathcal{P}\}
including

\alpha^{\prime}(j)
for some
1\leq j\leq d
, and by Theorem

3.11
, the two stream collections
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}

for the two Frobenius normal forms are eventually
equal. Hence if a stream
\alpha^{\prime}(j)
has positive measure for its
path space, then
the streams
\alpha^{\prime}(j)
and
\alpha(k)
are eventually equal.

The last part of
(i)
is the bijection between measures. But due to
the uniqueness just proved, this now follows from
(i)
of Theorem

5.1
.

Part
(ii)
is proved using now part
(ii)
of Theorem

5.1
, as everything remains valid for our choice
M\equiv A_{k}
.

Proof of
(iii)
:
By taking
smaller alphabets
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, we can assure that the matrix sequence
{M^{\prime}}
is reduced, see part
(i)
of Remark

2.44
.
We apply Theorem

3.11
to this
reduced sequence: we
reorder
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and
gather so that
{M^{\prime}}

is in
fixed–size Frobenius normal form
with diagonal blocks
A_{1},\dots A_{\check{l}}
.

Since by hypothesis
\nu_{M^{\prime}}
is positive finite, by
(ii)
of Theorem

5.1
there exists a unique primitive reduced subblock

A_{k}
on the diagonal of
{M^{\prime}}

and a unique

\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}
, with

\nu_{M^{\prime}}=\nu_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}
, where
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\iota_{M^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})
.

Now we would like to be able to choose
M=A_{k}
as the matrix
M\leq M^{\prime}
with
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}.
However,
M^{\prime}
does not itself appear as a diagonal block of
\widehat{M}
, so
neither do its subblocks
A_{l}
.

This is where the use of the canonical cover matrix
\widetilde{M}
will be
crucial.
We note that by Proposition
2.67
the canonical cover
introduces a measure isomorphism.
A version of
M^{\prime}
will
appear as a diagonal block of the cover matrix
\widetilde{M}
. By doing this
carefully we can make use of the fixed-size normal form already achieved for

M^{\prime}
with
A_{k}
as one of the diagonal blocks.

To do this,
we augment the alphabet sequence for
{M^{\prime}}
from
\mathcal{A}

to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, as in part
(ii)
of Remark

2.44
. We then order
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
by placing the symbols of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\mathcal{A}
last. Now this augmented version, also called
{M^{\prime}}
,
may no longer be reduced, but it is still in fixed-size
