normal (upper triangular) form, as these last symbols only add zero matrix elements,
including a zero block on
the diagonal.

Then we construct the
canonical cover matrix
\widetilde{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{M}&C\\
0&{M^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right]
where
C=\widehat{M}-{M^{\prime}}
,
as in Theorem
2.58
. The alphabet
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
remains ordered
as above. We use this alphabet for the
block
\widehat{M}
(that is, the original
\widehat{M}
has been conjugated by a sequence of
permutation matrices), but now we use a copy
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
for

M^{\prime}
, so the alphabet for the cover matrix is

\widehat{A}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
. Thus the subblock

{M^{\prime}}
is still in
fixed–size Frobenius normal form.

We define
M
to be the diagonal subblock of
M^{\prime}
, now with augmented
alphabet
\widehat{A}^{\prime}
, which corresponds to
A_{k}
; this will be the claimed eventually unique
primitive reduced subsequence. As above, we have the unique

\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M}
, with

\nu_{M^{\prime}}=\nu_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}
, where
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=\iota_{M^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})
.
Of course this implies that
\mathbf{w}
is
M^{\prime}/M
–distinguished.

We claim that
\nu
itself is finite iff
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is
\widehat{M}/{M^{\prime}}
–
distinguished, iff
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{M}/M
–  distinguished.
Now we know from
(ii)
of Theorem
2.37
that
\nu
being
finite ergodic is
equivalent to

\nu=\nu_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}}
for some
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{\widehat{M}}
. The proof will
be complete if we show that
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}=\iota_{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=\iota_{\widehat{M}}(\mathbf{w})
.

At this point, we have reordered the alphabets

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and its copy
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
in the same way, so as to put the
subblock
{M^{\prime}}
of
\widetilde{M}
in
fixed–size Frobenius normal form.
We next apply Theorem

3.11
to the subblock
\widehat{M}
so as to put it in fixed–size Frobenius
normal form as well, however this time
reordering

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}

without
changing the order of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, so as to keep the work
already done there.

The result is that (after a second gathering) this second reordering
has conjugated
\widetilde{M}
via
Q

of the form

Q=\left[\begin{matrix}P&0\\
0&I\end{matrix}\right]
, where
P
is a permutation matrix sequence,
to

\widetilde{M}^{\prime}=Q\widetilde{M}Q^{-1}=\left[\begin{matrix}P\widehat{M}P^{-1}&PC\\
0&{M^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{M}^{\prime}&C^{\prime}\\
0&{M^{\prime}}\end{matrix}\right]

in fixed–size Frobenius normal form with
diagonal blocks
\widehat{A}_{0},\dots,\widehat{A}_{\widehat{l}},A_{1},\dots A_{\check{l}}
. (These are all sequences, so this
means that for all
i\geq 0
,

\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{i}=Q_{i}\widetilde{M}_{i}Q^{-1}_{i+1}
.)

Since we have not reordered
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, we no longer have that

C^{\prime}
equals
\widehat{M}^{\prime}-{M^{\prime}}
. Nonetheless, the nonnegative eigenvector sequence
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}
for

\widetilde{M}
determines that for
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}
: it
is
Q\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}
is in
fixed–size Frobenius normal form, we can apply
(ii)
of Theorem

5.1

to conclude that
Q\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}=\iota_{\widetilde{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})
, where
\mathbf{w}
is a
nonnegative eigenvector sequence for one of the diagonal subblocks of
\widehat{A}_{0},\dots,\widehat{A}_{\widehat{l}},A_{1},\dots A_{\check{l}}
. But this must be the subblock

M=A_{k}
of
M^{\prime}
already found above, by the uniqueness in part
(i)
of Theorem

5.1
, applied now to
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}
. (This only
required the diagonal primitive blocks being reduced, not the full
matrix; see Remark
5.2
).

We write
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{M}
for the alphabet sequences of
\widetilde{M},\widetilde{M}^{\prime},M=A_{k}
respectively.
Recalling from Definition
4.7
that
\mathtt{e}
denotes the
embedding of vector sequences, so
\mathtt{e}_{\widetilde{M}^{\prime}}:V_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}\to V_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}}
and
\mathtt{e}_{\widetilde{M}}:V_{\mathcal{A}_{M}}\to V_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}
,
we note that
\mathtt{e}_{\widetilde{M}^{\prime}}=Q\mathtt{e}_{\widetilde{M}}
since we are just permuting
the
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
– coordinates of the embedding. Therefore

Q\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}=\iota_{\widetilde{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}(\widetilde{M}^{\prime})^{n}\;\widetilde{\iota}_{\widetilde{M}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{w})=\lim_{n\to\infty}Q\widetilde{M}^{n}Q^{-1}Q\mathtt{e}_{\widetilde{M}}(\mathbf{w})=Q\iota_{\widetilde{M}}(\mathbf{w})

whence

\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}=\iota_{\widetilde{M}}(\mathbf{w})
. So in particular,
\mathbf{w}
is
\widetilde{M}/M
–  distinguished.

We claim that also

\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}=\iota_{\widetilde{M}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})
.
But the inverse of

\iota
is a projection, and (as in the proof of
(ii)
of Theorem

5.1
),
since
\nu
restricts to
\nu_{M^{\prime}}
which in turn restricts to
\nu_{M}
,
also
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}
projects to
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
on
\Sigma_{{M^{\prime}}}^{0,+}
, which projects to

\mathbf{w}
on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, verifying the
claim.

This shows that

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is
\widetilde{M}/{M^{\prime}}
–
distinguished. But according to part
(ii)
of Corollary
4.22
,
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is

\widetilde{M}/{M^{\prime}}
– distinguished iff it is
\widehat{M}/{M^{\prime}}
– distinguished.

Now we know that
\mathbf{w}
is

{M^{\prime}}/M
– distinguished and
\widetilde{M}/M
– distinguished.
Therefore, by part
(iii)
of Corollary
4.22
,
\mathbf{w}
is

\widehat{M}/M
– distinguished.

The gatherings we have employed do not affect these conclusions: from Corollary

4.25
,
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{w}
are
distinguished for the original (non-gathered) sequence.

This completes the proof of
(iii)
. Part
(iv)
follows the previous parts.

∎

Example 5.4
.

Integer Cantor sets (Nested odometers)

Let us consider our two models for the Integer Cantor Set
(see Example
2.66
) in the light of this theorem. The first model is the infinite measure
\nu
on the
triadic odometer, with
the constant matrix sequence
\widehat{M}_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}3\\
\end{matrix}\right]
for all
k\geq 0,
and its subset of the embedded dyadic
odometer, with matrix sequence
M\leq\widehat{M}
where

M_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}2\\
\end{matrix}\right]
. The second is the canonical cover of this, with matrix
sequence

\widetilde{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{M}&C\\
0&M\end{matrix}\right]

with
C
satisfying

C_{i}=\widehat{M}_{i}-M_{i}=[2],
so
\widetilde{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}3&1\\
0&2\end{matrix}\right]
;
see
Fig.
4
.
For the first model, both
\widehat{M}
and
M
are primitive matrix
sequences and hence give primitive path spaces. But the embeddings of the path space

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
are completely different in the two larger spaces

\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
and
\Sigma_{\widetilde{M}}^{0,+}
.
In the cover space
\Sigma_{\widetilde{M}}^{0,+}
it is open, indeed the tower
\Sigma_{\widetilde{M}/M}^{0,+}
is
open dense in
\Sigma_{\widetilde{M}}^{0,+}
, and
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
itself is the maximal primitive subset containing

\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
, while in
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
it is neither open
(its tower
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
is a dense set with empty
interior in
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
, see Examples
2.66
and
5.14
.)
nor the maximal primitive path space containing
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
(as
that is
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
).

For part
(ii)
, considering the cover
\widetilde{M}
, then the subdiagram for
M
is a maximal reduced
primitive subdiagram with finite measure (Bernoulli measure of the dyadic
odometer), and the eigenvector sequence for this is
not

\widetilde{M}/M
- distinguished, whence the tower measure is infinite.

Regarding the first model (with
M\leq\widehat{M}
), part
(i)
tells us that since the measure
is infinite on the open set
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
, it is
locally infinite. For the first model, part
(ii)
does not say much
as
\widehat{M}
is
the maximal primitive subdiagram and the measure is infinite
there.

Now for this model part
(iii)

is more appropriate and interesting:
there is a subdiagram (that given by
the embedding of
M
) on which the measure is finite.
Thus part
(iii)
for the first model corresponds to part
(ii)
for
the second.

For an example of nested odometers which exhibits finite total measure, let
0<b_{k}
such that
\sum_{0}^{\infty}b_{k}=c<\infty
. Then for
a_{k}=e^{-b_{k}}

define
n_{k}=\lceil 1/a_{k}\rceil
, where this denotes the least integer greater than or
equal to
1/a_{k}
.

Then
1/n_{k}\leq a_{k}
, so
we have

0<\varepsilon\equiv e^{-c}=\Pi_{0}^{\infty}a_{k}\leq\Pi_{0}^{\infty}1/n_{k}<1.

Let
\widehat{M}
be
the
(1\times 1)
matrix sequence defined for
k\geq 0
by
\widehat{M}_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}n_{k}\\
\end{matrix}\right]
. The unique
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}-
invariant Borel measure on

\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
is nonstationary Bernoulli measure. That is,
take
\nu_{k}(\{e\})=1/n_{k}
for each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}_{k}=\{1,\dots n_{k}\}
and set

\nu=\otimes_{0}^{\infty}\nu_{k}
, the product measure. Define
M\leq\widehat{M}
by removing one edge from
each matrix; thus,
M_{k}=\left[\begin{matrix}n_{k}-1\\
\end{matrix}\right]
.
We have removed the set with edge
n_{k}
for each
k
, which by independence has measure

\Pi_{0}^{\infty}1/n_{k}>\varepsilon
.
Thus
0<\nu(\Sigma_{M}^{0,+})<1-\varepsilon
.

The unique
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
-invariant measure on
\nu(\Sigma_{M}^{0,+})
is a constant times the restriction

\nu_{M}
, and is strictly positive and
<1
. This gives a nonmaximal primitive stream
different from that of the unique maximal stream
\widehat{M}
.

We note that a similar construction produces a nested sequence

\dots M^{(2)}\leq\dots M^{(1)}\leq M^{(0)}\leq\widehat{M}
such that

\nu(\Sigma_{M^{(k)}}^{0,+})
decreases to any desired
c\geq 0
. These are
nested odometers on
nested closed subsets whose intersection is given by a
subdiagram
which may have measure
0
.

Remark 5.5
.

We note that statement
(ii)
applies immediately to the following more general situation: that there exists a gathering of
\widehat{M}^{\prime}

of
\widehat{M}
,
such that there exists
M^{\prime}\leq\widehat{M}^{\prime}
as in
(ii)
. This is more
general because e.g. erasing a single edge in the diagram for
\widehat{M}^{\prime}

gathered along the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\geq 0}

at time
k

corresponds to removing an edge cylinder set
[e_{n_{k}}\dots e_{n_{k+1}-1}]
, not a single edge (which is a larger set). A related point was made in the proof of Proposition

2.39
. A concrete example is given by the nested rotations of
Example
5.18
, where the subshift defined by
removing edges from the diagram for the
multiplicative continued fraction cannot be realized by removing edges
for the additive continued fraction.

By removing edges from nested subsequences which define gatherings
(i.e. by nested telescoping of the Bratteli diagrams) one can
produce a sequence of gathered subdiagrams
such that their measure
decreases to some
c\geq 0
, but
such that the intersection is a closed set which is
not
itself
given by a subdiagram. We mention that it could be interesting to further investigate
such examples, and to consider measures which are finite on some
closed subset.

Summarizing, we have completely analyzed the measures which are finite on some
subdiagram. Then,
if it is infinite, we can, by part
(iv)
of the theorem, look for a further subdiagram with

M^{\prime}\leq M
such that
0<\nu(\Sigma_{M^{\prime}}^{0,+})<\infty
.

We have as a corollary of Theorem
5.3
:

Theorem 5.6
.

(Measure classification
for adic towers)
Given nested Bratteli diagrams
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E},M}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\widehat{\mathcal{E}},\widehat{M}}
with
bounded alphabet size, let

\nu
be an ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
– invariant
Borel measure on the adic tower
{\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}}
. Then:

(i)The restriction
\nu_{M}
of
\nu
to
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
is a
positive ergodic invariant Borel measure, whose tower extension
\widehat{\nu}_{M}
equals
\nu
.
The measure
\nu
is
positive finite on some open set of the tower iff

\nu_{M}
is
positive finite on some open set of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
.
In this case there exists
A
primitive with
A\leq M\leq\widehat{M}

such that
\nu_{A}
is positive finite, with
\nu
the tower extension
\widehat{\nu}_{A}
.

(ii) If
\nu
(ergodic invariant on the tower) is positive finite on
{\Sigma_{A}^{0,+}}
for some
A\leq\widehat{M}
, then
A\leq M
and
there exists a unique
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A}
such that
\nu_{A}=\nu_{\mathbf{w}}
with

\nu
the tower measure over
\nu_{\mathbf{w}}
on
{\Sigma_{A}^{0,+}}
.
Furthermore
\nu
has finite total mass iff
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{M}/A
– distinguished.

There exists
A_{0}\leq A
primitive and
\mathbf{w}\in\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A_{0}}
such that
\nu
is the tower measure over
\nu_{\mathbf{w}}
on

\Sigma_{A_{0}}^{0,+}
, with
\nu
finite iff
\mathbf{w}
is
\widehat{M}/A_{0}
– distinguished.

Proof.

From
(ii)
of Theorem
2.63
, since any invariant
measure
\nu
on the tower gives positive mass to
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
,
\nu

is the tower measure over
\nu_{M}
.
From
(iii)
of Theorem
2.63
,
\nu
is
positive finite on some open set of
{\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}}
iff

\nu_{M}
is
positive finite on some open set of
{\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}}
. The remaining
statements follow from
Theorem
5.3
.
∎

Now we return to the problem of estimating the number of ergodic
measures. Making use of the Frobenius decomposition, we get a new
proof of Proposition
2.42
with a generally better upper
bound, and now can also include the infinite measures.

We then have from Theorems
5.1
and
5.3
:

Corollary 5.7
.

(Counting the finite and infinite ergodic central measures) Let
(M_{i})_{i\geq 0}

be a sequence of nonnegative integer
matrices with bounded alphabet size. Without loss of generality,
assume these are in fixed–size Frobenius
normal form, with (zero or primitive) diagonal blocks
A_{k}
of size

\widetilde{l}_{k}
, for
1\leq k\leq\widehat{l}
.

Then the number of finite or infinite central measures
(i.e. the conservative ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}-
invariant
measures which are positive on some open subset), determined up to
multiplication by a positive constant, is equal to
\sum_{A_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}}\#\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}}^{\Delta}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\widehat{l}}\widetilde{l}_{k}\leq\liminf l_{n}
. The number of (finite)
central
measures equals the
number of distinguished extreme points
\#\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{M}=\sum_{A_{k}\neq\mathbf{0}}\#\text{Ext\,}\mathcal{V}_{A_{k}dist}^{\Delta}
. The number of infinite central measures equals the
number of nondistinguished extreme points.
∎

5.3.
Examples

Statement (
39
) below (that the associated measure is finite
iff this series converges) is independently presented in equation (6.10) of

[
BKMS13
]
. (Their series is
equivalent to ours after a change of starting time; transposed matrices
are used throughout that paper, so the order of multiplication is
reversed, and one has lower rather than upper
triangular Frobenius form). We give two
proofs, the first an application of our definition of distinguished
eigenvector sequences and the general result Theorem

5.1
, the second geometric, in Remark

5.10
. The geometry is simple because we are in the

(2\times 2)
case. In fact this geometric
argument, once reformulated abstractly, led to the general approach
including the above notion of distinguished.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
N_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}a_{i}&c_{i}\\
0&b_{i}\end{matrix}\right]
for
i\geq 0
with real entries
a_{i},b_{i}>0
and
c_{i}\geq 0
.
Writing
a_{0}^{k}=a_{0}a_{1}\cdots a_{k}
and

b_{0}^{k}=b_{0}b_{1}\cdots b_{k}
, we
define
\mathbf{w}_{0}=1
,
\mathbf{w}_{k+1}=(b_{0}^{k})^{-1}
; this is the unique normalized nonnegative eigenvector sequence with eigenvalue
1
for

[b_{i}]_{i\geq 0}
.
Then
\iota(\mathbf{w})
exists (i.e. for

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\end{matrix}\right]
,

\mathbf{w}
is an
N/B
- distinguished
eigenvector sequence) iff

\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{a_{0}^{k}}{b_{0}^{k}}\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}}<\infty.

(35)

In the special case where
a_{i}\geq b_{i}
and
c_{i}=a_{i}-b_{i}
, then
(
35
)
converges iff

\limsup\frac{a_{0}^{n}}{b_{0}^{n}}<\infty.

Proof.

From Definition
4.7
,

\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a
distinguished
eigenvector sequence iff
for all
{i\geq 0}
,

\iota(\mathbf{w})_{i}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}N_{i}^{n}(\mathtt{e}_{k}\mathbf{w})_{n+1}
exists.
Defining

\widehat{c}_{i}^{n}
by

N_{i}^{n}=\left[\begin{matrix}a_{i}^{n}&\widehat{c}_{i}^{n}\\
0&b_{i}^{n}\end{matrix}\right],

then equivalently
\widehat{c}_{i}^{i}=c_{i}
, and for
n>i

\widehat{c}_{i}^{n+1}=a_{i}^{n}c_{n+1}+\widehat{c}_{i}^{n}b_{n+1}

(36)

(compare (
34
)). For
i=0
we have

N_{0}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{c}_{0}^{n}(b_{0}^{n})^{-1}\\
1\end{matrix}\right]

and by induction, using (
36
), we get

\widehat{c}_{0}^{n}(b_{0}^{n})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{a_{0}^{k}}{b_{0}^{k}}\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}}.

(37)

Similarly, for
i\geq 0

N_{i}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{c}_{i}^{n}(b_{i}^{n})^{-1}\\
(b_{0}^{i-1})^{-1}\end{matrix}\right]

and now

\widehat{c}_{i}^{n}(b_{i}^{n})^{-1}=\sum_{k=i}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{k}}{b_{i}^{k}}\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}},

(38)

and since this converges as
n\to\infty
iff
the sum for
i=0
does, we indeed need only check (
35
).

For the special case, by
(ii)
of Lemma
4.21
,
\mathbf{w}
is distinguished iff

\lim{a_{0}^{n}}/{b_{0}^{n}}
exists (and is finite), but since

a_{i}/b_{i}\geq 1
this sequence is nondecreasing so the statement for
the
\limsup
is equivalent to this.

∎

That was the statement for real matrices; we next draw the consequence
for integer matrices and hence for adic transformations:

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
M_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}a_{i}&c_{i}\\
0&b_{i}\end{matrix}\right]
for
i\geq 0
with integer entries
a_{i},b_{i}>0
and
c_{i}\geq 0
. We consider the

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
– invariant
Borel
measures which are finite positive on some open subset of
\Sigma^{0,+}_{M}
.
Writing
a_{0}^{k}=a_{0}a_{1}\cdots a_{k}
and

b_{0}^{k}=b_{0}b_{1}\cdots b_{k}
,
then if

\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{a_{0}^{k}}{b_{0}^{k}}\frac{c_{k}}{a_{k}}<\infty,

(39)

there are exactly two such ergodic
invariant probability measures;
if the sum is infinite, there is
one such ergodic invariant probability measure and one such (up to
multiplication by a constant)

\sigma
– finite infinite conservative ergodic invariant
measure.

In the special case where
a_{i}\geq b_{i}
and
c_{i}=a_{i}-b_{i}
, then
(
39
)
converges iff

\limsup\frac{a_{0}^{n}}{b_{0}^{n}}<\infty.

Proof.

Considering first the
(1\times 1)
matrix sequence
[a]=[a_{i}]_{i\geq 0}
, with associated nonstationary edge shift space

\Sigma_{[a]}^{0,+}=\Pi_{0}^{\infty}\{1,\dots,a_{i}\}
,
then there is a unique ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{[a]}-
invariant
probability measure

\nu_{[a]}
.

This is
nonstationary Bernoulli (product) measure
with equal weights
1/a_{i}
.
There are two cases here: if
a_{i}=1
except for finitely many
1
,
then
\Sigma_{[a]}^{0,+}
is a finite set, with
\Pi_{0}^{\infty}a_{i}
point masses
permuted by
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{[a]}
;
otherwise
\Sigma_{[a]}^{0,+}
is a Cantor set (a nonstationary
odometer, see Example 1 of
[
Fis09
]
).

Considering
M_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}a_{i}&c_{i}\\
0&b_{i}\end{matrix}\right]
, since
\nu_{[a]}
extended to
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
is also
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
–  invariant, and is ergodic, we have our
first measure.

By the same reasoning, for the
(1\times 1)
matrix sequence
[b]=[b_{i}]_{i\geq 0}

there is a unique ergodic

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{[b]}-
invariant
probability measure

\nu_{[b]}
on
\Sigma_{[b]}^{0,+}
. By part
(ii)
of Theorem
5.1
, the second conservative ergodic
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{M}
–  invariant
measure on
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
which is positive on some open subset is
the tower measure
\widehat{\nu}_{[b]}
over the clopen set and tower base
base
(\Sigma_{[b]}^{0,+},\nu_{[b]})
.

The unique normalized nonnegative eigenvector sequence with eigenvalue
1
for

[b]

is
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with
\mathbf{w}_{0}=1
,
\mathbf{w}_{k+1}=(b_{0}^{k})^{-1}
.
From Theorem
5.1
,

\widehat{\nu}_{[b]}
is a finite measure iff
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a
distinguished
eigenvector sequence.
Applying the Lemma concludes the first proof.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Here is the promised geometric proof of Proposition
5.9
. Recalling that
C_{n}
denotes
the positive cone of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
at time
n
, the extreme rays of the nested cones

M_{0}^{n}C_{n}
are generated by the vectors

M_{0}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]
and

M_{0}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
1\end{matrix}\right];

the first gives, projectively, the vector

\left[\begin{matrix}1\\
0\end{matrix}\right]
while the second is projectively the same as

M_{0}^{n}\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
\mathbf{w}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right].

So the nested cones have as their intersection a single ray
iff
the slope of this last vector approaches
zero. This slope is the inverse of the sum in (
37
),
so goes to
0
iff the sum is infinite.
We have to check this also starting at time
i
, in which case the sum
is that in (
38
), and as above this converges iff the sum
starting at
0
does.
Hence, by Proposition
2.42
, we have a single nonnegative eigenvector
sequence of eigenvalue one, and so a single probability measure,
leading to the same conclusion as for the previous proof.

Here are some simple examples:

Corollary 5.11
.

If
in Proposition
5.9
for all
k
we have

a_{k}=a,b_{k}=b
and
0<\kappa_{1}<c_{k}<\kappa_{2}<\infty
,
then if
a<b
there exist exactly two
ergodic invariant probability measures which are finite positive on
some open subset of
\Sigma^{0,+}_{M}
; if
a\geq b

there exists exactly one such probability measure
and (up to normalization) one such
\sigma
– finite infinite conservative ergodic
invariant Borel measure on
\Sigma^{0,+}_{M}
.

If
a_{k}=a=b_{k}=b
(but not assuming the bounds on
c_{k}
),
then this second measure is finite iff
\sum c_{k}<\infty
.

Remark 5.12
.

The second possibility above can be visualized as two
systems
(take say
a=b=2
, giving two odometers) hooked together by an arrow
with
nonstationary probabilities

c_{k}
of the mass “leaking” over from the second to the first
system.

One can imagine generalizing this example to model two stationary dynamical
systems with nonstationary communication from the first to the second.

As a next example we reprove a result of
[
Fis92
]
: the Integer Cantor Set
transformation satisfies the following:

Definition 5.13
.

We say a homeomorphism of a Polish space is
infinite measure
uniquely ergodic
if
there is up to normalization a unique infinite invariant Borel measure
which is positive on
some open set.

Example 5.14
.

(the Integer Cantor Set inside the 3-adic odometer)
As in Example
2.66
, see Fig.
4
, we take

\widehat{M}_{i}=[3]
for all
i\geq 0
, with edge alphabet
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{i}=\{a,b,c\}
and
M_{i}=[2]
with
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\{a,c\}
. The canonical cover
matrix is

\widetilde{M}=\left[\begin{matrix}\widehat{M}&C\\
0&M\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}3&1\\
0&2\end{matrix}\right].

Now
\mathcal{V}_{M}^{\Delta}
has the single element,
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{0}\mathbf{w}_{1}\dots)
with
\mathbf{w}_{n}=2^{-n}
; then by Lemma
5.8
, or directly by checking the criterion of Lemma
4.21
part
(ii)
,
\mathbf{w}
is not
\widehat{M}/M
-
distinguished; indeed,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{M}_{i}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=3^{-i}\lim_{n\to\infty}3^{n}2^{-n}=+\infty
.

The
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}_{\widehat{M}}
-
orbit of
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
is the tower
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
,
a dense subset of
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
.

Since
\mathbf{w}
is not distinguished, by Theorem
5.1
, and as in Proposition
5.9
, the tower measure
\nu=\nu_{\iota(\mathbf{w})}
on
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}

is infinite on every open subset of the triadic odometer,
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
, and is finite on the compact
subset
\Sigma_{M}^{0,+}
(the dyadic odometer).

By part
(iii)
of Theorem
5.3
,
\nu
is up to normalization the unique
invariant Borel measure which is positive finite on an open set for the adic
cover tower
\Sigma_{\widetilde{M}/M}^{0,+}
,
and by Theorem
2.63
also for the adic tower

\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
. These towers are infinite measure uniquely ergodic.

By the special case in the Proposition, the ICS example generalizes to:

Corollary 5.15
.

(Nested nonstationary odometers)
Consider two nested odometers, the first the
(1\times 1)

nonnegative integer
matrix sequence
\widehat{M}
with
\widehat{M}_{i}=[a_{i}]
and the second
M\leq\widehat{M}
with

M_{i}=[b_{i}]
(so
b_{i}\leq a_{i})
.
Then the tower
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
has up to normalization a single
invariant ergodic measure which is positive on some (in fact all) open
sets; this is locally finite and inner regular, and is infinite
\sigma
– finite
iff

\limsup\frac{a_{0}^{n}}{b_{0}^{n}}=\infty.

On the space
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
the corresponding measure is
infinite on every open subset.

Remark 5.16
.

Henk Bruin asked us in a conversation (in 2007, after we gave a talk
about the ICS example at CIRM) about the existence of
other infinite invariant Borel measures. Afterwards, we realized there are many, as shown by the
next construction; by
Lemma
2.62
, these are infinite on
every open subset of the space.

Example 5.17
.

We take
\widehat{M}_{i}=[a_{i}]
with
a_{i}=2
for all
i\geq 0
(so
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
is the
dyadic odometer space) and
b_{i}=2
for
i
even
,=1
for
i

odd.
Then
{a_{0}^{2n}}/{b_{0}^{2n}}=2^{n}\to\infty
, so the tower measure is
infinite.

Next, let us consider
a_{i},b_{i}
as just defined, and
\widehat{a}_{i}=3
. Then for

\widehat{M}=[\widehat{a}]
,
M=[a]
and
N=[b]
, the adic tower map on
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}

gives the Integer Cantor Set transformation of the previous example, with measure
\nu
the unique (up to scaling) infinite
invariant Borel measure which is positive on open sets. Now
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/N}^{0,+}
has the infinite measure
\widetilde{\nu}
which is
positive on open sets of that tower
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
, but
which is infinite on every open subset of
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
(since
\widehat{M}=[2]
is
primitive, by
Lemma
2.62
).

Geometrically interesting examples of subdiagrams can be constructed
within
circle rotations, as follows:

Example 5.18
.

(Nested circle rotations) This example produces
interesting conservative ergodic measures for irrational circle
rotations, which are infinite on every nonempty open subset.

From
Example 3 of
[
Fis09
]
, also see
[
AF01
]
, we can
code an irrational circle rotation by an adic transformation, as follows.

Taking as alphabet sequence
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{A,B\}
for all
i\geq 0
,
we define
a pair of substitutions
\rho_{+},\rho_{-}
by

\displaystyle\rho_{+}(A)=AB,

\displaystyle\rho_{-}(A)=A,

\displaystyle\rho_{+}(B)=B,

\displaystyle\rho_{-}(B)=AB

The associated matrices (the abelianizations of the substitutions) are

P=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{matrix}\right],\;Q=\left[\begin{matrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right]

Given now a sequence
(n_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of positive integers, we
define
substitution and matrix sequences
(\rho_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
by

N_{i}=P
or
Q
, with
the first occuring
n_{0}
times, followed by the other
n_{1}
times and
so on and similarly for
\rho_{i}
. We call this a one-sided
additive family
as it is related
to the additive mapping family on the torus of
[
AF05
]

and to the additive continued fraction.

Now we gather the sequence
N
to form the
multiplicative
family

M
with

M_{i}=\left[\begin{matrix}1&0\\
n_{i}&1\end{matrix}\right]\text{for $i$ even,}\left[\begin{matrix}1&n_{i}\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right]\text{ for $i$ odd. }

The resulting adic
transformation (on the path space of the Bratteli diagram order given by the substitution sequence)
is isomorphic to an irrational circle rotation
R_{\theta}
of angle

\theta

defined by the exchange of the two intervals of lengths
1
and
\alpha

with

\alpha=[n_{0}\dots n_{k}\dots]\equiv\cfrac{1}{n_{0}+\cfrac{1}{n_{1}+\dotsb}}

Here
\theta=\alpha/(1+\alpha)
(and would be

1/(1+\alpha)
if we had started with parity
(-)
instead of
(+)
).

Now suppose we are given
(\widehat{n}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with
1\leq n_{i}\leq\widehat{n}_{i}
. Defining the sequence
\widehat{M}
, note that indeed
M\leq\widehat{M}
;
we call the resulting pair of nested adic transformations
nested circle
rotations
. This has geometrical meaning: indeed,
if
n_{i}<\widehat{n}_{i}
infinitely often, then the rotation for
n
naturally
embeds
in an order-preserving way as a rotation on a Cantor subset of the circle
rotation for
\widehat{n}
. And regarding the measures, building on a result of

[
AF05
]
together with this paper, we have:

Theorem 5.19
.

Given two nested circle rotations with
multiplicative
matrix sequences
M\leq\widehat{M}
, that is,
we have

n_{i}\leq\widehat{n}_{i}
for all
{i\geq 0}
,
let
\nu
denote the unique
central measure for
\Sigma^{0,+}_{M}
, writing
\widehat{\nu}
for its extension to the
adic tower

\Sigma_{\widehat{M}/M}^{0,+}
and for the corresponding
R_{\widehat{\theta}}

-invariant Borel measure on the circle.

Writing
\lambda_{i}=([n_{i}n_{i+1}\dots])^{-1}
and similarly for
\widehat{\lambda}
,
then
the tower measure
\widehat{\nu}
is inner regular; it is finite iff

\liminf||\widehat{M}_{0}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}||<\infty
, and that holds iff

\liminf({\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{n}})/({\lambda_{0}^{n}})<\infty.

When the measure is infinite, it is
infinite on every open subset of
\Sigma_{\widehat{M}}^{0,+}
(and of the
circle), hence is not (recall Definition
2.35
) Radon.

We mention regarding the infinite measure ergodic theory that at least
in the case of periodic combinatorics (nested quadratic irrational
rotations) one can go farther: following methods of

[
MS14
]
, one can prove an
order-two ergodic theorem as shown in
[
Fis92
]
for the Integer Cantor Set.
We thank
Solomyak for conversations regarding this, and the case of
nonperiodic combinatorics, which remains an intriguing question.
Note that
[
MS14
]
extends the discussion to
graph-directed sets and self-similar
tilings of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 5.20
.

Similar examples can be constructed for minimal
interval exchange transformations, by considering an induced map on a
subset of measure zero defined by a subdiagram, and the adic tower over that.
Explicitly, take a
path in the Rauzy graph which has iterated loops, gather along the
returns to a node of that loop, and define a
subdiagram with fewer iterates. The above is the simplest case, a minimal exchange
of two intervals, i.e. an irrational circle rotation.

6.
Appendix: Comparison with the classical (stationary)
theorems

Here we compare our nonstationary Perron-Frobenius, Frobenius Decomposition
and Frobenius–Victory theorems to the stationary case. In particular
we discuss Victory’s definition of distinguished
eigenvector sequence, relating that to
the construction of Parry measures.

We recall:
for a real or complex
(d\times d)
matrix
M
, having a right
eigenvector

\mathbf{w}

with
eigenvalue

\lambda
means by definition that for

\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
we have

M\mathbf{w}=\lambda\mathbf{w}
. For an example of a real matrix where
complex
\lambda
and
\mathbf{w}
come up naturally, see the permutation example
below.

Recall that a
(d\times d)
nonnegative real matrix
M
is

irreducible
iff for all
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, there
exists
n=n(a,b)\geq 1
such that
(M^{n})_{ab}>0
. It is

primitive
iff
\exists n\geq 1
which does not depend on the
entries, i.e. such that for all
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
,

(M^{n})_{ab}>0
.
A basic example of a
matrix which is irreducible but not primitive is a permutation matrix
with a single cycle, i.e. with one communicating class.

Now the
Perron-Frobenius Theorem as generally stated addresses the primitive
case, saying that there exists a
nonnegative right eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
, called the
Perron-Frobenius
eigenvector
and unique up to multiples;
\mathbf{w}
is
strictly positive; its
eigenvalue
\lambda
is (strictly) positive, and for any other
eigenvalue
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, we have
|\alpha|<\lambda.

Historically however, this part of the theorem is due to Perron, while
the full Perron-Frobenius theorem includes Frobenius’ contribution to
the wider irreducible case, which we next describe.

Definition 6.1
.

For irreducible
M
one defines
the
period
of a state
a\in\mathcal{A}
to be the greatest common
divisor (
gcd
)   of
\{n:M^{n}_{aa}>0\}
; the period of
M
is the
gcd
of the periods of its
states.

One shows that
the period is the same for all states in a communicating class, see
the clear proof in
[
LM95
]
and also below.

The statement of Frobenius’ theorem is the same as Perron’s except that now
|\alpha|\leq\lambda
(it may not be
strictly
less in modulus); and moreover
if
p
is the period of
M
, then collection of eigenvalues is invariant by rotation in the complex plane by angle

2\pi/p
, whence there are

p
nonnegative eigenvectors equal to
\lambda
times the
p^{\text{th}}
roots of
unity, with the rest being smaller in modulus.
See
[
Gan59
]
,
Theorem XIII, 2.1 and 2.2.

Theorem 6.2
.

(Stationary Perron-Frobenius Theorem)
Let
M
be a
(d\times d)
nonnegative
real matrix.

{(I)}
Primitive case (Perron): Let
M
be a
(d\times d)
nonnegative
real matrix. Assume that
M
is primitive. Then:

(i) there exists (up to multiplication by a constant) a
unique nonnegative right
eigenvector; it is strictly positive. Its eigenvalue
\lambda
is strictly
positive, and is equal to that for the
left nonnegative eigenvector.

(ii)
Any other eigenvalue
\alpha
has strictly smaller modulus:

|\alpha|<\lambda
.

{(II)}
Irreducible case, eigenvectors. (Frobenius)
Suppose that
M
is irreducible, with period
p
. Then:

(i)
there exists a nonnegative eigenvector, unique up to multiplication by
a positive constant. This is strictly
positive, and has a positive eigenvalue
\lambda^{+}
.

(ii)
There are another

p-1
eigenvalues, equal to
\lambda^{+}
times the
p^{\text{th}}
roots of
unity. All other eigenvalues have smaller modulus.

{(III)}
Irreducible case, eigenvector sequences.
There are

p
nonnegative eigenvector sequences of eigenvalue one. These
correspond to the
p
eigenvectors for
M^{p}
, one for each of the
diagonal primitive blocks.

For our purpose of studying
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}
-invariant Borel measures for
adic transformations, as we know from Theorem 2.9 of

[
BKMS10
]
, see
Theorem
2.37
above, what is important are the nonnegative eigenvector

sequences
of eigenvalue one. Thus
in the above theorem, part
II
which treats the eigen
vectors

plays no role: all we care about is parts
I
and
III
.

We can see this clearly with
a simple
example. For
a,b,c
positive real numbers, let

M=\left[\begin{matrix}0&a&0\\
0&0&b\\
c&0&0\end{matrix}\right]
. The characteristic polynomial is

p(\lambda)=\left|\begin{matrix}-\lambda&a&0\\
0&-\lambda&b\\
c&0&-\lambda\end{matrix}\right|=-\lambda^{3}+abc
so
M
has
three eigenvalues equal to the three
complex solutions of
\lambda^{3}=abc
. Only for one of these,
\lambda=\lambda^{+}
, the
Perron-Frobenius eigenvalue
is positive real.
The right eigenvector corresponding to each
\lambda
is
a multiple of
\mathbf{v}=(1,\lambda/a,\lambda^{2}/ab)
.The eigenvector
\mathbf{v}_{\lambda^{+}}
corresponding to

\lambda^{+}
is
called the
Perron-Frobenius
eigenvector
. Note that the other eigenvalues and eigenvectors are
given by multiplication of
\lambda^{+},\mathbf{v}_{\lambda^{+}}
by the three complex roots of
1
.

Now we can conjugate the stationary sequence
M,M,M,\dots
via a periodic sequence of permutation
matrices to the
periodic sequence

\left[\begin{matrix}a&0&0\\
0&b&0\\
0&0&c\end{matrix}\right],\left[\begin{matrix}b&0&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&a\end{matrix}\right],\left[\begin{matrix}c&0&0\\
0&b&0\\
0&0&a\end{matrix}\right]\dots.

Note that
M^{3}=\left[\begin{matrix}abc&0&0\\
0&bca&0\\
0&0&cba\end{matrix}\right]=abc\cdot I.

Choosing
a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we draw the Bratteli diagram for
M
, and
examine the
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{C}-
invariant Borel measures. Let us consider for
example

M=\left[\begin{matrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&2\\
3&0&0\end{matrix}\right]
.
We know that the central measures are
preserved by taking a gathering, e.g. in the stationary case by taking
powers of the matrix. For the Bratteli diagram of
M^{3}=6\cdot I

we see there are three embedded
6
-adic odometers, each giving an ergodic
measure. But how do we see this for the original matrix
M
? The
answer is given by the insight of Theorem 2.9 of

[
BKMS10
]
, see
Theorem
2.37
above: invariant Borel measures correspond (even in
the stationary case) not to the eigen
vectors
but to the nonnegative eigenvector

sequences
of eigenvalue one;
there is one associated to each symbol, and so there are three
extreme sequences, and three ergodic measures, one for each odometer.

Further, as above we can conjugate the sequence
M,M,M,\dots
to the
periodic sequence

\left[\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&2&0\\
0&0&3\end{matrix}\right],\left[\begin{matrix}2&0&0\\
0&3&0\\
0&0&1\end{matrix}\right],\left[\begin{matrix}3&0&0\\
0&2&0\\
0&0&1\end{matrix}\right]\dots
and now in the conjugate Bratteli diagram we clearly
see the three periodic nonstationary odometers, each a shift of the next.

Now the invariant Borel measures correspond, from Theorem
2.37
(even in a stationary
case, as here!) to the eigenvector
sequences
of eigenvalue one, and the
ergodic measures to the extreme points of this collection of
sequences. Defining
(\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1}\dots)
to be
(a_{0}^{-1}(1,0,0),a_{1}^{-1}((0,1,0),a_{2}^{-1}(0,0,1),\dots)
where
\underline{a}=(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}\dots)=(1,2,6;6\cdot(1,2,6);6^{2}\cdot(1,2,6);\dots)

then in this example the three extreme eigensequences of eigenvalue
one are (up to multiples) the three shifts of the sequence
\mathbf{w}
. For
the untwisted matrix
\widetilde{M}
, the three eigenvector sequences are
the three shifts of
\underline{a}
(up to a constant multiple) times
the standard basis vectors.
Note that although for
M
there are three eigenvectors, as above,
(1,\lambda,\lambda^{2}/2)
where
\lambda
are the three solutions of

\lambda^{3}=6
, these do
not
play a role in finding the
three ergodic invariant Borel measures. Indeed, for the simplest case

a,b,c=1
, then the Perron-Frobenius eigenvector is
\mathbf{v}=(1,1,1)
, but as
an eigenvector sequence this is not an extreme point- it is in the
middle of the unit simplex; in this case since
3
is odd it is also the
unique real eigenvector. The three extreme points of the simplex are
permuted by
M
, and these give the three eigenvector sequences:

(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},\dots)
and its shifts, each
defining an invariant Borel measure (each a point mass, in this case). The
Perron-Frobenius eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
does give an eigenvector sequence,
the constant sequence
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v},\dots)
and hence a measure, but this is
nonergodic being a convex combination of the point masses. So, once
again, the Perron-Frobenius eigenvector plays no special role in the
measure theory, and the right way to understand things is in terms of the
eigenvector sequences.

Something very similar to this example occurs for a general
irreducible
nonnegative
(d\times d)
matrix
M
. The Frobenius theory then says
the following. The alphabet can be partitioned into
p
subsets called

period classes
(see Proposition 4.5.6 of

[
LM95
]
) with
l_{i}\geq 1
elements for
1\leq i\leq p
,
so
l_{1}+l_{2}+\dots+l_{p}=d
;
reordering
\mathcal{A}
accordingly,
M
has the
(p\times p)
block form of a
permutation matrix
with nonzero blocks
B_{i}
which are
(l_{i}\times l_{i+1})
for
i<p
and
(l_{i}\times l_{1})
for
i=p
; taking for example
p=3
,

M=\left[\begin{matrix}0&B_{1}&0\\
0&0&B_{2}\\
B_{3}&0&0\end{matrix}\right]
.

Frobenius’ theorem then identifies the eigenvalues and eigenvectors, saying that the spectrum of
M
is invariant
with respect to multiplication by the

p^{\text{th}}
roots of unity. In particular,
M
has exactly
p

eigenvalues of modulus
\lambda
, multiples
by the
p^{\text{th}}
roots of unity.
For more on this theory see especially
[
LM95
]
, also
[
Gan59
]
and

[
BKMS10
]
.

We can understand this as follows. But moreover, we can find the nonnegative
eigenvector sequences of eigenvalue one, which are what we need for
the invariant Borel measures; as above, the eigenvectors themselves are of
no help there.

Thus,
for
A_{1}=B_{1}B_{2}B_{3},A_{2}=B_{2}B_{3}B_{1},A_{3}=B_{3}B_{1}B_{2}
we have

M^{3}=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&0&0\\
0&A_{2}&0\\
0&0&A_{3}\end{matrix}\right]
. Since the diagonal blocks are
primitive, there is a further power with diagonal blocks all strictly
positive. Once again, we can untwist the Bratteli diagram by
conjugation with a
periodic sequence of permutation matrices, to an untwisted matrix
\widetilde{M}
, with three diagonal primitive subblocks
B_{i}
. As before, the three
eigenvectors for these blocks correspond, in the
original diagram, to three eigenvector sequences
which differ by a time shift.

For the conclusion of Frobenius’ theorem,
note that each of the

(l_{i}\times l_{i})
matrices
A_{i}

has the same collection of eigenvalues. (This is easy to prove directly
by seeing how the eigenvectors correspond.)

Again, the ergodic measures are given not by these

p
eigenvectors for
M
, but rather by the
p
nonnegative eigenvectors for the
untwisted matrix
\widetilde{M}
.

Note that from the cone point of view, as in the projective metric
proof of the Perron-Frobenius Theorem for the primitive case, there
are three subcones of the positive cone which are permuted and mapped
into each other, nesting down to the extreme sequences.

6.1.
Comparison with Parry measures

Given a
(d\times d)
primitive matrix
A
, for simplicity with
entries
0,1
, the
adic-invariant
central measure
\nu
and the shift-invariant Borel measure of maximal entropy (the
Parry measure)
\mu
are both unique and have closely related formulas. For
the irreducible case this situation changes dramatically, as we
explain.

It will be convenient to use the following matrix formalism in
describing these measures. Let
\mathbf{v}^{t},\mathbf{w}
be the left and right
Perron-Frobenius eigenvectors of
A
, with eigenvalue
\lambda.
We
normalize the vectors so that
\mathbf{v}\in\Delta

and
\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{w}=1
. We write
\mathbf{1}
for the
(d\times 1)
column
vector of all
1^{\prime}s
, and
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{t}
for the probability row vector with
entries
v_{i}w_{i}
. We define
W
to be the diagonal matrix with entries

W_{ii}=w_{i}
.
Then
W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{w}
and so

P\equiv\frac{1}{\lambda}W^{-1}AW

(40)

satisfies
P\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
, i.e.
P
is row–stochastic; moreover,

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{t}P=\boldsymbol{\pi}^{t}
.

The formula (
40
) is just a matrix version of the familiar
Parry-Shannon formula

P_{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{w_{j}}{w_{i}}A_{ij}.

(41)

We define the
Parry measure

\mu
on the vertex shift space
\Sigma_{A}^{0,+}
by

\mu[x_{0}\dots x_{n}]=\pi_{x_{0}}P_{x_{0}x_{1}}\cdots P_{x_{n-1}x_{n}}=\lambda^{-n}v_{x_{0}}w_{x_{n}}

where we use (
41
) to calculate the collapsing product.
It follows from
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{t}P=\boldsymbol{\pi}^{t}
that this definition is
shift-invariant, so
\mu
extends to an invariant probability measure on the bilateral
shift space

\Sigma_{A}
.

By contrast, the central measure only depends on the right nonnegative eigenvector:

\nu[x_{0}\dots x_{n}]=\lambda^{-n}w_{x_{n}}.

This agrees with our definition in
(
7
) since
(\mathbf{w})=(\mathbf{w}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
with
\mathbf{w}_{k}\equiv\lambda^{-k}\mathbf{w}
is an
nonnegative eigenvector sequence of eigenvalue
1
.

Now consider the irreducible case.
There the Perron-Frobenius eigenvectors
\mathbf{v}^{t},\mathbf{w}
are still unique,
and the same formula gives the Parry measure
\mu
, again the unique
measure of maximal entropy (equal to
\log\lambda
).

However as we have seen, for ergodic central measures the Perron-Frobenius eigenvector itself plays
no role, and there are now
p
such measures, each governed by
one of the
p
extreme nonnegative eigenvector sequences.

We offer two explanations for this striking contrast to the primitive case.
First, there are in fact
p

nonstationary

Parry measure
sequences
, see
[
Fis09
]
, periodic of
period
p
,
and each of entropy

\log\lambda
. In fact, in the above example,
each lives on a
sub
-subshift, given by the
p

periodic matrix sequences such as
N_{0},N_{1},N_{2},\dots
where

N_{0}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&B_{1}&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right],N_{1}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&B_{2}\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right],N_{2}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
B_{3}&0&0\end{matrix}\right]
. Again, conjugation by a periodic sequence of
matrices straightens out
(N_{i})
so we are actually studying the periodic

sft
given by the
primitive periodic
sequence
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},\dots
.

A second explanation
comes via Lemma
2.4 of Bowen and Marcus in

[
BM77
]
, where the uniqueness of the central
measure for a
primitive
sft
was proved via the mixing of the Parry measure,
which could be summarized as
“mixing of the hyperbolic dynamics implies unique
ergodicity of the transverse dynamics”
.
But it is exactly in the irreducible nonprimitive case that the Parry measure
is
not
mixing, and indeed, as we have seen, unique ergodicity
fails as there are
p

central measures where
p
is the period of the matrix, see Def.
6.1
.

6.2.
Comparison with the classical Frobenius–Victory Theorem

Frobenius went on from his study of the irreducible case to analyze
the stationary reducible case.
This second case, as we now explain, is much more involved.
Here the tools are the Frobenius decomposition and
the Frobenius–Victory theorem.

The Frobenius decomposition theorem, see §4.4 of

[
LM95
]
, equation (69) §XIII.4 of
[
Gan59
]
, and
equation (4) of
[
BKMS10
]
,
states that a nonnegative square matrix

N
can be put
in upper triangular block form

N=\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&C_{12}&\dots&C_{1\widehat{l}}\\
0_{21}&A_{2}&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0_{\widehat{l}1}&\dots&&A_{\widehat{l}}\end{matrix}\right]

(42)

with
A_{j}
square matrices that are zero or irreducible. Using what we have just seen,
by taking a power, we can achieve that the diagonal blocks
(now possibly larger in number) are zero or primitive.

We recall the statement of
the Frobenius–Victory theorem, so named e.g. in

[
TS00
]
.
See Proposition 1 of
[
Vic85
]
,
Theorem 6 of §XIII.4 of
[
Gan59
]
, and Theorem 3.1 of

[
BKMS10
]
; apparently the theorem
is actually due to Frobenius. We shall explain how this
agrees with the
nonstationary version above in Theorem
4.19
.

Here is the standard definition (following Victory) of distinguished
eigenvalue, eigenvector and communicating class:

Definition 6.3
.

Given a
(\widehat{l}\times\widehat{l})
nonnegative real matrix

N
, then an eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
, with eigenvalue
\lambda
, is

distinguished
iff it is
nonnegative, and in this case
\lambda
is a
distinguished eigenvalue
. If
\beta
is the class of collection of
communicating states
corresponding to
\mathbf{w}
, then
\beta
is a
distinguished class
iff its
eigenvalue

\lambda_{\beta}
is strictly greater than
\lambda_{\alpha}
for any
collection of communicating states
\alpha

such that
\alpha
communicates to
\beta
.

Note that the eigenvector for
\beta
is a Perron-Frobenius
eigenvector for an irreducible matrix corresponding to
\beta
, since
this is a communicating class.

In terms of the upper triangular form of the single matrix
N

in upper triangular block form of (
42
),
this
means the following: letting
C_{kj}^{(n)}
denote the
kj
-block of

A^{n}
, then
an eigenvector
\mathbf{w}
for
A_{j}
is distinguished
iff

||A_{j}||>||A_{k}||

for all
k
such that for some
n\geq 0
(hence for all larger
n
)
the block
C_{kj}^{(n)}
is nonzero.

Note that since for an irreducible matrix the Perron-Frobenius
eigenvalue
is the same for
all

Thus for example if all the upper blocks
C_{kj}
are nonzero, then
the Perron-Frobenius
eigenvector for
each diagonal block is distinguished iff

||A_{1}||<||A_{2}||<\dots||A_{\widehat{l}}||.

Proposition 6.4
.

Given an
(\widehat{l}\times\widehat{l})
nonnegative real matrix
N_{0}

then a nonnegative eigenvector
\mathbf{w}_{0}
for a communicating class is distinguished in
the sense of Definition
6.3
iff, defining

\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for
n\geq 0
where

\mathbf{w}_{n}=\lambda^{-n}\mathbf{w}_{0}
, then this nonnegative eigenvector sequence of eigenvalue one
is distinguished in the sense of
Definition
4.7
, for the stationary sequence sequence
N=(N_{i})_{i\geq 0}
with
N_{i}\equiv N_{0}
.

Proof.

We cut the matrix down to the upper-left
(j\times j)
– block submatrix, written as

\left[\begin{matrix}A&C\\
0&B\\
\end{matrix}\right]

(43)

where
B=A_{j}
. Now
B
is a primitive matrix, so by the
Perron-Frobenius theorem it has a unique nonnegative eigenvector
\mathbf{w}_{0}

with eigenvalue
\lambda>0
; moreover,
\lambda=||B||
.
We define from this a nonnegative eigenvector sequence
\mathbf{w}
of eigenvalue one,
with
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
\mathbf{w}_{n}=\lambda^{-n}\mathbf{w}_{0}
and
B^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n}=\mathbf{w}_{0}
.
From
Lemma
4.20
,
\mathbf{w}
is distinguished iff

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{C}_{0}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}

exists,
and we have

\widehat{C}_{0}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}CB^{n}_{k+1}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}C\mathbf{w}_{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}C\mathbf{w}_{0}\lambda^{-k}.

Now

||A^{k}C\lambda^{-k}\mathbf{w}_{0}||=||A^{k}C\mathbf{w}_{0}||\cdot||B||^{-k}\leq||C||\bigl(\frac{||A||}{||B||}\bigr)^{k}

so the sum will converge if
||A||<||B||
.
We want a necessary and sufficient condition however,
for this note first that since each
A_{j}
is primitive, all
vectors in the corresponding nonnegative cone grow asymptotically at the same rate
as the Perron-Frobenius eigenvector
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
; in other words, given
A_{j}

(l\times l)
with
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{l,+}
then there exists
a>0
and

0<c_{1}<c_{2}
such that
c_{1}\lambda_{j}^{n}<||A_{j}^{n}(a\mathbf{v})||<c_{2}\lambda_{j}^{n}

for
n
large;
see e.g. Theorem 8 of §XVI of

[
Bir67
]
.
We consider

J=\{k:\,A_{k}\text{ communicates to }A_{j}\}
. Set
K=\max_{1\leq k\in J\leq j-1}||A_{k}||^{n}
.
Then

\widehat{C}_{0}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}CB^{n}_{k+1}\mathbf{w}_{n+1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}C\mathbf{w}_{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}A^{k}C\mathbf{w}_{0}\lambda^{-k}.

so if for some
k\in J
,
||A_{k}||=||A_{j}||
then the sum
will explode.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

With this, we can at last explain the choice of the term “distinguished” in
Definition
4.17
.
For the nonstationary
case, the communicating classes
get replaced by streams
\beta,\alpha
and the
condition is that
\alpha\leq\beta.
The term “distinguished” is
being used in the sense of
distinguished from
i.e.
separated
from;
if this holds then the nonnegative eigenvector sequence from
\beta
generates an
nonnegative eigenvector sequence for the larger matrix
N
which remains distinct
from that of
\alpha
alone, whereas if it is not distinguished, the
generated eigenvector gets attracted to and swallowed up by that for

\alpha
, under the iteration defined here by
(\mathbf{w})
.

Remark 6.6
.

We mention that,
regarding Parry measures for the reducible case, the analysis is
identical to that above; thus the relevant “Parry measures” are now
built from the central measures, whether finite or infinite, by
multiplying by a left nonnegative eigenvector sequence.
The same relationship between Parry and central measures holds in the
nonstationary setting, see §4 of

[
Fis09
]
.
